0,2026-10-14,"Almost nine years ago, a tanned, relatively well-rested-looking CEO named Elon Musk announced that his electric car company would make a new sports car, the Tesla Roadster 2. It would be “the fastest sports car ever made, period,” Musk said on stage in 2017. “The point of doing this is to give a hardcore smackdown to gasoline cars.” Lights flashed, and the red prototype did a few speedy laps behind him.
Times have changed. Musk is the leading donor to a political party that last year nixed buying incentives for electric vehicles and last month adjusted fuel-economy standards to make it easier for automakers to skip making EVs altogether. Musk has said he’s not even in the business of making cars anymore, instructing investors to think of Tesla as a robotics and autonomy company. On Thursday night, Tesla will host what it has billed as its last unveiling event for a human-driven vehicle. Six years after it was supposed to roll into driveways, the Roadster 2 is finally here. Tesla is slated to start deliveries next year.
“EVs exist today in a massively different environment than when the Roadster was introduced,” says Ed Kim, president of AutoPacific, an automotive research firm. When Tesla announced the car back in 2017, electrics were “toys for early adopters,” he says. By 2020, other automakers saw Tesla’s sky-high valuations and lawmakers’ plans to transition vehicles to batteries and made big electric promises, investing heavily in the shift. China began to dominate EV-making. Now, after the Trump administration reversed the US’s course on the powertrain, EV sales are shrinking, not growing.
Still, lots more options are available now. In 2017, research firm Cox Automotive says, US customers had 15 EVs to choose from; today, that number has jumped to nearly 90. Potential EV buyers are more likely to be normies, and so their needs are more practical. “People are looking for range, performance, charging ability, resale value,” says Stephanie Valdez Streaty, director of Industry Insights at Cox.
What success looks like for the Roadster, then, is a bit complicated. It’s a hypercar targeted squarely at, well, non-normies—people who like to show off and go very, very fast. Some of them want that signature gasoline-engine power and roar. That’s why some luxury automakers have taken pains to reproduce the sound of a V-8 without one present. And it’s why others—Porsche and Ferrari among them—have said they’ll keep developing gas engines. Lamborghini said earlier this year it would shelve electrics altogether. Even Bugatti’s CEO, who founded his own electric hypercar company, said he expects loud ICE cars to become a status symbol among the wealthiest car buyers.
“EVs in this space really have to wow the shopper with a lot more than practical things,” says Kim. “Great range, really quiet, charging at home—none of that matters.” In 2017, Tesla said the car would start at $200,000; reservation holders put down $50,000 deposits.
In 2017, Musk promised a then-record-breaking sub-2-second 0-to-60 acceleration time. Now that a handful of other production cars have broken that barrier, Tesla will have to meet a high bar. One option: Musk has suggested several times over the years that the new Roadster will fly, and reports suggest that the automaker will get help from Musk’s rocket company SpaceX. (In 2018, SpaceX used its Falcon Heavy rocket to launch the first Roadster into space.)
Going that fast “is not terribly comfortable,"" says Shea Burns, a partner in the automotive and industrial practice at AlixPartners. “The laws of physics are catching up with them, outside of the rocket piece.” It would be an interesting way for an EV-maker to exit human driving.

2,2026-10-14,"Netflix is in a slump — and it's not just because you can't find a hot new show to watch.
The streaming titan's shares are down 42% in the last 12 months and have been nearly cut in half from their all-time high last summer. Netflix had its biggest round of layoffs in four years on Wednesday, cutting about 1,000 employees, roughly 6% of the company.
So, what's going wrong?
Netflix's viewership growth has slowed as free competitors like YouTube gain momentum, and initiatives like games and podcasts have yet to become breakout hits. Add in the company's surprise bid for Warner Bros., which it later abandoned, and some investors are feeling on edge.
Co-CEOs Ted Sarandos and Greg Peters said in a memo that entertaining the world ""requires us to be disciplined about where we prioritize our people and resources"" but added that the company is still on track to reach its financial goals this year.
Many of Netflix's struggles aren't unique to the ""Stranger Things"" maker, which disrupted Hollywood by pioneering paid TV streaming and has a major influence over culture.
""Even the top streaming companies are not immune to stagnation and financial difficulties,"" said Ross Benes, a media analyst at EMARKETER, Business Insider's sister company.
Other entertainment giants like Disney and NBCUniversal have turned to layoffs recently.
Here are five of the biggest challenges facing Netflix right now.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Although Netflix is still growing revenue at a healthy pace while generating billions in profits, Wall Street is concerned about its viewership trends.
Netflix co-CEO Ted Sarandos told Bloomberg at an event last month that ""we're not growing as fast as I want us to, and we're working on making that move faster.""
Netflix grew global viewing hours by 2% year over year to 97 billion in the first half of 2026. The streaming giant also announced last quarter that it would share its engagement report only once a year, down from twice a year.
When announcing the move, Netflix told investors that ""engagement is not just the quantity of view hours, but also refers to the quality and variety of our offering.""
Investors are worried that slowing engagement growth could hurt Netflix's ability to retain customers and persuade them to pay more, though the streamer has an industry-low cancellation rate.
Engagement is also ""the golden compass for advertisers"" since higher usage helps them reach broader audiences, said Brandon Katz, an analyst at Greenlight Analytics. If Netflix keeps growing viewership, it could charge advertisers higher rates.
The lack of a new breakout hit is also troubling for Netflix analysts. ""Stranger Things,"" the platform's biggest show ever, ended last year, and the streamer needs to find new franchises and buzzy hits that can replace it.
""Entertainment is an inherently subjective and streaky industry,"" Katz said.
While Netflix is still leading its paid peers in viewership and profits, free streaming services like YouTube, Tubi, and The Roku Channel are gaining popularity, especially among younger audiences.
YouTube's viewership share on US TVs was 14.2% in July — nearly double Netflix's 7.8% share, according to Nielsen.
Roku's free streamer and Tubi are also formidable competitors with shares of 2.9% and 2%, respectively. These no-cost services have grown as paid streamers like Netflix have raised their prices.
Netflix is considering starting a free offering in some markets, co-CEO Greg Peters has said.
Then there's the popularity of social media platforms like TikTok and Instagram, which are hoovering up viewership hours.
Netflix is looking to counter social-media foes by investing in video podcasts and creators to increase engagement and connect with younger viewers without breaking the bank.
""With pressure mounting, Netflix will look to turn the tide by getting a boost from low-cost content,"" Benes said.
Netflix spooked some shareholders by making an $82.7 billion bid for the Warner Bros. studio and streaming assets. Paramount Skydance eventually bought the whole company.
Although adding HBO Max and the Warner Bros. studio could have given Netflix a boost, investors worried that its leaders had run out of ideas for growth since Netflix hasn't historically turned to major dealmaking. Besides that narrative, shareholders were concerned about Netflix taking on a massive debt load and going through a complex integration process, Katz said.
Even though Netflix pocketed a $2.8 billion breakup fee when the deal fell apart, the stock never fully recovered.
Netflix has made major pushes into games and podcasts, though neither has been a game changer so far.
The streamer shut down a pair of game studios this summer and laid off some staffers in October 2024 as well.
Netflix hasn't shared many details about how its games or podcasts are performing. The company said in its July shareholder letter that it had successfully launched games like ""FIFA World Cup: Launch Edition"" and ""Unhinged,"" and that the kids app Netflix Playground tripled its daily user count, but it didn't specify how much time those games generated.
Neither games nor podcasts were on last quarter's engagement report. If either were a substantial hit, company leadership would likely want to share specific metrics of their success to investors eager to see new growth areas.
Games are ""not a bad idea"" but likely are ""not going to get any subs,"" said Alan Wolk, a media analyst at research firm TVREV. He added that it ""remains to be seen if people will start watching podcasts on Netflix,"" saying the streamer should adjust its mobile app interface to improve the experience.
Still, podcasts are a cheap way to expand into verticals like news and are also ""laundry folding programming"" that can form habits, Katz said.
Another way Netflix is trying to boost engagement is investing in live sports — including NFL games on Christmas Day and the day before Thanksgiving, the MLB Home Run Derby, and one-off events like a boxing match between Tyson Fury and Anthony Joshua.
While Netflix executives have said these events drive subscriptions at an unusually high rate, sports rights are also increasingly expensive due to bidding wars between TV networks like ESPN and tech giants like Amazon and YouTube.
Live sports are an effective acquisition tool and can help Netflix scale its budding advertising business.
""Sports are the last vestige of the monoculture and where they can reach all the people who never otherwise see ads,"" Wolk said.
However, sports aren't cheap and offer little to no replay value, making them more of a temporary fix for engagement than a long-term driver of viewership.
Netflix also has to be creative with its sports investments since most of the marquee rights — from the main NFL packages to the NBA, college football, NFL, and international soccer — are tied up for years. The streamer did secure live rights to the Women's World Cup in 2027 and 2031, which it hopes will capture the cultural zeitgeist.

3,2026-10-14,"Apple today became the second company ever to close with a market valuation north of $5 trillion, following NVIDIA’s landmark achievement in October 2025. Here are the details.
Apple’s stock closed at $342.96 today, up 0.5% on the Nasdaq. Although modest, the gain was enough to push the company’s market capitalization past the $5 trillion threshold, ending the day at $5.01 trillion.
Today’s result was particularly notable as most of the tech sector operated in the red, with Oracle closing down 8.83%, Meta down 3.27%, Nvidia down 3.16%, Microsoft down 1.8%, Amazon down 1.74%, and Google down 1.68%, to name a few.
Although Apple closed above the $5 trillion mark for the first time, this was not the first time it had reached that valuation. As The WSJ noted, today marked the seventh time Apple crossed the $5 trillion threshold during intraday trading, but the first time it managed to stay above it through the closing bell.
During intraday trading, Apple’s stock reached $347.07 per share, just 0.31% below its record high of $348.15.
Today’s milestone comes on the heels of a series of major product announcements from Apple, including the iPhone 18 Pro lineup, the iPhone Duo, and, just yesterday, the new Apple HomeView, HomePod mini 2, and Apple TV 4K.
It also comes ahead of the company’s fiscal fourth-quarter earnings report on November 2, which will offer a clearer picture of how Apple has navigated rising component costs and the impact of its recent hardware price increases.
Be that as it may, Apple has seen its stock rise roughly 5% since John Ternus took over as CEO on September 1 (or 8.2% if you count the August 31 closing price of $316.85), with today’s milestone further underscoring investor confidence in the company under its new leadership.

4,2026-10-14,"The forecast rests on surplus GPU capacity late this decade, and compute prices are currently going the other way
AI Learning and Artificial Intelligence
Bloomberg Intelligence forecasts that streamed games will become a $38B market by 2035 and that the dedicated console will stop being a mass-market product. The case depends on surplus GPU capacity pushing compute prices down late this decade, which has not happened yet.
Streamed games will be a $38B market by 2035, and the console may stop being a mass-market product. That is new research from Bloomberg Intelligence.
The report is led by BI analyst Nathan Naidu and was reported by Bloomberg on Tuesday. AI does double duty, cutting costs and supplying the data centres streaming runs on.
Consoles become a niche. Naidu compares them to record players, kept on by enthusiasts and collectors, and calls their role relatively uneconomic.
The economics rest on a glut that has not arrived. Naidu concedes AI demand is pushing compute prices up now, and expects the build-out to leave surplus GPU capacity late this decade.
Prices are moving the other way. AWS raised GPU prices about 20% in July for reserved capacity, its second rise in six months. The cause is a memory shortage analysts do not expect to ease before 2028.
The company calls are specific. Microsoft holds roughly 40% of streaming with Xbox Cloud Gaming, which BI estimates at 220 million hours last quarter. Nvidia has the edge with GeForce Now, and Tencent leads in China.
The operators sound less certain. Microsoft began testing ad-supported streaming in July and Amazon folded Luna into Prime Video, moves our own reporting read as an admission that cloud gaming has not worked yet.
Naidu puts Nintendo in the worst position, because it has treated streaming as peripheral. Sony fares better on PlayStation Plus and the PlayStation Portal.
Microsoft is building a console anyway. Xbox chief Asha Sharma announced a next-generation machine for 2027 in July, while cutting 3,200 roles and shedding five studios, two of them European.
The pressure there is margin, not streaming. Xbox’s profit margin fell to 3% this financial year, and Microsoft has not ruled out a spinoff or an outright sale.
Europe has a legal stake. Brussels made cloud streaming a condition of the Activision deal, and Microsoft’s published commitments give EEA players and streaming providers free licences for those games.
The clock matters. They run ten years from October 2023, lapsing two years before BI’s $38B date, though licences taken inside the window last indefinitely.
Game-making is moving faster than game-playing. Bloomberg reports that Epic chief Tim Sweeney said the company is retooling for maximum AI acceleration, having already built Claude and Gemini into Unreal Engine 6. A survey of more than 2,300 developers found 52% think generative AI is harming the industry.

5,2026-10-14,"President Donald Trump's new White House press secretary, Katie Zacharia, got off to a somewhat rocky start with her 14-minute debut media briefing on Tuesday.
And now bettors on prediction markets like Polymarket and Kalshi are putting money down on how long the conservative commentator will last in the high-profile job.
On Kalshi, bettors gave Zacharia a 38% chance of being out as White House press secretary before January 1 in a wager that had more than $313,000 in trading volume as of early afternoon Wednesday.
Kalshi traders were more bearish over the longer term, putting the chance of Zacharia's departure before July 1 at 60% and before July 1, 2028 at 96%.
The wager's trading volume indicated meaningful interest in the tenure of the newly appointed press secretary, though it remained well below Kalshi's biggest political markets. A market on whether Defense Secretary Pete Hegeseth will be out of a job, for example, had drawn more than $1.4 million in volume.
Polymarket's site has a similar market about Zacharia, asking whether she would be out as press secretary by the end of the year or by the end of October.
Bettors gave a 38% chance of an announcement by October 31 and a 51% chance by December 31.
When asked for comment on Wednesday, White House communications director Steven Cheung told Business Insider: ""Katie is a fabulous and wonderful addition to the team.""
""She is uniquely qualified and whip smart to help inform the American people about all the great things President Trump and his Administration have been doing, and she will do an incredible job as Press Secretary,"" Cheung said.

Beyond the hiccups in her first press briefing, Zacharia — who has repeatedly described herself as an attorney — faced scrutiny over her legal qualifications. When asked what state she passed the bar exam in, Zacharia told reporters, ""When I moved to China.""

8,2026-10-14,"Chinese companies aren't winning overseas just because they're cheaper, according to Goldman Sachs, challenging one of the biggest assumptions behind China's export boom.
""Intuitively, I think we would all associate with the Chinese product, with price deflation, price cuts,"" Trina Chen, co-head of Greater China equity research at Goldman Sachs, said on the bank's ""Exchanges"" podcast published Tuesday.
But Goldman found Chinese products often become more expensive after entering foreign markets, partly as their quality improves, while price cuts tend to come later as established rivals fight to defend market share.
The finding challenges the view that Chinese firms win foreign markets by relentlessly undercutting rivals on price.
It comes as policymakers from Washington to Brussels grapple with a surge in Chinese exports, part of what someeconomists have dubbed ""China shock 2.0.""
China's weak domestic demand and years of deflationary pressure have also fueled concerns that its exporters could push down prices overseas.
Goldman analyzed 11 sectors and 40 Chinese and global companies to study where Chinese firms are gaining market share.
Chinese products typically enter new markets at about a 30% discount to incumbent rivals, but prices often rise in subsequent years.
Chen said higher prices could reflect factors including inflation, economic cycles, and improvements in product quality.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
Chinese companies often raise prices as their products improve without losing their price advantage over competitors. Chen said the discount versus competitors generally remained stable.
Chinese electric vehicles are one example. They sell at roughly a 20% discount to comparable global models, a gap Chen said has remained stable over the past three to four years.
Robot vacuum cleaners show an even more dramatic shift. Chen said Chinese products in the category entered the US market at around a 40% discount but now command a roughly 60% premium as manufacturers continue innovating and adding new functions.
""I think it's fair to say the entry of the Chinese product does not necessarily create additional pricing pressure for many years post the initial entry,"" Chen said.
Instead, price cuts often come later, when established competitors struggle to defend their positions.
""These price cuts typically are initiated by companies who have been losing market share and trying to defend their market share in a very challenging situation and competition,"" Chen said.
Chen said Chinese companies tend to gain market share fastest in modular, consumer-facing, high-growth products.
Progress has been slower in mature, interconnected business-to-business markets, where customer trust, servicing networks, local distribution channels, and long operating track records make it harder for Chinese companies to gain market share.

10,2026-10-14,"The Tallinn insurtech, which insures platforms such as Bolt, Voi, and Turo, will spend it on products and sales.
The Cachet team in Tallinn
Tallinn startup Cachet, which sells insurance to ride-hailing, scooter, and gig-work apps, has closed a funding round led by Spintop Ventures. The deal raises its total capital to about €11 million.
Moreover, Truffle Capital and Icebreaker.vc, both earlier backers, joined the round.
Cachet acts as a licensed broker rather than an insurer. Its policies are shaped by real use of each car, scooter, or worker, and they plug straight into the apps. Cachet runs the policies and claims, and large insurers take on the risk.
Standard cover, by contrast, is set once a year and in one country at a time. On an app, the risk shifts with every ride.
Cachet holds licences in 16 European markets and has more than 50 platform clients, among them Bolt, Voi, Ryde, and Turo. Bolt, also from Tallinn, has raised €220M as it prepares for a stock market listing.
The company said it more than doubled in size over the past year. It also recorded an operating profit, measured as EBITDA, for the first time across all of 2025.
The new cash will go on product work and more sales staff. Cachet wants to cover new forms of platform work, such as employer-of-record set-ups, where a third party is the workers’ legal employer.
It also plans to cover self-driving vehicles as they reach European roads, such as driverless cars in Finland.
Some of the money will fund a tool that reads live data from a client’s fleet and tells it how to reduce risk during the policy period.
The goal is to prevent incidents rather than only pay out after them. Cachet first used the tool in the Nordic countries and now plans to bring it to other markets.
“We are building the adaptive insurance infrastructure for what comes next, from shared and autonomous vehicles to distributed work and AI liability,” said Hedi Mardisoo, Cachet’s chief executive and co-founder.
“There is a lot of noise right now from insurance start-ups burning capital to buy growth at high valuations. Cachet has taken the opposite route,” said Erik Wenngren, a partner at Spintop Ventures.
The deal is Spintop’s first investment in Estonia, Wenngren said. Mardisoo and Kalle Palling founded Cachet in 2018, and it last raised €5.5 million in 2022, in a round led by Truffle Capital.

11,2026-10-14,"Chief Correspondent covering media and technology
The good news for Netflix: It's been here before.
In 2022, after years of breakneck growth and go-go spending, the streamer suddenly lost subscribers. Cue layoffs, a hasty pivot to an ad model it had always criticized, and tons of industry schadenfreude.
But it turned out that Netflix's growth problems were overstated. Or at least, that its growth problems weren't a sign of opportunity for its streaming rivals.
Years later, Netflix's position at the top of paid streaming services remains unchanged. Meanwhile, Wall Street has grown tired of watching would-be competitors like Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery struggle to make headway — which is why both of those companies are now owned by the Ellison family.
The bad news for Netflix: The levers it pulled four years ago no longer seem available. It has already done the ads thing, and the live sports thing — another move they had previously sworn off. A foray into video games doesn't seem to have produced much at all.
Meanwhile there has been a steady series of reports indicating that not only is Netflix's subscriber and revenue growth slowing, but its existing subscribers are finding the service less engaging than in the past.
Netflix responded to that one by arguing that its engagement was just fine, really — and then announced it would make it harder to understand its performance by cutting back on the audience data it provides to the outside world.
Most concerning: Wall Street and the rest of the media industry have started catching up to the fact that Netflix's competition isn't just other paid streamers, but giant platforms — YouTube, Instagram, and TikTok. Those companies may not have the next ""Stranger Things,"" but they don't have to. They have unlimited content that costs them either nothing (Instagram and TikTok) or nothing upfront (YouTube). And they provide it to consumers for free, in a never-ending river of clips.
It's very hard to compete with that, though you can see Netflix trying: YouTube has a lot of video podcasts, so now Netflix is doing that, too.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
None of that has convinced Wall Street, which is why Netflix stock is down 43% over the last year. Some of that was a reaction to the company's pursuit of HBO and Warner Bros. — an uncharacteristic mega M&A swing that investors hated. But after Netflix bailed on that one, shareholders remain skeptical.
That may be why Netflix stock isn't moving on Wednesday, after the company announced it is laying off 6% of its workforce. Cutting heads can cut Netflix's costs and improve its margins. But it doesn't help the company grow — or help it compete with ruthless Big Tech platforms.

12,2026-10-14,"AI Agents
Picture two AI trading agents running the same model and the same strategy. A signal arrives during a fast market, both send an order, and one platform answers in milliseconds while the other times out. Same intelligence, different result.
The difference sat in the plumbing. As agents take on more of the work of trading, the infrastructure connecting them to markets plays a growing role in what they can actually deliver.
For a consumer app, reliability is a user-experience problem. A delay means a spinning wheel, and the person taps again. For an agent it turns into a correctness problem. An agent making hundreds of automated calls reads a timeout as missing data, so it may act on stale account information, skip a check or retry an order into a worse price.
That gap is drawing money. Arcade raised $60 million in June on the view that most agents fail because the infrastructure around them isn’t ready, and TechCrunch reported that MCP servers have had to do extra work simply to track sessions across load-balanced machines, one reason large integrations were slow to ship.
“Markets do not pause when someone closes an app or steps away from a screen. The early adoption of Agent OS points to growing demand for agents that can remain connected as market conditions change: in the first week, MCP connections doubled and call volumes increased twentyfold, within two weeks, daily requests exceeded 90,000 with 95% completed within 60 milliseconds and a 97% success rate,” says Jeff Li, VP of Product at Binance. “The next phase of market access will be shaped not only by what agents can do, but by how quickly and reliably they can respond when the market moves.”
Those figures reward a close reading. A 95th-percentile result means one request in 20 took longer than 60 milliseconds, and fast markets are exactly where that slower tail matters. At a 97% success rate, a system handling more than 90,000 daily requests would see roughly 2,700 fail each day, by simple arithmetic. The published numbers also leave open where latency is measured and what counts as a failure.
Latency, success rate and behavior under load look like separate metrics. For an agent they multiply. A person sends one request and reacts, while an agent chains several together. Suppose a workflow needs five sequential calls to check a balance and read the order book as well as size a position and place the order and confirm the fill, and each call succeeds 97% of the time independently. In that case the full sequence completes cleanly about 86% of the time.
Load is the hardest of the three, because demand spikes precisely when prices move and failures tend to cluster at the worst moment. MCP’s maintainers moved to a stateless design in July so servers can run behind standard load balancers without relying on one machine to remember each session.
“Before, you needed to have a session store and manage session IDs, and if one of your compute pods went down, all of a sudden the requests would start failing,” says Den Delimarsky, a lead maintainer of MCP.
Binance Research made a related point when it shared its two-week figures: as agents become more capable, the constraint may increasingly shift from model intelligence to the infrastructure needed to access data and execute reliably.
Trading has learned this before, at considerable cost. On August 1, 2012, a faulty software deployment at Knight Capital sent more than 4 million orders into the market in 45 minutes while trying to fill 212 customer orders. The firm traded more than 397 million shares and lost more than $460 million, according to the SEC, which later settled market access rule charges with Knight for $12 million.
For agent builders, the takeaway is uncomfortable. Automated systems can fail faster than anyone can step in, so reliability has to be designed in from the start.
Payments went through a similar evolution. Card networks and settlement systems still compete on availability and throughput, yet over time those properties became the price of entry for handling other people’s money. Agent platforms look set to follow. Once one publishes latency and success-rate figures, the others will be asked for theirs, and the published number turns into a floor.
Anyone connecting an agent to a funded account can put a few direct questions to the platform. At which percentile is latency reported, and is it measured at the server or end to end? What counts as a failed request, and what does the agent do after one?
Context matters as much as averages. How did the figures hold up during the busiest hour of the most volatile day, compared with a launch-week snapshot? Are there rate limits, and what happens to requests above them? Does the platform publish these numbers over time, or only once?
Agent platforms may increasingly publish execution metrics the way exchanges already publish system status, and the ones that do it consistently will make those questions much easier to answer.

14,2026-10-14,"From left: Markus Grabmeier (President of Electrical Products at Siemens AG), Taavi Madiberk (CEO of Skeleton Technologies), Barry Powell (President and Regional CEO of Siemens Electrical Products)
Siemens has bought into Skeleton Technologies, an Estonian maker of energy storage systems. With this, the deal values Skeleton at more than $1 billion, its CEO Taavi Madiberk told Bloomberg.
The deal closed this month and was worth more than $100 million, the company said, but it did not give the exact figure.
In return, Siemens can sell Skeleton’s high-power storage systems to data centres, and no one else can, Siemens tech chief Peter Koerte told Bloomberg.
Skeleton’s systems help data centres smooth out sudden swings in power use and reduce energy waste.
“What we see is that also up to 40% more compute can be had on the chips if the power management is done right and the energy is not burned off,” Madiberk said.
Skeleton says it works with all the big US cloud firms, and that its kit will soon be used across a gigawatt of data centre capacity.
Skeleton plans to spend more than $200 million on growing in the US, including building factories there, Madiberk said. Bloomberg reported in March that the company is aiming for a US stock market listing next year.
The two firms have already worked together on Skeleton’s €220 million plant near Leipzig, Germany, where Siemens helped build an automated production line. Siemens wants to sell data centres as a full package, Koerte said. It already supplies them with power gear and design software.
“We don’t need the next OpenAI right now, what we need is the next generation of AI, which will primarily operate at the application level,” Koerte said. “We need to further expand the infrastructure.”
Skeleton was founded 17 years ago, and it brings together more than 10 Estonian tech companies valued at over $1 billion, a high number for a country of 1.3 million people. Rising power demand from AI is putting pressure on grids and data centre builders across Europe.
Other big tech groups are also investing in Europe’s AI build-out. This week, Nvidia announced new European investments, including stakes in four French startups.

15,2026-10-14,"The all-cash deal pays $6.05 a share, a 138% premium to the dating app’s price before news of the talks.
Bumble founder Whitney Wolfe Herd at TechCrunch Disrupt in San Francisco, September 2018.
Bumble has agreed to be acquired by Ithaca Holdings in an all-cash deal valuing the dating app at about $1.3 billion, the companies said on Tuesday.
Ithaca will pay $6.05 per Bumble Class A share, about 138% more than the stock’s closing price on October 9, before reporting on Monday that the two sides were in advanced talks.
The deal does not need a shareholder vote. Funds linked to Blackstone and founder Whitney Wolfe Herd have already approved it in writing, giving it the backing it needs, according to a filing with US regulators.
A committee of independent board members reviewed the offer and recommended it. Wolfe Herd, who is Bumble’s chief executive, did not take part in the board vote.
Once the deal closes, Ithaca’s co-chief executives, Ravi Gupta and John Hegeman, will also serve as co-CEOs of Bumble.
Wolfe Herd will stay closely involved, the companies said, and Bumble will remain based in Austin, Texas. The companies did not say when they expect the deal to close.
“Bumble was founded on a simple belief: meaningful relationships are essential to a healthy, happy life,” Wolfe Herd said.
“I am incredibly grateful to the entire Bumble team, whose dedication and belief in our mission have made this company what it is today. In Ithaca, we have found a partner who shares our vision, believes in our women-first mission, and is committed to investing in Bumble’s future. I am grateful to the Special Committee for their thoughtful work and am pleased to support this transaction based on the Board’s recommendation.”
Gupta and Hegeman said they “see huge potential to take it into the next era together”.
“Bumble has a unique combination of essential attributes, including its trusted brand and loyal community. As technology and dating habits evolve, we see huge potential to take it into the next era together, building on the foundation Whitney and the talented Bumble team have built. Whitney has a clear vision and strong principles, and we’re grateful for her partnership as we carry her mission forward together.”
The sale ends five years into Bumble’s stock market listing, when it was valued at $14 billion when it listed on the Nasdaq in 2021.
Its business has shrunk since. In the second quarter of 2026, revenue fell 15.2% from the prior year to $211 million, and paying users dropped 16.4% to about 3.2 million.
Under Wolfe Herd, who returned as CEO in March 2025, Bumble has tried to win users back. This year, it launched an AI dating assistant called Bee, and in August it began allowing men to send the first message.
Bumble will not publish a results release or hold an earnings call for the third quarter, and it will file its quarterly report by November 9.

16,2026-10-14,"I recently walked into the macelleria in our Sicilian village — a combo butcher, deli, and corner store — and started babbling excitedly that my favorite cheese was back in stock.
One of the owners joked in Italian that ""Camellia moved here just for the food."" She wasn't completely wrong.
After 20 years in New York City, my husband and I moved to a small town in Italy for some of the more classic reasons: quality food, a slower pace of life, and community-oriented culture.
Now that we've lived here for nearly three years, though, we've also experienced the reality behind the tourism taglines. Our village has its own rhythm — one I've learned to appreciate, even when it's pushed me to change and adapt.
Buying food here looks very different from the way we shopped in the US.
With no large grocery store within a 40-minute walk, we shop hyper-local. This includes the macelleria, weekly outdoor market, and vendors who drive our narrow streets selling fruit, vegetables, eggs, fish, fresh bread, and more.
Each truck passes by only on certain days and times — and the schedule isn't written down. We had to learn it by living it.
We've now gotten to know local vendors and farmers, and we message them in advance to find out what's available. We eat what's in season, like broccoli and citrus in winter, or melon with prosciutto in summer.
Some specialties, like prickly pears, fresh figs, and wild asparagus, are only available for a few weeks each year.
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
This hyper-local food delivery system can be a lifesaver, too, if you have limited mobility. Our older neighbors lean out their balconies to call down orders for eggs and artichokes.
If you're trying to pack in an afternoon of errands on your own schedule, small-town Sicily is not the place to do it.
Where we live, most businesses and public offices close for around three hours every afternoon. Especially in the hot summer months, the streets are nearly deserted between 1 p.m. and 4 p.m. each day.
At first, this caught me off guard. I'd head out to grab a prescription or a quick lunch, and find everything closed. Then I realized this was what a ""slower pace of life"" actually entails.
Taking an afternoon break to eat, rest, and step away from your work is the norm, not the exception.
But the break doesn't mean people don't work exceptionally hard. Our village comes back to life in the late afternoon, and then, many businesses stay open until 7 or 8 p.m.
My husband and I have grown to appreciate this rhythm. Most evenings after work, we head out for a long walk, do our shopping, and chat with people along the way.
In New York, we could use an app to schedule an appointment or get groceries delivered. Here, getting things done requires building personal relationships. As newcomers, that's meant showing up in person — a lot.
When we first moved, we started our residency and immigration paperwork at the vital records office at city hall. But here's the thing: It's only open to the public one morning and one afternoon each week.
To learn the schedule, we had to go in person. Then, we had to show up on the right day with initial questions … and return another day to submit our forms.
If you're used to taking care of business from behind a screen, this can seem inefficient. Now that I've adjusted to it, though, I'm convinced this system is actually a feature. Yes, tasks can take more time, but showing up in person has helped us connect with people in our community.
And it can pay off in unexpected ways. When my husband's distant cousin recently visited from the US, we stopped at city hall to show them the beautiful, historic building. A staff member recognized us — thanks to all our visits — and offered an impromptu private tour!
Personal connections can also turn into support when you need it most. Now, I feel comfortable using WhatsApp to send urgent questions to our pharmacist and doctors, even on weekends.
Before moving, I imagined I'd fall naturally into an Italian pace of life.
But I've learned that we carry our habits and personalities with us — the good and the bad. I spent 20 years as a deadline-driven nonprofit fundraiser, and even though I thought Italy would help me slow down, I found myself reproducing that same workaholic approach here.
Changing took longer than I expected, but I've learned to prioritize connection over the illusion of efficiency, and I've become more intentional about balancing urgency versus patience.
Now, I try to live by the common saying here — ""piano piano,"" or ""little by little"" — and trust that what's important will get done in its own time.

17,2026-10-14,"The upcoming main navigation can be gotten through utilizing the tab key. Any buttons that open a sub navigation can be triggered by the space or enter key.
In October 2025, Beijing imposed sweeping controls on its rare earth exports. Since then, those controls have been suspended one short reprieve at a time, in the latest instance until Nov. 10, with a broader trade truce through Jan. 10, 2027. The reprieves have repeatedly come only after American concessions, demonstrating China’s leverage in the export fight.
Yet China has constricted sales even during the truce, as buyers have few options but to take what they can get. In June, with the suspension in force, Beijing placed MP Materials and USA Rare Earth, the two companies the U.S. is counting on to build an alternative supply to China, on an export control list.
Washington’s effort to rebuild the supply chain, meanwhile, has not yet reached the scale or durability the problem requires. With up to $100 billion in loan authority specifically earmarked for critical minerals in the One Big Beautiful Bill Act, the capital undoubtedly exists. What is missing is alignment of interests among the parties that would supply and benefit from it, and a political system patient enough to hold a course for the decade or more that rebuilding a supply chain takes.
This article’s authors work at the intersection of finance and national security. Between the three of us, we have watched the same on-again, off-again rhythm play out in both Chinese statecraft and U.S. budget fights. A consistent pattern plays out: Capital markets are good at financing efficient supply but are not good at financing a binary outcome, such as insurance against a monopoly state seller that can manipulate both price and availability. And subsidies alone will not close that gap. We have seen firsthand that what the U.S. government has to offer right now insufficiently incentivizes private investors, because its tools are market tools deployed in an environment of market failure. Instead, we propose a financing instrument that could clear both economic and political hurdles: a contingent convertible security that converts into ownership if Beijing weaponizes supply.
Rare Earths Are a Market Failure
The history of how we got here is well known, but worth retelling briefly. The Chinese Communist Party inherited its strategic focus on building industrial capacity from the Soviet Union. In 1964, Deng Xiaoping inspected an iron ore mine near the Mongolian border holding the world’s largest rare earth deposit. He ordered the engineers to develop steel and rare earths alike. Since then, as technologies have increasingly relied on specialized materials, the Chinese Communist Party under successive leaders has deliberately invested in and consolidated control over the rare earths industry. China refines more than 90 percent of the world’s rare earths and close to 99 percent of heavy rare earths today. It processes nearly all the heavy elements that go into precision-guided weapons and advanced chips.
When Beijing tightened export controls in October 2025, Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent characterized it as “pointing a bazooka at the supply chains and industrial base of the entire free world.” Remarkably, the entire market for rare earth oxides is worth less than $10 billion. That a $10 billion market can grind to a halt trillions in global gross domestic product is the definition of a chokepoint. Beijing controls this chokepoint today by design. Decades of state policy work laid the groundwork for the Chinese Communist Party to enact an international licensing system by 2025. General Secretary Xi Jinping himself openly speaks about the importance of maintaining global dependency on China’s industrial supply chains.
Hindsight treats this as neglect, something policymakers lost sight of and let slip during the U.S.’s unipolar moment. That is a necessary, but only partial, explanation. The truth is the United States did not lose its rare earth industry due to carelessness. Private American companies willingly sold it, for reasons that made perfect economic sense at the time and still do. China bought it for reasons that were never purely economic. That mismatch explains why, after years of investment and policy attention, the dependency remains.
Until the late 1980s, the United States was the world’s leading producer and refiner of rare earths. But it was a dirty business: The Mountain Pass mine in California leaked roughly 600,000 gallons of radioactive and other waste into the Mojave. As cleanup and regulatory costs mounted and profits fell, the mine shut down in stages starting in the 1990s until it was fully idle by 2002. Meanwhile, the work moved to China, where the state subsidized unprofitable operations, tolerated pollution, and sold the processed oxides back to the U.S. at rock-bottom prices.
The same thing happened to the magnets: In 1995, General Motors sold Magnequench, its magnet unit, to a Chinese-backed consortium. By 2006, the last U.S. plant had closed, and the machine tools and equipment had been shipped to China. Economists would say this was the right move for a free market economy. Comparative advantage did what it was supposed to do, and a dirty, low-margin business left a wealthy country for a developing one. There was even a strategic rationale: Drawing China into the trading system, the wishful thinking went, would also make it a responsible stakeholder rather than a rival.
The category error was to conceive rare earths as a market, rather than recognizing it as a geostrategic industry. A market solves for the lowest cost. To be sure, Chinese producers competed on cost, and they rode a tech boom with surging demand for their product. But a state will pay almost any price to control a chokepoint, because tolerating a money-losing business pales in comparison to the geopolitical leverage that it brings. Over time, China has become less market driven, not more. Meanwhile, the U.S. continued to treat as a market what the Chinese Communist Party came to appreciate as a strategic program.
Control over the chokepoint gives Beijing a switch. When it lets the material flow, no private interest will build alternative supply chains at scale, because Chinese state-funded producers can sell at or below cost indefinitely, and no rational private investor will willingly sign up to lose money. When China cuts off the flow, the incentives reverse overnight, but capacity cannot follow overnight, because standing up the full supply chain and rebuilding the lost know-how can take a decade or longer.
This is classic market failure in which the same critical material can become worthless or priceless on command. In 2010, during a maritime dispute with Japan, Beijing reportedly halted rare earth shipments, and Tokyo quickly conceded. The short-term truces of the past year, each struck around contentious but episodic trade disputes, further entrench that market failure. Each successive loosening and tightening seems designed to keep the United States and its allies off balance and frustrate any long-term plan to reduce Beijing’s leverage.
Government Money Has to Survive Politics
If the market will not rebuild the rare earths supply chain, the government must do so.
Administrations under both parties have tried, and their attempts have run into a predictable morass of misaligned interests. The parties to government financing all want different things. Private investors want attractive returns in fat years, while having the government carry losses in lean ones. Commercial lenders want years of fully contracted offtake to guarantee interest payments, while Congress typically appropriates money for the next year. Markets want flexibility while Congress wants tight oversight and guardrails. Washington often wants to be the last (and lightest) money in, which signals to investors that the government is hedging. And cheap, open-ended financing is exactly the wrong signal to send to management teams.
The Pentagon’s latest playbook acknowledges that the market, left to its own devices, simply will not finance uneconomic redundancy. As a result, the July 2025 deal with MP Materials includes $400 million in convertible preferred stock, loans, equity warrants, a 10-year guaranteed price floor, and a 10-year offtake commitment. The deal was right for its moment, as it gets the producer going and sustains it while Beijing floods the market. But the deal blurs two different roles: In ordinary years, the government is a lender, financing capacity the market will not; in a crisis, it has insured against a geopolitical event. These two roles call for two different return profiles. Because the deal does not separate them, it puts the government in the unenviable position of simultaneously absorbing losses and inviting the charge of nationalization, all without a clear exit strategy.
Without some structure to navigate potentially painful political costs, these financing arrangements will come under pressure during budget cycles and electoral turnover. And that is simply untenable, because when it comes to the decade-plus timeline to stand up a rare earths supply chain, whatever Washington builds must outlast the administration that built it.
A Financial Instrument That Responds to Beijing’s Behavior
Our proposal is a financial instrument that aligns these interests. In financial parlance, the instrument is called a contingent convertible security, also known as a CoCo bond, issued by the producer to the government and any private lenders who wish to join, paired with guaranteed offtake at a price floor. Its form changes with two externally observable conditions: the producer’s capacity build-out and Beijing’s behavior.
Here’s how it works. In its starting form (and in quiet years), a CoCo bond is a simple loan paying interest. Two triggers change its form. The first is capacity. The producer must cross a defined capacity threshold by a fixed date. Capacity can be measured as units of output in mining, refining, or manufacturing critical minerals. Management’s immediate incentives should be tied to the build-out itself.
The second trigger is either a sustained breach of an agreed price band or an externally observed exercise of the chokepoint. This could be denial of export licenses, a bureaucratic delay beyond a specified period, or another form of “constructive denial” that materially interrupts supply to the United States or allied countries. Importantly, no part of this trigger should rely on Beijing’s confirmation, because it is not incentivized to admit to a cutoff. The trigger should be narrow enough that it cannot be declared for political convenience, but broad enough that an adversary cannot game it simply by changing the form of the restriction.
When the first trigger fires, the lender receives a pro rata share of the producer’s free cash flow, in effect, a dividend. The lender paid to build the capacity, so it shares in what that capacity earns. When both triggers fire, the lender also gains the right to convert into common equity. The conversion event does not create new capacity—the financing has already done that work—but rather matches the payoff to the risk. If lenders financed years of otherwise uneconomic capacity because a cutoff might someday make it necessary, they share in the upside if and when the cutoff comes. The cash participation matters too: In a crisis, the market may treat a price spike as temporary and event-driven and not value the equity, but the spike still flows through the producer’s cash flow, which benefits the lender either way.
Why would a producer agree to this? Because it gets something unique that the market will not otherwise provide. In effect, the producer buys a put—the price floor that carries it through Beijing’s dumping—and pays for it by selling a call—the cash share and the conversion right. It trades some of the windfall in a crisis for the protection of surviving quiet years. Either way, the taxpayer is paid for the risk actually borne, when it is borne, rather than for a risk merely feared.
Nor does the government have to finance the entire CoCo bond. Pre-vetted private co-investors, including foreign co-investors subject to Committee on Foreign Investment in the United States (CFIUS) screening, can offset government money dollar-for-dollar. Ideally, co-investors should be firms that fear a cutoff the most, such as defense contractors and automakers. Private co-investors would receive a pro rata right to offtake as a further incentive and could potentially even trade the bond with its associated offtake rights with other investors. Japan’s JARE consortium did something similar when it financed Australia’s Lynas in return for supply. Finally, private holders also give the program a constituency that outlasts any single administration.
Neither Nationalization nor Welfare
This instrument compensates for the asymmetric risk a lender (whether taxpayer or private) assumes when countering an adversary-controlled chokepoint, by providing an asymmetric reward if that chokepoint is ever exercised.
The risk here is not from a natural shortage or an ordinary price cycle, but from the Chinese Communist Party’s policy decisions. The same logic reaches other chokepoint materials such as pharmaceutical precursors. Rare earths are the clearest case and, at under $10 billion in market size, the cheapest place for the U.S. government to start. And it can start under existing authorities. For example, the Pentagon has leaned on Title III of the Defense Production Act for its recent mineral deals involving equity. The Pentagon’s Office of Strategic Capital can lend, but its equity authority remains unclear. The Senate is currently revising the National Defense Authorization Act for 2027 to clarify those authorities. Its language should cover instruments like this CoCo bond.
Our CoCo bond proposal does not replace stockpiles, price floors, or offtake agreements. These instruments solve significant but different problems. Stockpiles buy time in a crisis, price floors protect producers against predatory pricing, and offtake agreements guarantee demand. The CoCo bond, in contrast, distributes risk and reward to investors in a binary, geopolitical event outside the producer’s control. It also enables the government to lend as a portfolio—some developers will fail, but the winners, and any crisis, can carry the portfolio as a whole. And it may have some deterrent effect as well: If this instrument makes capacity more likely to get built, and that prospect enters Beijing’s risk calculus, so much the better.
Economically, the conversion right works like the equity warrants the Pentagon took in MP Materials. The difference lies in the quiet years, when under this structure the government holds no equity. Outright equity stakes invite the charge of nationalization; cheap financing with no upside smacks of corporate welfare. We believe a structure that is intentionally designed to avoid both at the outset—but flexible enough to change form in response to an adversary’s actions, not domestic political developments—has the best chance of surviving enough political cycles to complete a decade-plus build-out.
*          *          *
Translating this concept into reality raises important implementation questions about statutory authority, precise trigger definitions, CFIUS integration, and corporate governance. We offer the CoCo bond model to stimulate discussions around aligning interests in a market failure and national security context, and we invite the legal, policy, and financial communities to help refine the “plumbing” necessary to make asymmetric financing a workable pillar of U.S. statecraft.
A market will always sell a chokepoint to whoever runs it cheapest, and by market logic, it would be the right thing to do. Subsidies alone cannot win it back, because they only affect price; Beijing prices for geopolitical leverage, not profit. Neutralizing a chokepoint created through market failure requires capital willing to absorb years of subpar returns or even losses against an extraordinary geopolitical crisis. In quiet years, the government should earn like a lender, with the price floor protecting against Beijing’s dumping. When capacity is built, it should share in what it financed. And if Beijing ever reaches for the switch, the taxpayers and private investors who paid to insure against that moment should participate in the value their capital created. The risk is asymmetric. The financing should be too.
Hard National Security Choices"
18,2026-10-14,"JPMorgan and MUFG are part of the group that has spent more than six months trying to distribute $38 billion in construction debt tied to a Texas and Wisconsin data center project leased to Oracle.
JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, SMBC and MUFG are quietly shopping pieces of the AI debt pile they helped build, a sign the lenders closest to the money have started hedging their own bet.
Six months ago, banks were racing each other to underwrite data center loans tied to Oracle, Meta and the rest of the AI buildout. Now some of the same banks are trying to get that debt off their books. JPMorgan, Morgan Stanley, SMBC and MUFG have been exploring significant risk transfers, according to a report from the Financial Times. These are deals that let a bank keep a loan on paper while shifting its riskiest slice to outside investors. The banks are doing this because they are bumping up against internal limits on how much AI-linked exposure any one lender can hold.
One case shows how stuck this has gotten. A group of banks including JPMorgan and MUFG has spent more than six months trying to distribute $38 billion in construction debt tied to a Texas and Wisconsin data center project leased to Oracle, Bloomberg has reported. Some lenders are now willing to sell pieces of it to non-bank buyers at a discount, just to move it. Six months is a long time to sit on debt nobody underwrote to hold forever.
Matthew Moniot, co-head of credit risk sharing at Man Group, told the Financial Times the deal sizes involved are ""out of scale to anything we've thought about, ever."" That is not a hedge-fund manager talking down a trade he wants no part of. It is someone whose job is pricing exactly this kind of risk, saying the numbers no longer fit any model he has used before.
Oracle is the clearest tell. Its five-year credit default swaps, the cost of insuring against the company defaulting, climbed to around 151 basis points in early October, levels last seen in 2009, according to market data cited by Bloomberg. S&P had already cut Oracle's credit rating to BBB-, a single notch above junk, back in July. The company is reportedly targeting more than $40 billion in additional debt and equity this fiscal year. That is on top of the $18 billion in bonds it sold in a single day last September, and the money is needed to keep funding its Texas, Wisconsin and New Mexico sites.
Oracle sued Wisconsin's Public Service Commission over a rule that could force it to post more than $7 billion in collateral for its Port Washington data center campus. The fight helped push S&P to cut Oracle's credit rating to BBB-, one notch above junk, as the company's AI spending outpaces its cash flow. - Oracle Wisconsin data center collateral dispute - utilities regulator $7 billion lawsuit
Demand for that paper has thinned out, too. Bonds from Alphabet, Amazon, Meta, Microsoft and Oracle now trade at a premium to similarly rated non-AI corporate debt. Order books for their offerings have shrunk from roughly five times the amount on sale back in February to under two times by July, Bloomberg has reported. That is the difference between a bond that sells itself and one a bank has to work to place.
None of this is happening in isolation. The 10-year Treasury yield hit 5.304% this month, its highest since 2002, after a $39 billion auction cleared at 5.30%, the highest auction yield since 2000. The Federal Reserve's own September meeting minutes named AI-related corporate borrowing as a contributor to that move. Capital Economics argues the bigger driver is plain old inflation and government deficits, not tech company bond sales. Whoever is right about the cause, the effect is the same for anyone trying to finance a data center right now: the risk-free rate they are borrowing against just got a lot more expensive.
Frankly, the mechanics here matter more than the headline number. Banks do not sell down debt they are confident in. They sell down debt when they are worried about concentration, which is exactly what ""approaching internal limits"" means in plain English: too much of this bank's balance sheet is now one bet on one sector holding up. Spreading $120 billion of AI infrastructure spending across special purpose vehicles was supposed to make that risk easier to swallow. That is the structure Oracle, Meta, xAI and CoreWeave have all used to keep debt off their own balance sheets. Instead the risk is turning up at the banks that financed the SPVs in the first place.
The investors who still want in have gotten pickier about where. Capital is rotating toward data centers leased to investment-grade hyperscalers like Microsoft and Google, and away from weaker credits, Bloomberg has reported. That is exactly why CoreWeave-leased paper prices at 7.00% to 9.875%, against the 5.70% to 7.875% that hyperscaler-leased bonds command, per bond market data compiled by CCIR. A smaller, less capitalized AI infrastructure company trying to raise debt next quarter is now competing for the same nervous pool of lenders as Oracle. Except without Oracle's balance sheet or its existing customer base to point to.
That's the part of this story that does not show up in a single day's stock move. The AI boom still runs on the assumption that credit stays open indefinitely at reasonable terms. Six months of a bank struggling to sell $38 billion in loans is the clearest evidence yet that assumption is being tested in real time, not in some theoretical downturn still years away.

19,2026-10-14,"The American figures are quarterly, and Europe registered more electric cars in July alone than the US sold in three months
US EV sales rose 6.1% on the previous quarter to almost 263,000, their highest in a year, against more than 437,000 in the same quarter of 2025. Europe registered 277,006 electric cars in July alone.
American electric car sales rose 6.1% on the previous quarter, their best in a year. They were still about 40% down on a year earlier, and Europe registered more electric cars in July alone.
Cox Automotive put the quarter at almost 263,000 against more than 437,000 a year before, in figures reported by InsideEVs. The improvement is on the second quarter, not on 2025.
Last year’s number was a rush. The federal tax credit was being scrapped, buyers moved early, and the third quarter of 2025 set a record that will not be matched soon.
Stephanie Valdez Streaty, Cox’s director of industry insights, said the market is finding its footing and that the reset phase is largely behind it.
Europe’s single month was bigger than America’s quarter. July brought 277,006 electric cars across the EU, the UK and the EFTA states, a 51% rise and a 25% share, on Dataforce figures.
The two trends have one cause, read from opposite ends. America paid buyers and stopped. Europe obliges manufacturers.
EU battery-electric sales reached 1.64 million in the first eight months, up 45%, with 16 models priced under €25,000 due on sale by the end of the year.
A fleet-average rule makes a cheap electric car a compliance requirement. A consumer rebate makes it a promotion, and promotions end.
America’s bright spots were product rather than policy. Toyota sold five times as many electric cars as a year earlier, Lexus grew similarly and Rivian rose 46%, while GM’s Equinox EV fell 92.4%.
Cox’s analyst also credited petrol above $4 a gallon, which does not travel. The American average was $4.41 that quarter, against an EU weighted average of €2.092 a litre, about $8.95 a gallon.
The other difference is who is selling. Chinese brands took a record 10.9% of the European market in June, on 150,272 registrations, more than double a year earlier.
Europe’s rules are being rewritten even so. Parliament’s rapporteur would soften the 2035 target to a 90% cut, which campaigners model as cutting sales of the cheapest models by about three quarters by 2030.
The carmaker worst hit in America is German. Volkswagen’s US electric sales fell 90% in a quarter.

20,2026-10-14,"Life feels especially expensive these days as inflation and rising costs of many essentials stretch household budgets.
However, we still need to eat. Two of my biggest money-saving strategies right now include cooking more meals at home and seeking out cheaper groceries for my family of four.
Luckily, a lot of grocery chains are betting on price cuts to attract shoppers right now — and the discount grocers Aldi and Lidl are especially on my radar.
Both German-owned retailers primarily stock their own private-label products, which allows them to cut out the middlemen and offer lower prices to customers. 
Since I have both a Lidl and an Aldi within a 10-minute drive of my house, I decided to visit each to compare the selections, shopping experience, and prices to find the better store for me.
At Aldi, shoppers must insert a quarter into a special slot in order to use a shopping cart. They get their quarter back when they return it.
It sounds like a smart strategy to incentivize people to put carts back and keep the parking lot clean. Many fans of the retailer have even embraced this by carrying special keychains that hold their ""Aldi quarters."" 
However, I don't always have a quarter with me and didn't love this extra step. In comparison, my local Lidl had carts available to borrow, no quarter needed, which I like a lot better.
The two chains originated in Germany, but Aldi has been in the United States longer. Its first US location opened in 1976, while Lidl didn't arrive until 2017.
That said, the Lidl I visited opened in 2017, and the Aldi in 2007 — a decade prior, which could explain why it felt a bit more dated. 
This Aldi felt like a typical discount chain to me when I walked in — the lighting seemed surprisingly dim, the floor tiles didn't look very clean, and some areas felt cluttered, with product haphazardly strewn about on some of the shelves.
First impressions count, and it really started my shopping on a sour note.
My local Lidl felt more welcoming to me with its higher ceilings, shiny flooring, and much brighter lighting. Everything looked clean, and I didn't necessarily feel like I was walking into a bargain store.
At both chains, most items were displayed in the ready-to-shelve boxes they're shipped in. This differs from traditional grocery stores and is another money-saving move — it makes products quicker to stock.
I was impressed by both stores' selections of ready-made meals, most of which were private-label. There were a number of meals my family would eat, like the chicken fajitas from Aldi and the pulled chicken from Lidl.
The prices were generally similar, typically between $5.99 and $6.99, though Aldi beat Lidl in several side-by-side comparisons.
For example, Aldi's 16-ounce pork carnitas were $5.99 compared to Lidl's for $6.59.
The cereal selections seemed similar and broad at both Lidl and Aldi.
Both stores had a mix of name-brand offerings like Fruity Pebbles, Honey Nut Cheerios, and Lucky Charms, plus private-label options that seemed like dupes for popular cereals like Reese's Puffs and Honey Bunches of Oats.
The cereals at both stores were mostly comparable in price, ranging from just under $2 for private-label options to about $5 for name-brand boxes.
However, in a few side-by-side price comparisons, Aldi just slightly came out on top.
For example, Aldi had a 16.8-ounce box of Cinnamon Toast Crunch for $3.99, or $0.24 an ounce, while Lidl had an 18.8-ounce box for $4.97, or $0.26 an ounce.
As I shopped, I noticed that both stores carried similar product assortments across several categories, and the price differences were often in Aldi's favor, though typically only by a few cents.
On this trip, a gallon of whole milk was $0.06 cheaper at Aldi.
Both retailers had 27-ounce bags of organic seeded bread in packaging that seemed nearly identical (and resembled the packaging for the popular Dave's Killer Bread).
A loaf of Aldi's bread was $4.19, and Lidl's rang in at $4.59.
All in all, the fruit seemed priced similarly between the stores, with a few price differences here and there.
Seedless green grapes were both $1.25 a pound across stores. A 3-pound bag of Mandarin oranges, one of my family's other favorites, was $0.04 cheaper at Aldi. Lidl's 1-pound container of organic strawberries was $4.29, and Aldi's was $4.65.
Aldi's bananas were $0.02 cheaper per pound, but they were in a large box on a wooden pallet near the ground. It made me feel like I was in a budget store.
At Lidl, there was a nicer, eye-level display that felt more akin to how a traditional grocery store would display bananas.
Although the cost of food is important, presentation does matter to me. In terms of displays, Lidl's appealed to me more — especially in the produce section. 
With a new kindergartener, I needed to stock up on lunchbox snacks.
I was pleased to see that the same mixed 18-pack of Frito-Lay chips, with varieties like Cheetos and Doritos, cost $8.97 in both stores.
Plus, a 12-pack of 3.2-ounce squeezable private-label applesauce pouches was $6.49 at both Aldi and Lidl.
This Lidl had a much wider selection of pizzas and more of them in stock than my local Aldi. Aldi's inventory seemed so low, I'd be worried about not being able to snag one on future shopping trips.
However, I could have also visited the store right before a restock. (And Aldi's premade pizzas do get rave reviews — maybe that's why so many were missing.)
Prices were generally comparable, but for the variety I wanted, Aldi's prices were slightly better. A basic, thin-crust pepperoni pizza was $2.49 at Lidl and $2.29 at Aldi.
Aldi is famous for its ""finds"" — a rotating selection of items sold in limited quantities for a limited time.
These change weekly, and many are located in what loyal shoppers jokingly call the ""aisle of shame"" because going down it often leads them to make nonessential impulse buys.
My ""aisle of shame"" was just one aisle. In it, I found a range of items, from foam seat cushions to water bottles to seasonal doormats and kids' activity books, all at discounted prices.
At Lidl, I found about three aisles stocked with nonessential and seasonal ""finds,"" from food-storage containers and frying pans to basic long-sleeved shirts and pumpkin-decorating kits.
At both chains, these selections of ""finds"" rotate frequently, making it fun to see what's new every time you shop. However, since Lidl had more space dedicated to them, it felt more likely I'd find something I wanted there.
By the end of my comparison, it was clear that shopping at Aldi would have saved me a couple of dollars off my bill.
Many products I looked at were priced the same at both retailers, but most were a few cents cheaper at Aldi. It feels like the best chain for anyone who's truly obsessed with savings. 
Personally, though, I preferred my shopping experience at Lidl. The store felt more welcoming and larger, with a broader mix of products. 
Lidl's prices may not have beaten Aldi's overall, but I'd likely still save money by shopping there rather than at a traditional supermarket. 
Of course, prices vary, and this is based on my local store locations and the limited number of products I reviewed during these trips. I'd be curious to visit a newer, more recently updated Aldi to see if it changes my perspective.
I'm also aware that shopping at Lidl won't be an option for as many people in the US. Lidl has around 200 stores in nine US states, all on the East Coast, whereas Aldi is projected to have 2,800 locations across more than 30 US states by the end of this year.
I would shop at Aldi if I didn't have a Lidl nearby to take advantage of the savings — but for now, I'll stick to the brighter, more welcoming store.

When reached for comment, a spokesperson for Aldi said:

21,2026-10-14,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
When your business is “stuck” in neutral, sometimes you have to take an uncomfortable leap of faith to get your business moving again.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
I have been working with a business that is basically “stuck” The business is doing OK, about break-even. But it is not growing and doesn’t have a current path to correct itself. That said, it has a great business opportunity in front of it, but investors do not want to fund the business, largely because the historical unit economics are not as profitable as the future unit economics projected, and that has investors feeling that it is a leap of faith farther than they are willing to jump. 
They are looking at opening a new location, which will materially propel the business to new heights, maximizing revenues, profits and unit economics while proving it can successfully open a new market. The business needs $1 million to comfortably open this location but has only $500,000 to work with today. But sometimes a business has no choice but to take a big risk  to get it “unstuck.”
Doing nothing is clearly the safest path in this scenario, as it will not put the company in a materially negative cash position, in terms of sources and uses of capital. Why take the risk of signing up for a project that you know will be 50% underfunded out of the gate, on the hope you will find the extra needed capital in the next few months to fill that gap; a financing that may never happen.  At least today, the business is breaking even and has enough cash in the bank to meet its needs. Don’t do it!
If the business does nothing, it will never get itself “unstuck.” At the end of the day, who really wants to be running a break-even business? Executives are not being fully paid, which is demoralizing them, and investors are not seeing any growth story or cash distributions, so they are not happy either. The company is simply on the “hamster wheel,” running as fast as they can but making no material progress.  That is not sustainable. Let’s do it!
By taking this risk, it will accomplish a lot of things for this company. It will put the business back into growth mode, which current investors will like. It will prove out the future unit economics and successfully open a new market, which future investors will like. It will materially increase the company’s profitability, enabling management to receive their full salaries. And it finally gets the business “unstuck,” taking it to the next level of growth. Let’s do it!
Risks wouldn’t be risks if they didn’t present downside disadvantages. If the company doesn’t secure the financing needed to cover the shortfall in the next few months, it could go out of business with no cash in the bank. And by adding this new location, the business is also taking on a long-term lease liability that will persist for years to come. And there is no guarantee that this new location will perform the way you hope, so if anything falls short of plan, it will further exacerbate this liquidity crunch. Don’t do it!
You can see why this situation is causing management to lose sleep. They are damned if they do, and they are damned if they don’t. Nine times out of ten, I would always go down the conservative path, and not unnecessarily put the business at risk with no cash cushion to work with. 
But in this case, the business is already in jeopardy; it is not fun or economically rewarding for anyone involved, which is another way of saying it is “dead” already. Sometimes you need to take the leap of faith that everything will work out in the end. It may be painfully uncomfortable for the next year, holding your breath, wondering how you are going to make ends meet. But if you can endure the next year of ramping up the new location, the business should be in a much better position by then. That’s a position that you would never have achieved had you not taken the risk. And, in the worst-case scenario of not raising funding and the company is forced out of business, maybe that is the outcome you were heading towards anyway in your current “stuck” form.  
“Go big, or go home”, in this case, as the upside far outweighs the downside, which is not materially different than where the business was today.
As you study your own businesses, figure out if you are “stuck” or not. Are your revenues flat? Are your profits break-even or less? Are you attractive to investors in your current form? Is your staff fully compensated and happy? Are your current investors seeing a clear path to a return on their investment? Depending on your answers, you will be a good candidate to take an “uncomfortable” leap to get your business “unstuck”.

22,2026-10-14,"Traditional joysticks typically use potentiometers or hall effect sensors to determine the displacement of the stick itself when moved by the operator. Force-sensing joysticks are considered more desirable for certain purposes like side-stick flight simulators, because they measure the force applied instead, as per the name. They can be expensive to buy off-the-shelf, but you can build a cheap set on your own if you know what you’re doing, as [tekasza] demonstrates.
The build that [tekasza] put together is based around the Teensy 4.0 microcontroller. It’s set up to read a series of load cells that determine the force applied to the stick in the X and Y axes. The cells are wired up to HX711 load cell amplifiers so they can be easily read by the microcontroller. The Teensy does the work to take the load cell readings and convert them into axis outputs for a USB HID game controller, allowing the stick to be used with just about any game or simulator on the market. [tekasza] has the stick integrated with pedals as well as a whole bunch of buttons and switches to control other simulator features, built up into a full seated sim setup.
If you’ve ever wanted to use a force-sensitive stick like modern fighter pilots do, this build could be very relevant to your interests. We’ve also seen plenty of other great sim hardware builds in recent years, too.
