23,2026-10-14,"The reception of Revolut’s new global headquarters at YY London in Canary Wharf. 
Revolut has launched its own mobile phone plan in Germany, the company announced today. The fintech, which recently won a French banking licence, is pushing further beyond its banking app.
Revolut Mobile costs €14.99 a month and includes 120GB of data at full speed, then unlimited data at a slower 2 Mbit/s, plus unlimited calls and texts within Germany.
Also, customers can cancel each month, and there are no connection or switching fees.
The service runs on Telefónica Germany’s network, with speeds of up to 100 Mbit/s. It is run by telecoms firm 1GLOBAL, with Revolut acting on its behalf.
The plan works only as an eSIM and is set up inside the Revolut app. It will be rolled out to the company’s more than 3 million customers in Germany over the coming weeks.
Users get 40GB of roaming data in Europe, a free NordVPN subscription, and a pass for messaging apps in more than 140 other countries.
They can keep their number, add up to three extra numbers, or pay more for a so-called VIP number. Revolut’s reward points can cover all or part of the bill.
“The telecom industry has been rigid and complex for years,” Beatrice Cossa-Dumurgier, Revolut’s chief executive for Western Europe, wrote on LinkedIn. “We’re bringing the same disruptive energy to mobile that we brought to banking.”
Revolut Mobile was built as an internal startup under the company’s “New Bets” programme, she said.
The company now has 11 product lines, each bringing in more than €120 million a year, it said, and has more than 80 million customers worldwide.
Germany is not the first market for the service. Revolut started rolling out Revolut Mobile in the UK in December 2025, on Vodafone’s network.
“Our partnership with Revolut and 1GLOBAL marks a new chapter for the German mobile market,” said Jörg Kablitz, chief partner and wholesale officer at Telefónica Germany.
Revolut has been adding products well outside payments, such as bundling free ChatGPT Go into its paid subscriptions.

24,2026-10-14,"Europe's most valuable company says its order book runs past the bubble warnings that hit chip stocks this week
ASML air freight containers in Tainan, May 2025
ASML says its supply plans mean it cannot fully meet demand in 2027 or 2028, and some customers want to place orders for 2029 and 2030. It raised the low end of its 2026 sales guidance and its shares rose 2.8%.
ASML says it will not be able to meet demand for its machines in 2027 or 2028. It is looking for ways to build more.
The Dutch company raised the low end of its 2026 sales guidance, taking the range to between €44B and €45B, Bloomberg reported, and its shares rose 2.8% to €1,551.60 in Amsterdam.
In July it said it would add 30% more capacity for 2027 and was studying a further 30% for 2028. Chief executive Christophe Fouquet now says it is investigating going beyond that.
Customers are asking every single day for more tools, Fouquet said. Some are expressing an interest in placing orders for 2029 and even 2030.
That is an awkward week to say it. Chip stocks sold off after Broadcom’s chief executive compared investor sentiment to a casino, and Seoul fell 7% intraday.
ASML is the hardest evidence in that argument. Its most advanced tools take more than a year between order and delivery, so its book is a record of what chipmakers have already committed to rather than what they say.
The financial warnings are about something else. The IMF called the sector’s circular financing wrong-way risk this week, because the investment sours when the promised revenue does.
An order for a lithography machine is not a revenue promise. It is a purchase of a physical object from the only company that makes it.
That monopoly has made ASML Europe’s most valuable company. Its shares are up 64% this year, and it was about $300B short of a trillion in July.
Most of the rise came early. The stock was up about 60% by mid-July, so it has been close to flat through the autumn.
It also remains an exception at home. Europe’s best earnings quarter in three years was an energy story, not an AI one.
Pricing has moved too. ASML has completed price conversations with key customers, with the effect visible partly through 2027 and fully from 2028, and China remains about a fifth of this year’s revenue.
A second Dutch campus is under construction, with the first phase due in 2029. That is the horizon customers are now asking it to quote for.

25,2026-10-14,"A senior Microsoft cloud boss has asked team leaders to find ways to cut costs by at least 20%, The Information reported, citing people who know about the plans.
We have not independently verified the report, and Microsoft disputes it.
“Azure leadership has not directed teams to develop cost-cutting plans,” a Microsoft spokesperson told The Information.
“Every responsible company prepares for a range of scenarios and market dynamics, both positive and negative,” the spokesperson added.
According to the report, the request came from Girish Bablani, who runs the Azure group that builds software for Microsoft’s cloud servers. The group has about 11,500 staff, Stocktwits reported, citing The Information.
The savings would help pay for Microsoft’s heavy spending on AI data centres, the report said. They would also fund higher pay to retain staff being courted by rivals such as Meta and Anthropic.
The exact steps are still being discussed, and could include ending deals with outside contractors. The report did not say whether jobs would go. Microsoft last made deep cuts in July 2025, when it laid off about 9,000 people.

Under that plan, Arun Ulag will oversee Bablani and other top leaders, such as Azure’s tech chief Mark Russinovich and security head Charlie Bell. Ulag will also run Azure Core, which sells computing power to firms including OpenAI.
Microsoft has been spending heavily to build up AI capacity. On Monday, it said its own Maia chips would become the main system for running its AI services from 2027. It plans to share more at its Ignite conference next month.

Work from home is dead: You've probably heard some version of this declaration from your boss over the past year. But the numbers show that WFH is actually very much here to stay. The prevalence of working from home has barely budged over the last two years, at around 26% of all American work days.
That's a lot less than the 62% we saw in the early months of the pandemic — but a near quadrupling since 2019, when that share was at a miniscule 7%. Had the pandemic never happened, it probably would have taken us 40 years to get to work from home as much as we do today.
We've reached a new normal for the corporate workplace — one where remote jobs have become a bigger minority, and it's also become totally normal for white-collar professionals to work from home at least a day or two a week.
That shift has brought about all kinds of other changes in American life — some profound, some life-saving, some strange and unexpected. Nobody's reflected on those changes more than Nick Bloom, an economist at Stanford who's the world's leading expert on remote work.
When I first spoke to him in early 2021, Bloom was right in the midst of helping rewrite corporate America's work policies, presenting his research to managers at companies including Microsoft, Facebook, Google, and Apple. He warned that years of intense turmoil would follow as employees and employers fought over the new terms of where we work — and he couldn't have been more right. Now that the dust has largely settled, I caught up with him at his house on one of his WFH days to take stock of the most lasting impacts of America's work-from-home revolution.
One of the biggest reasons why people love working from home is because it saves them time. The average commute in the US is about 40 minutes each way, and ahead of venturing out into the world, you need to spend a little extra time making yourself presentable to the world by showering and getting dressed. Rolling out of bed and opening your laptop? That takes 30 seconds. By Bloom's estimates, the typical American worker saves an hour and a half for every day they work from home. For those in WFH-friendly sectors like tech and finance, those time savings average about 3 hours a week.
What have people done with that extra time? They're getting more sleep and exercise, and they're cooking — and if they have kids, they're spending a lot more time on parenting. One study in the Netherlands found that when parents were allowed to work from home, their kids scored higher on an important standardized test that helps determine their high school track. ""It's because you're home,"" Bloom says. ""You know that your kid is up there doing their homework and you can smell it if they're smoking weed.""
In 2020, 77% of Americans without a disability had a job, compared with only 32% for those with a disability. In a lot of cases, disabled people wanted to work but couldn't in an office-only world. Maybe they couldn't drive because they were blind, or they had knee and back issues that made navigating the stairs at the office too difficult.
The boom in remote and hybrid jobs in the pandemic changed that, increasing the ranks of disabled people with full-time roles by more than a quarter million people. Add to that all the women who, thanks to the flexibility of working from home, no longer had to quit working to juggle their childcare responsibilities.
That's been great for those workers, and great for the economy as a whole. ""It's not impossible that hybrid increases labor participation rates by 1 to 2%, which probably increases GDP by about 1 to 2%,"" says Bloom. That would amount to an additional $300 billion to $600 billion in economic value. ""This is huge.""
From 2023 to 2025, people who worked from home at least one day a week had more babies compared to daily office-goers, Bloom and his cofounders found. They also planned to have more kids in the future. That's likely because of the increased flexibility of remote work that makes having more kids feel feasible — and also some basic biology. ""My friend had this great line,"" Bloom tells me. ""He said, 'My wife and I discovered she can't get pregnant by email.'""
Bloom estimates that working from home is associated with a roughly 5% increase in fertility in the US and Northern Europe — statistically significant, but not huge. Still, any increase is desperately needed in much of the developed world, which is fighting a declining birth rate. The fertility bump, Bloom notes, has been the most pronounced among women 30 and older, who have college degrees, and already have one kid.
In the early days of the lockdown, urban dwellers around the world got to enjoy better air quality. Did these improvements continue even once the workplace settled into a new hybrid world? Bloom and his coauthors looked at levels of nitrogen dioxide — which is linked to lung and heart conditions — across US cities to see.
In 2025, the largest city centers had 8% less nitrogen dioxide than in 2019. That translates to between 1,500 and 3,500 fewer deaths every year in the country.
One clear loser of WFH has been city centers. Cities from San Francisco to Washington DC are struggling with budget shortfalls that they've attributed to a weaker commercial real estate market (in San Francisco, 29% of office space sat vacant in the second quarter, eight times higher than in 2019). In an even dire state are public transit systems in major cities. BART, which connects San Francisco to its neighboring areas, used to be able to cover about 70% of its operating costs with fares and parking. Now, it's 23%. Next month, Bay Area residents will vote on a ballot measure that would increase the sales tax to keep BART and other public transit systems afloat.

Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
First, it was work from home; now it's AI. Is it a coincidence that these two asteroids hurtled into white-collar work within the span of just a few years? Bloom argues that the two are related. The transition to remote and hybrid work, he says, made way for the rapid uptake of AI in the workplace.
For one, the switch to digital communication — Slack, email, Zoom — created an explosion of data for companies to train their AI models on. Before, these conversations were happening in person, disappearing into the ether as soon as they escaped our vocal cords. Now, so many of them are happening on some digital medium, recorded for posterity to fine-tune the machines.
Businesses can't get enough of this data. That's why Google recently purchased Spirit Airlines for $10 million in a recent bankruptcy auction. Google's goal wasn't to start its own airline, but to get access to Spirit's 100 million emails, 500 million Microsoft Teams messages, and 30 million lines of code.
Bloom argues that the shift to digital communication also created new norms for the AI era. Imagine your coworker pulling out their phone to record the audio of a meeting so they could send around their AI-transcribed notes to everyone afterwards. That would have been pretty weird, back in 2019. But that's totally normal now, thanks to the fact that we started using Zoom a lot more in the pandemic, and because Zoom has a record button. ""It's just changed a lot of stuff that's made it more feasible to use AI,"" Bloom says.
Most white-collar workers like that they get to work from home more often than they used to. They see the remote work revolution as largely a positive one. But if it also accelerated AI's spread in the corporate workplace, that's more of a mixed bag. Would they go back to the daily slog into the office if it meant not having to worry about losing their job to Claude?
After all, Bloom notes, it's the fully remote back-office jobs that are probably the easiest to automate. The jobs that still require in-person charm, collaboration, and logistics are the ones the machines still can't touch.

27,2026-10-14,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
Benjamin Fix and Greg Reinecker started a business to appeal to the next generation.
Copied to clipboard
About a decade ago, plumbing-industry veteran Benjamin Fix was tasked with creating a bathroom collection for the next generation of consumers — at the time, millennials. 
As Fix considered what might resonate with young shoppers, a few problems became apparent. Most millennials were renters, not homeowners who could make permanent changes to their bathrooms. Additionally, the plumbing industry’s “antiquated” messaging didn’t appeal to them, and the products themselves lacked imagination. 
“We had three colors, which was chrome, chrome and chrome,”  Fix tells Entrepreneur. “And then there was nothing else. This next generation wants to have colors to express themselves. They want to have pink or green.”
If the plumbing industry was going to successfully cater to young consumers, its products needed to evolve. And, though Gen Z is now the newest set of shoppers on the scene, an interest in fun, colorful designs remains consistent. 
Fix teamed up with his former colleague, Greg Reinecker, who became a co-founder and product designer at their business: sproos!, a renter-friendly, direct-to-consumer home and bath brand. 
Fix’s partner Cecile Scius also joined the effort as the company’s manager of customer success and community lead. 
Sproos! officially launched in November 2022 after the co-founders raised about $250,000 from professional contacts and friends, and it has been growing since. 
The business brought in $3.9 million in revenue in 2025 and is on track to cross the $10 million mark this year. It also boasts about 35,000 active subscribers receiving its shower filtration cartridges (roughly 70% of new customers opt in) and a monthly churn rate under 3%. 
Entrepreneur sat down with Fix and Scius to learn more about how they’re cultivating a loyal community that helps sproos! keep leveling up. 
Like most new businesses angling for adoption, sproos! tapped into social media from the start. The company sent products to lifestyle creators on Instagram, and more recently TikTok, to generate bathroom-makeover content. College students hoping to spruce up their rental spaces were a particularly strong pool. 
However, unlike some brands that might consider those published posts the end goal, for sproos!, they’re only one facet of an ongoing — and invaluable — dynamic that hinges on deep consumer relationships. 
Listening to customer feedback led to two critical additions at the company: a shower head filtration product and its supporting subscription model. 
Sproos! holds round tables with its most loyal community members, dubbed “super fans,” four times a year, which helped develop its first filtration product. 
The company learned exactly what people wanted, from pain-point removals to aesthetic preferences, so it could address them before its product ever hit the market. The development process took place over about two years. 
Sproos!’s subscription model, which delivers a new filter cartridge to subscribers every three months, also emerged as a result of those discussions — and proved a boon for business. 
“This was the jackpot,” Fix says. “The customers are super happy. They’re telling us how much they love our filtration. And we can create a recurring revenue model. That’s a game-changer.”
The subscription model provides helpful stability and a foundation on which to build, Scius adds. 
In addition, every month, sproos! generates a report based on what people are calling, writing or posting on social media about. “ We extract data from all the customer communication that we have in order to see trends [so that] we can improve,” Scius says. 
Sproos! can use that data to fix existing problems, develop new products or launch highly requested colors. 
For example, the report once highlighted a shower head leak that led to a significant unlock. 
After noting an increase in leaks month over month, the company’s engineering department drilled down on the cause: the washers on the hose. The “little donuts” were small and transparent, so if they happened to fall off, many people didn’t realize it. 
Sproos! added a lip to the mold for extra security — and it became an industry standard. 
“ Our customers are not just informing product betterments within our company, but when they see what we’re doing, it then spreads to all of the major brands as well,” Scius says. “Because if we’re having the problem, we’re not the only ones. We’re just really adamant about doing something about it.”
Following its success with DTC, sproos! began to sell on Amazon and continues to explore brick-and-mortar expansion. “Of course, we have ambitions to grow,” Fix says, “and for us, going into retail is a good way to reach new audiences, and to bring people into our ecosystem.”
The company is adding to its shower-system product line, branching into storage, curtains, bath mats, lighting and accessories, as it focuses on an expanding HomeGoods partnership and upcoming Home Depot rollout.
And that initial HomeGoods placement? It, too, was a direct result of the company’s community, which cited HomeGoods as one of their favorite places to shop. 
The company launched with a small HomeGoods placement in the U.S. last summer. It went well and brought larger second and third orders, and interest from Canada HomeGoods. 
“You don’t have to go from zero to 100 in one step,” Scius says. “Growing a business is incremental.  The goal is really to continuously improve, to continuously grow, to continuously learn. That’s how you’re going to get to the finish line.”
Urban Outfitters recently featured sproos! in a back-to-school curation, and the company partnered with student housing developer Greystar for on-campus pop-ups, which pushed local sales up 200% and got its products built into lease-renewal incentives. 
Sproos! started on a mission to understand its target consumer, and the company continues to see success because of that ongoing commitment. The market will let you know exactly what it wants — but you have to be willing to listen, Fix notes. 
“The consumers will tell you,” Fix says. “Be open to that feedback, and don’t overanalyze it. If 10 people ask for pink, just do pink. Be humble enough to have your mind changed by the people who you want to reach.”
About a decade ago, plumbing-industry veteran Benjamin Fix was tasked with creating a bathroom collection for the next generation of consumers — at the time, millennials. 
As Fix considered what might resonate with young shoppers, a few problems became apparent. Most millennials were renters, not homeowners who could make permanent changes to their bathrooms. Additionally, the plumbing industry’s “antiquated” messaging didn’t appeal to them, and the products themselves lacked imagination. 
“We had three colors, which was chrome, chrome and chrome,”  Fix tells Entrepreneur. “And then there was nothing else. This next generation wants to have colors to express themselves. They want to have pink or green.”

28,2026-10-14,"INmune Bio Inc. and Quantum Computing Inc. collaboration
Boca Raton, Florida, October 14th, 2026, Financewire
Collaboration combines INmune Bio’s clinical research with QCi’s Dirac-3S quantum optimization machine to identify potential new treatment indications and biomarkers for XPro. Initial engagement marks QCi’s first commercial customer in pharmaceutical and biotechnology markets, expanding the application of quantum computing to drug development.
INmune Bio Inc. (Nasdaq: INMB) and Quantum Computing Inc. (Nasdaq: QUBT) today announced a collaboration to apply quantum computing, artificial intelligence (AI) and data analytics to identify new potential applications for XPro (XPro1595), INmune Bio’s investigational treatment for Alzheimer’s disease and other disease conditions.
The collaboration brings together INmune Bio’s data from pre-clinical research through four clinical trials and understanding of disease biology with QCi’s Dirac-3S quantum optimization machine. Working with clinical and publicly available biomedical datasets, the companies intend to use quantum computing alongside AI and conventional data analytics to uncover relationships that could help find diseases driven by the same biology XPro targets, and to prioritize those with the greatest potential for success.
The Stage 1 engagement represents QCi’s first commercial customer in the pharmaceutical and biotechnology market. The agreement also provides options for additional work as INmune Bio advances its clinical development programs.
“We’ve built a large clinical dataset on Alzheimer’s and other diseases, backed by years of work with XPro,” said David Moss, Chief Executive Officer of INmune Bio. “This collaboration allows us to examine patient populations and biomarkers most likely to predict responses by analyzing large data sets. We’ll look for the diseases with the greatest potential for success, those whose human biology matches what we’ve already seen XPro do, starting with rare diseases where trials can be smaller, faster and more capital-efficient, and where qualifying pediatric approvals may earn a Priority Review Voucher.”
Together, the companies will build on INmune Bio’s research into XPro to explore where its approach to targeting inflammation may offer the greatest potential. XPro is designed to selectively inhibit soluble tumor necrosis factor (TNF), a protein involved in inflammation, while preserving other TNF functions important to the immune system. INmune Bio is investigating its potential in neurological and rare diseases where this targeted approach may offer a therapeutic benefit.
Using its Dirac-3S entropy quantum optimization machine alongside AI and conventional analytics, QCi will look for patterns and relationships across these datasets that could help researchers better understand treatment responses, identify promising patient populations and prioritize new indications for further study.
“This is a great example of what practical quantum computing can look like today,” said Yuping Huang, Chairman and Chief Executive Officer of QCi. “INmune Bio has a complex scientific problem and a substantial body of clinical and biological data. Our job is to bring new computing capabilities to that problem and see what we can learn. The potential to help researchers make better decisions about future treatments is exciting, and it opens an important new market for QCi.”
An important part of the program will be testing where quantum optimization adds value. The companies plan to compare results from QCi’s quantum optimization machine with appropriate conventional statistical and machine-learning methods, evaluating which approaches are best suited to different parts of the analysis.
The work is also intended to establish a repeatable discovery process through quantum computing that INmune Bio can use with additional datasets and potential disease indications.
For INmune Bio, the collaboration offers a new way to evaluate the broader potential of XPro and prioritize future development opportunities. For QCi, it represents an opportunity to showcase how its room-temperature, low-power quantum computing technology can be applied to complex, real-world problems in pharmaceutical research.
About INmune Bio Inc.
INmune Bio Inc. is a publicly traded (NASDAQ: INMB), late-stage biotechnology company focused on developing treatments that target the innate immune system to fight disease. The Company’s clinical-development strategy centers on advanced precision medicine, matching drug mechanisms directly to patient biology to optimize clinical outcomes.
About Quantum Computing Inc.
Quantum Computing Inc. (Nasdaq: QUBT) is an integrated quantum photonics company developing and manufacturing technologies that use light to compute complex information, capture hard-to-detect signals and communicate more securely. Headquartered in Hoboken, New Jersey, QCi has operations across the United States.

29,2026-10-14,"Beijing extracted a filter letting the government approve model updates, and got a date. Siri AI reaches neither China nor the EU
Apple plans to launch Apple Intelligence in mainland China in December with iOS 27.3, two years after the US, excluding the rebuilt Siri AI. Siri AI is also still missing in the EU, with no indication of when approval might come.
Apple will ship Apple Intelligence in mainland China in December, two years after America got it. The version Europe is still waiting for is not in it.
The launch comes with an iOS 27.3 update and excludes the rebuilt Siri AI, Bloomberg reported, citing people with knowledge of the plans.
What ships is the 2024 set. Genmoji, notification summaries, writing tools and removing objects from photos, the features widely panned at the time for not working properly.
What does not ship is this year’s work. That means the rebuilt Siri, photo expansion, upgraded Genmoji and visual intelligence in the camera, and executives are not optimistic about Siri AI arriving there soon.
Getting this far took concessions. Apple trained its own model for China with Alibaba’s help, becoming the first foreign company Beijing has approved to offer a proprietary AI model in the mainland.
The guardrails were built with Alibaba and include a filter that lets the government approve ongoing updates to the models. Baidu handles search and some Siri features.
Image generation is out. Apple’s Image Playground will not ship there, and some text generation will be limited in some apps, to comply with the rules.
Approval came slowly. The Cyberspace Administration logged the service in July, about 22 months after Apple Intelligence launched elsewhere, and permits covering recent iPhones followed on 29 September.
It nearly went out early. Apple Intelligence briefly appeared in mainland settings in March, before approval, and was pulled.
Tim Cook, now Apple’s executive chairman rather than its chief executive, attended a White House state dinner for Xi Jinping last month.
Europe is the other hard market, and the obstacle there is different. Most of this year’s features cleared on schedule in September, but Siri AI is still missing from iPhones, iPads and Apple Watches in the EU.
Nobody will say when. People with knowledge of the matter say it is unclear whether or when Apple will get approval for a local release.
The two delays are not the same. In China Apple negotiated terms and got a date, while in Europe its talks with Brussels have produced a date for nothing.

30,2026-10-14,"CoreWeave, a cloud computing company operating 51 data centers, had a brutal day on the stock and credit markets yesterday, and may indicate troubles coming for the broader AI industry.
The trouble started when yields on their corporate debt spiked dramatically.
Venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya tweeted the following to his 2.4M followers:

It didn’t help when Elon Musk replied, “Yikes.”
Accompanied by an essay titled “Deep Dive: Is the AI buildout creating a private credit bubble?”
The AI buildout is increasingly funded by debt, and private credit is playing a much bigger role than it did even a year ago.
In the second quarter of 2026, Apollo and Blackstone led a $35 billion vehicle to buy chips and lease them to Anthropic. UBS called it the largest private credit deal on record. For comparison, direct lenders made about $23 billion of loans to private-equity-backed U.S. companies that quarter. One AI financing was larger than all the traditional PE-backed direct lending PitchBook counted during the same period.
…
The structure of some AI financings adds another layer to the picture. CoreWeave borrowed for five years against customer contracts averaging about three, while Meta’s Hyperion bonds run to 2049 against leases renewed every four years.
The market is also already distinguishing among these deals. Apollo bought debt financing chips leased to xAI at 99 cents in December 2025, and after SpaceX bought xAI, it traded as high as 106. Project Jupiter, an $18 billion AI data-center financing, later traded at 89 to 91 cents as investors reassessed the project’s credit.
So rather than ask whether AI itself is a bubble, our team at Social Capital looked beneath the surface at a different question. Is private credit, which is funding a growing portion of the AI buildout, a bubble?
Interested readers will need to check out the full report to find out Palihapitiya’s conclusion, but what I can tell you is that the impact on CoreWeave’s stock price was immediate and dramatic:

Yahoo! Finance added that CoreWeave’s debt has tripled in a year:

Twitter market analyst Hedgie posted the following graph:

Hedgie commented:
CoreWeave stock fell about 13% Wednesday after its bonds due in 2032 traded at a record 14.5% yield. CoreWeave sold those bonds in June at 9.625%, so in four months the cost of lending to it without collateral jumped about five points. Venture capitalist Chamath Palihapitiya posted the yield chart as a warning about a private credit bubble around AI.
I’ve been saying that the AI bubble lives in the debt more than the demand, and CoreWeave is an example. It raised more than $35 billion this year, carries about $52 billion in debt and leases, and paid $640 million in net interest last quarter, roughly a quarter of its revenue. The $104 billion backlog sounds great, but a backlog doesn’t cover interest until the data centers get built and the customers pay.
Palihapitiya’s numbers show private lenders charge AI borrowers almost the same spread as everyone else, 6.2 points versus 6.1. I think the bond market just repriced that risk for them. My guess is CoreWeave’s next raise costs more, comes backed by chips, or dilutes shareholders, and Nebius and the other AI cloud companies face the same questions. If you own a private credit fund, find out how much AI and software debt it holds, because those loans don’t get repriced every day the way CoreWeave’s bonds do.
A Bit More About CoreWeave
This Wired profile from 2024 tells CoreWeave’s story the way they like it told.
After starting out as a bunch of bros who pooled their fantasy football money to buy GPUs to do some cryptomining as a joke but then took it seriously when prices soared in 2016, they had to pivot when crypto prices crashed:
When crypto prices cratered heading into 2019, Venturo and team pivoted. They raised money to buy cut-price GPUs from struggling crypto miners, rebranded as CoreWeave, and offered up their tens of thousands GPUs as a specialist cloud service for companies in need of the powerful chips, like visual effects studios and AI startups. GPUs are essential to machine learning projects like training algorithms to process images or text. CoreWeave was unknown in those spheres, but won over clients by dropping everything to address support queries. “We would resolve things in 15 minutes,” a manager from that era says. “The dedication to customers was phenomenal.”
Everything changed after OpenAI debuted ChatGPT late in 2022. A rush of interest and investment in all things generative AI caused a GPU shortage—especially of Nvidia’s prized chips. With its couple of data centers packed with GPUs and a long-standing relationship with Nvidia, CoreWeave quickly became vital to the accelerating AI hype train. The company picked up clients including Microsoft, OpenAI’s crucial sponsor, and image-generation startup Stability AI, according to former CoreWeave employees. Venturo’s biggest regret is not ordering more Nvidia chips back when he could. “If I had unlimited amounts, I could have it all sold in two weeks,” he says.
Things still worked out. As demand for CoreWeave’s GPUs surged through 2023, the company piled its new revenue into buying more of the chips and building out new data centers. A company that began as a beer-money side project now doesn’t touch crypto, and is all in on cloud computing. Over the past year the startup has picked up a $2.3 billion loan—using GPUs as collateral—and nearly $2.2 billion in investment funding, including $1.1 billion announced this week, from investors including Nvidia. With its latest infusion of cash, the startup’s valuation jumped to $19 billion.
By the end of this year, CoreWeave expects to have 28 data center campuses spread across a dozen or more US states, Europe, and potentially Asia—overall more than nine times its footprint at the beginning of 2023.
Note they’ve since more than doubled the number of data centers they operate while tripling their debt.
As of June, those lovable bros had sold $2.3 billion in stock since their IPO, per Bloomberg.
But let’s get back to their debt.
Ed Zitron Would Like a Word
In his October 6th piece, “Credit Crunch“, Zitron writes:
CoreWeave, which has [obscene] spreads of 672bps to 882bps, with the effective yield on its shortest-dated debt (four years) sitting at 11.53% and its longest-dated (a mere six years) sitting at 13.49%.
There are, of course, ways around borrowing on your credit profile. For example, when CoreWeave opened an $8.5 billion delayed draw term loan facility in March 2026, it was able to get it rated as investment-grade and at SOFR (the percentage on overnight borrowing from the Fed for banks) plus 2.25%, all because the counterparty was Meta, and thus the underlying payments would be considered “safe.”
This may seem like the cheat code to get around all these horrifying rates, except CoreWeave, per analysts at UBS, needs to raise $102 billion in debt through 2030, which means it will have to keep raising on its own two feet.
Well, there’s a problem there.
Then he cites the following from The Information saying cracks are beginning to form” in the AI data center debt boom:
Bond markets are getting tough for lower-rated borrowers. One example: CleanSpark, which is developing a data center for Meta Platforms, had to offer investors big concessions to land financing earlier this month.
That deal is among the clearest signs yet that financing the data center boom is getting expensive across the board, and investors are getting picky about which new projects they’ll back. The bank loan market too is showing signs of strain, with some lenders like Société Générale, Sumitomo Mitsui Banking Corp. and Mitsubishi UFJ Financial Group becoming more selective in lending to data center projects, people arranging the deals say. Those dynamics mean companies could struggle to borrow for planned data centers, jeopardizing growth plans for the broader AI industry.
Zitron has more commentary on CoreWeave in “The Hater’s Guide To Junk” from the 9th:
…this industry needs hundreds of billions of dollars to fund the theoretical buildout of these potentially-possible data centers — CoreWeave alone, per UBS, needs to raise $102 billion in debt through 2030 — at a rate with no historical comparison. Even the railroad bubble of the 1800s had, based on rough calculations of inflation, only around $250 billion in total investment compared to the $400 billion in issuance expected from hyperscalers alone in 2027.
He also cites Standard & Poor’s discussion of CoreWeave’s material weaknesses in its accounting mentioned in its S-1:
CoreWeave’s efforts to address material weaknesses in internal control over financial reporting are progressing as we expected, but will remain a rating constraint until they are resolved. The company is working to address and remediate these weaknesses through IT risk assessments, enhanced control frameworks, and expanded finance and accounting staff. The measures also include engaging external advisors, completing an enterprise and IT risk assessment, and adding experienced senior leadership to strengthen oversight and execution.
While CoreWeave’s progress and timeline for addressing previously identified material weaknesses align with our expectations, potential credit risks could still arise so long as the weaknesses remain. This is because the company will be required to provide a management report on internal controls beginning with its 2026 Form 10-K, including diminished reliability of financial reporting and an increased risk of financial restatement or receiving an unqualified opinion from auditors, which would constitute a violation of its affirmative covenants under its credit agreements.
Nevertheless, based on the remediation efforts management intends to undertake throughout 2026, we expect the company to do so within this required timeframe.
…
NVIDIA’s $2 billion equity investment in CoreWeave in January 2026 nearly doubled its ownership stake and deepened commercial and technological integration. This includes preferential access to next-generation GPUs, leveraging their financial strength for multiyear AI factory buildout targeting more than 5 GW of incremental capacity by 2030, and integration of CoreWeave’s Mission Control and SUNK software into NVIDIA’s reference architectures.
We view the software integration as particularly significant, because it validates CoreWeave’s proprietary capabilities, enhances differentiation among neocloud providers, and introduces potential for incremental, higher-margin, asset-light software revenue over time.
Zitron goes on to add:
CoreWeave sits in the fourth tier of junk at B+, has been unprofitable for every single quarter of its existence, but is still only B+ because, and I quote, it has “broadened its customer base and reduced concentration through recent contract wins with Meta and OpenAI, as well as targeted efforts to engage downstream customers, including enterprises and AI labs.”
While S&P would argue that it’s making intelligent, market-based judgments, it is near-entirely basing its “positive” outlook on hype, to the point that it sees NVIDIA’s deeply-circular financing — which involves it renting back its own GPUs — as a good thing.
This is almost entirely nonsense, based on no practical evaluation of CoreWeave’s actual capacity, revenues or underlying technology. I would love to see someone from S&P explain what SUNK means, or who actually uses it — it is, from what I can tell, a specialist AI model training environment (making it unlikely to be used by any major AI lab, as they all have their own), and the one customer I can find is Impossible Inc, which I can’t find much about other than Naval Ravikant’s incomprehensible posts about it.
I should also add that NVIDIA’s $2 billion equity investment was not a means of “deepening its commercial and technological integration,” but a blatant attempt to make sure the company was capitalized enough to retain its current rating and continue to raise debt, and somehow NVIDIA backstopping CoreWeave’s data center leases didn’t come up in the evaluation.
This is the same baby-brained logic that potential creditors use too, because, to quote a friend of mine in fixed-income, nobody knows what they’re investing in.
Perhaps the folks at CoreWeave are hip to some of Ed’s concerns.
CoreWeave Targets ‘Unlimited’ Insurance Money
Per the FT:
CoreWeave is chasing insurers’ “unlimited” pools of capital as the Nvidia-backed AI cloud provider seeks cheaper ways to fund the tens of billions of dollars it is spending on chips and other AI infrastructure.
…
Brannin McBee, a co-founder who leads CoreWeave’s financing efforts, said winning investment-grade ratings for more of its chip-backed debt, and eventually the company itself, would allow it to borrow from insurers whose capital “is unlimited. Like, that’s all the money in the world that sits in the insurance tranche.”
…
Earlier this year, CoreWeave secured an $8.5bn loan backed by chips and a long-term contract with Meta to use them — a deal known in the industry as an offtake. It was the first of its chip-backed loans to be rated investment grade in full.
“Demonstrating that we can execute within that market, that’s massive, that’s so big for us,” McBee said in an interview.  The next step is getting insurers to buy loans backed by lower-rated customers, such as AI labs. “I don’t think that non-investment-grade offtakes from us are ready to go into the insurance tranche yet. I do think it’ll get there,” McBee said, adding that the upcoming stock market listings by the big AI labs would help.
…
Paying for the hundreds of thousands of Nvidia AI chips underpinning its platform has also turned CoreWeave into a financing machine. The group has secured more than $35bn this year across chip-backed loans, high-yield bonds, convertible bonds and share sales, bringing its debt and lease obligations to about $52bn at end June.
McBee, a former natural gas and electricity trader, struck the first deal in 2023, a $2.3bn loan led by Blackstone and secured against Nvidia’s graphics processing units and an offtake contract with Microsoft.
It carried an effective interest rate of about 15 per cent, far above typical junk bond yields, but opened a new market for chip-backed debt. The chips sit in subsidiaries, McBee said, and each dollar they earn first pays interest, principal and running costs, with the remaining 20 cents going to the parent. CoreWeave has since signed five more such loans at lower rates, securing about $27bn in total.
Well, that’s reassuring.

31,2026-10-14,"""If our homes freeze up ... our water pipes are gonna burst,"" a Maine mom said.
Americans who rely on heating oil to warm up their homes are bracing for higher costs this fall and winter.
“If you don't have heat, you will freeze to death,” said Brittney Worden, a mother of a young son from Limington, Maine.
“For our fellow Mainers, I'm worried. This is such a high increase and the overall effect that it's going to have on our community -- this is scary,” Worden added.
In Maine, the average statewide price for heating oil is $5.97 per gallon as of Oct. 5, 2026, according to the Maine Department of Energy Resources Heating Fuel Prices. Worden, who also owns a small firewood business, said the increased cost is a top concern for her family.
Worden said she paid about $4 per gallon for heating oil last year.
“I am predicting [paying] close to $5,000 to heat my home this winter. That's a lot of money,” she told ABC News.
Patrick De Haan, head of petroleum analysis at GasBuddy, said the jump in heating oil prices is directly connected to the rise in diesel prices because they are ""essentially the same product"" and are made through the same oil refining process.
""Diesel and heating oil prices are really more tied to the developing state of Ukraine attacking Russian oil refineries,"" De Haan told ABC News. ""Traditionally, Russia is a major supplier of diesel for the global market, and since July, as a result of the drone attacks, Russia has banned the export of diesel, so that has contributed to a very tight global market for diesel and driven prices up significantly.""
Global diesel prices have also been heavily strained by the war in Iran.
The current average cost of a gallon of diesel is $6.29, nearly double the price from last year, he noted.
""We've seen the price of diesel skyrocket,"" he said, adding that heating oil customers could be ""paying potentially $7 a gallon"" in the next few months.
Amid rising costs, President Donald Trump announced on Friday that he made a deal with Russian President Vladimir Putin to immediately import 300,000 tons of its diesel fuel from Russia, but his plan has drawn backlash from a small group of bipartisan senators who oppose lifting Russian oil sanctions amid the war with Ukraine.
The president defended his plan in a social media post on Monday, saying it’s ""the right choice for the American People.""
De Haan said he’s “not sure” if Trump’s plan to purchase diesel from Russia “will take place” because “Russia is finding it very difficult to produce as much diesel for their domestic market.”
“The situation amongst Ukraine and Russia is going to be very impactful for determining where diesel prices are during the winter,” he said.
According to a U.S. Energy Information Administration (EIA) report released last week, households that ""primarily use electricity or heating oil for space heating are expected to spend more,"" though natural gas and propane costs may not rise as much.
""Relatively high natural gas inventories” in the U.S. ""provide a cushion for heating demand, while households that rely on heating oil face higher prices because distillate markets remain tight,"" EIA Administrator Tristan Abbey said.
Bill Morrell, president of Atlantic Heating Company in Portland, Maine, told ABC News the rise in oil prices “hurts everybody.”
“When the oil gets this high, it hurts everybody. It hurts us. We have to pay more for a product that we're not making any more profit on,” Morrell said. “Then it hurts the consumer, obviously the worst, because the prices are astronomical right now and that's not good.”
Heating oil is primarily used to power furnaces, boilers and water heaters in residential or commercial heating systems.
According to the EIA, about 3% of U.S. households buy heating oil and customers will pay about 21% more than last winter.
Morrell said his company, which serves about 4,000 customers, is struggling too. Some of his customers have asked about converting their heating systems to propane or natural gas.
""It's a tough situation for everybody involved,"" he said.
De Haan said prices for natural gas and propane have been ""more insulated"" from the developments in the global market.
""Propane inventories are quite a bit above their average for this time of year. Natural gas supplies are above average for this time of year as well,"" De Haan said, adding that the U.S. has become ""the world's largest producer"" of natural gas.
Elizabeth Minor, a grandmother from Winston-Salem, North Carolina, primarily relies on heating oil to warm up her home.
“It's very sad that we're all struggling here and dealing with these prices of everything. And it's not just me, it's so many others,” she told ABC News.
According to Minor, the cost of filling up one tank of oil has nearly tripled in recent months, so she is looking at utilizing her propane-powered fireplace this winter because it’s cheaper.
“I'm on a fixed income and it's going to be very tight,” she said. “I gotta stretch it out … keep [the] thermostat on the lowest setting possible.”
Scott Glanz, an owner of Top Tier Fuel in Windsor Locks, Connecticut, told ABC News that many of his customers are concerned they won’t be able to heat their homes.
“I feel horrible for a lot of people because it's going to be a struggle this winter if things don't start coming down very shortly,” he said.
According to Glanz, heating oil prices have increased by more than $2 per gallon in the last year, with current prices now reaching $5.69 a gallon.
“I pray that the prices do come down because I just don't want to see people hurt financially,” he added.
Because fewer customers are able to afford filling up more than one tank, Glanz's trucks will have to make more trips to deliver incremental oil orders. Glanz acknowledged this year will be difficult to make a profit.
Worden echoed Glanz's concerns, saying rising costs have led to fear and anxiety in her New England community.
“People have been coming out of the woodwork because they're honestly scared and worried of how they're going to heat their homes,” she said. “If our homes freeze up here, our water pipes are gonna burst.”

33,2026-10-14,"Inflation stands well above the rate recorded prior to the Iran war.
Inflation climbed to a 4-month high in September as the Iran war continued to drive up gasoline prices, according to federal government data released on Wednesday. The reading came in slightly above economists' expectations.
Prices rose 3.7% in September compared to a year earlier, marking an uptick from an annual inflation rate of 3.4% in the prior month.
Inflation stands well above a rate of 2.4% recorded in February prior to the outbreak of the Iran war.
The fresh data arrived as affordability marks a top concern for voters ahead of early November's midterm elections.
Gas prices jumped more than 9% in September from August, accounting for more than half of the overall price increase last month, the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics said. Shelter prices -- which include home-owner payments and rental charges -- also rose in September.
Food prices proved stable, however. The price of food stayed flat in September from August, putting annual inflation for food at 2.5%. That figure, which includes prices for groceries and food eaten away from home, stands well below the annual pace of overall price increases.
The Middle East conflict drove up fuel prices and catapulted inflation to a three-year high in May, but inflation eased slightly over the summer after a preliminary peace agreement established an on-again, off-again pause in fighting.
The tentative accord between the U.S. and Iran expired in August, however, giving way to continued attacks on oil infrastructure and ships in the region.
Oil exports from the Gulf have improved in recent weeks but heightened shipping costs and persistent risks have kept the national average gas price above $4.30 a gallon, AAA data shows. Prior to the Iran war, the average price of U.S. gas stood below $3 a gallon.
Diesel prices, which feed into supply costs for groceries and just about every other product bought in stores, stood at $6.29 a gallon, according to AAA. That figure amounts to an increase of about 66% since the outbreak of the Iran War.
Last month, consumer sentiment dropped close to the lowest level ever recorded in the 74-year history of the University of Michigan's monthly survey.
By some measures, the economy remains robust.
Gross domestic product grew over three months ending in June, defying fear of a downturn triggered by the Iran war. Consumer spending, which accounts for about two-thirds of U.S. economic activity, rose 0.6% in August from July, the largest monthly increase since March 2025, government data on last month showed.
In other areas, however, the economy has wobbled. The inflation report came days after monthly jobs data showed sluggish hiring in September.
Employers added 29,000 jobs last month, a sharp slowdown from 162,000 jobs added in August, the U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics said earlier this month. The figure came in well below economists' expectations.
Prior to the latest jobs report, hiring had proven largely resilient this year, even as shoppers and businesses weathered higher costs.
The combination of elevated inflation and a sturdy labor market prompted the Fed to raise interest rates a quarter of a percentage point last month, marking the central bank's first rate increase since 2023.
""The plain fact is that inflation is too high and has been for too long,"" Fed Chair Kevin Warsh said at a press conference in Washington, D.C., last month.
In theory, an increase to the benchmark interest rate should slash inflation by slowing the economy and eating away at demand, though the policy typically yields results after a lag time lasting several months.
The next meeting of Fed policymakers takes place on Oct. 28, just days before the midterm elections. Investors peg the chance of another quarter-point rate increase at about 45%, according to CME Group’s FedWatch Tool, a measure of market sentiment.

36,2026-10-14,"Economists divided on Fed rate hike as inflation creates uncertainty in run-up to US midterm elections in a tense market.
Save
Share
United States consumer prices jumped in September in the final inflation report before the midterm elections, increasing chances of an interest rate hike later this month.
Annual inflation accelerated to 3.7 percent, up from 3.4 percent in August. On a monthly basis, prices rose by 0.6 percent, according to the Consumer Price Index report released by the Labor Department’s Bureau of Labor Statistics (BLS) on Wednesday.
Petrol prices led the surge as tensions between the US and Iran, as well as Russia and Ukraine, weighed on global energy prices. Petrol prices rose 9.1 percent in September, accounting for more than half of the CPI monthly increase, according to the BLS. They were up 34.2 percent from the same time a year ago.
“The directionless war in Iran continues to disrupt global supply chains, driving up the prices of food and fuel,” Kyle Moore, chief economist at The Century Foundation, an economic think tank, said in a statement provided to Al Jazeera.
Petrol prices have continued to climb. The average price for a gallon of petrol is $4.33 ($1.14 per litre), compared with $4.31 a month ago, according to the American Automobile Association (AAA), which tracks daily petrol prices. Diesel prices are higher, at $6.29 per gallon (1.66 per litre), compared with $6.23 a month ago.
In a post on X, Patrick DeHaan, head of petroleum analysis for GasBuddy, said, “2026 has now seen more days with average gas prices over $4/gal than any year before: 158 days.”
Shelter costs also jumped 0.4 percent from the previous month, following a 0.3 percent increase in August. Food costs were a bright spot in the report, remaining unchanged overall.
Food away from home, including restaurant meals, rose 0.2 percent, but that increase was offset by a 0.1 percent decline in food-at-home prices, which track grocery costs.
At the grocery store, poultry prices jumped 0.7 percent, fruit and vegetable prices rose 0.5 percent, and juice prices increased 1.1 percent. However, milk prices dropped 2.1 percent and beef prices fell 0.9 percent.
Amid heavy investment in AI infrastructure, prices for computer software and accessories have jumped 28 percent from the same time last year.
“The AI boom is playing a huge part, and we have all read about the hundreds of billions being poured into data centers, AI firms, and models,” Rami Cassis, principal of Parabellum Investment, said in comments provided to Al Jazeera.
The inflation data comes days before the US Federal Reserve’s interest rate decision later this month. Last month, the US central bank raised rates by 25 basis points, bringing the target range to 3.75–4.00 percent, the first hike in three years.
Economists are divided over whether the Fed will raise rates again or hold them steady. According to CME FedWatch, which tracks the likelihood of monetary policy decisions, there is a 52.9 percent chance that rates will remain in the 3.75–4.00 percent range, while a 47.1 percent chance of a quarter of a percentage point hike that would bring them to 4.00–4.25 percent.
The inflation data puts pressure on Republicans before the midterm elections on November 3. A recent Reuters/Ipsos poll showed that about 78 percent of Americans blamed US President Donald Trump’s policies for price increases.
US markets trended downward following the inflation report. The Nasdaq fell 0.8 percent and the S&P 500 dropped 0.6 percent, while the Dow Jones Industrial Average was roughly flat in midday trading.

37,2026-10-14,"You're viewing the US website. Looking for the Site edition?
The next AI advantage for entrepreneurs isn’t about more content or productivity. It’s moving from idea to execution faster.
Copied to clipboard

		Opinions expressed by Entrepreneur contributors are their own.	
For the past few years, much of the conversation around artificial intelligence in business has centered on productivity: writing emails, creating marketing content, summarizing meetings and analyzing spreadsheets.
Those applications are useful, but they may represent only the first phase of AI adoption.
The more significant opportunity for entrepreneurs is emerging deeper inside business operations, where ideas have to be turned into products, decisions have to become processes and concepts have to be translated into instructions that other people can execute.
That shift matters because speed of execution has always been one of the biggest advantages available to a small business.
A large company may have more employees, larger budgets and established systems. A smaller company can often move from an idea to a decision much faster. AI has the potential to make that advantage even more pronounced.
The first wave of business AI largely focused on knowledge work.
An entrepreneur could ask an AI system to draft a proposal, summarize customer feedback or generate ideas for a marketing campaign. These applications reduced the amount of time people spent producing information.
The next opportunity is more specialized. Instead of simply generating information, AI is increasingly being incorporated into specific business workflows. 
Consider what happens when a company decides to launch a new physical product. The process doesn’t end when someone has a good idea. There may be market research, product development, design, costing, sourcing, manufacturing, quality control, packaging and distribution. Each stage creates information that must be communicated accurately to the next person or organization.
A bottleneck anywhere in that chain can delay the entire launch. This is where specialized AI applications can become considerably more valuable than general-purpose tools.
Entrepreneurs don’t necessarily need AI to automate an entire department. Sometimes, improving one bottleneck can have a disproportionate effect on the business.
Imagine a small apparel company preparing a new collection. The founder may have a clear vision for the product, but turning that vision into manufacturing-ready specifications requires detailed documentation. Traditionally, this might involve several rounds of work between designers, technical specialists and manufacturers.
A tech pack, for example, can bring together information such as measurements, materials, colors, construction details and other specifications required to produce a garment.
AI-assisted workflows are now being applied to this type of specialized documentation. For businesses exploring AI tech packs, the broader opportunity is not simply generating a document faster. It is reducing the friction between a product concept and the information required to execute that concept.
That distinction is important. The value of AI isn’t necessarily the document itself. The value is what happens to the business when a previously slow step becomes faster and more repeatable.
Large organizations often have the resources to absorb inefficiencies.
A multinational company can maintain specialized teams for product development, research, documentation and operations. A small business may have one person performing several of those roles.
That creates an interesting opportunity for entrepreneurs. AI can give a small team access to capabilities that previously required additional people, outside specialists or significant amounts of manual work. 
This doesn’t mean a five-person company suddenly has the equivalent of a 500-person organization. It means the five-person company may be able to spend more of its limited human capacity on decisions that actually require human judgment.
That difference can be significant.
If an entrepreneur spends less time formatting documents, organizing information and performing repetitive research, more time becomes available for talking to customers, negotiating with suppliers, improving products and developing new opportunities.
There is, however, an important distinction entrepreneurs should keep in mind. AI can make a process faster without necessarily making the outcome correct. That is particularly important when AI is used in specialized business workflows.
A production specification still needs to be reviewed. A financial analysis still needs to be checked. A marketing strategy still needs to be tested against actual customer behavior.
AI can accelerate the creation of an initial output, but businesses still need people who understand the underlying process.
In many cases, this may actually increase the value of experienced employees. Instead of spending their time producing every first draft manually, specialists can spend more time reviewing outputs, identifying problems and making higher-level decisions.
The role changes from producing everything from scratch to directing and validating the process.
There is another implication that deserves attention: experimentation.
Entrepreneurship has always involved uncertainty. A company rarely knows with complete confidence whether a new product, service or market will succeed.
The traditional response has often been to conduct extensive research before committing resources. AI can potentially make early-stage experimentation cheaper and faster.
An entrepreneur can use AI to explore product concepts, analyze publicly available information, organize customer feedback, develop preliminary documentation and identify potential issues before investing heavily in production.
This doesn’t eliminate the need for traditional research or validation. It can, however, allow businesses to reach the validation stage with less time and fewer resources.
That changes the question from: “Can we afford to develop this idea?” to “Can we test this idea before making a large investment?”
For a startup or small business, that can be an important distinction.
AI tools are becoming increasingly accessible. That means simply having access to AI may not remain a meaningful competitive advantage for very long. 
If competitors can use similar models, similar software and similar automation tools, the differentiation has to come from somewhere else.
One possibility is execution.
A company that has integrated AI into its workflows may be able to respond to customer requests faster, test more ideas, prepare products more efficiently or process information with fewer manual steps.
The technology itself may be similar. The workflow built around it is not.
This is similar to what happened with cloud software and digital commerce. Once the technology became widely available, competitive advantage shifted toward how effectively businesses implemented it.
AI may follow a similar path.
One mistake businesses can make is starting with the technology rather than the problem.
An entrepreneur hears about a new AI tool and immediately asks, “How can we use this?” A better question may be: “Where are we losing the most time?”
The answer could be surprisingly ordinary. Perhaps employees spend hours preparing repetitive reports. Perhaps salespeople manually organize information before every client call. Perhaps product teams spend too much time converting concepts into technical documentation. Perhaps customer service teams repeatedly answer the same questions.
Those are the areas worth investigating. A simple framework is to look for processes that are:
These characteristics can indicate that an AI-assisted workflow may be worth testing.
Businesses also don’t need to transform everything at once. A more practical approach is to select one workflow and establish a baseline.
Then introduce an AI-assisted process and measure the same variables.
If the workflow takes eight hours and can reliably be completed in four hours with appropriate human review, the business has something tangible to evaluate.
If quality decreases or the review process takes longer than expected, the experiment has produced useful information as well.
This is a more disciplined approach than adopting AI simply because competitors are talking about it.
Some of the most valuable AI applications may not be the ones customers see.
A customer may never know that AI helped prepare a technical specification, organize research or streamline an internal workflow. That’s perfectly fine.
Businesses don’t need AI to be visible to customers for it to create value. In fact, some of the strongest applications may operate quietly in the background, helping employees complete complex processes more efficiently.
For entrepreneurs, this represents a broader opportunity. The goal isn’t to add AI to every part of the company. It is to identify the points where better information, faster execution or reduced manual effort can improve the economics of the business.
The companies that approach AI this way may discover that the biggest opportunity isn’t generating more content or adding another chatbot. It is shortening the distance between an idea and execution. And for an entrepreneur, that distance has always mattered.
For the past few years, much of the conversation around artificial intelligence in business has centered on productivity: writing emails, creating marketing content, summarizing meetings and analyzing spreadsheets.
Those applications are useful, but they may represent only the first phase of AI adoption.
The more significant opportunity for entrepreneurs is emerging deeper inside business operations, where ideas have to be turned into products, decisions have to become processes and concepts have to be translated into instructions that other people can execute.

We'll be in your inbox every morning Monday-Saturday with all the day’s top business news, inspiring stories, best advice and exclusive reporting from Entrepreneur.

38,2026-10-14,"ReadWrite's editorial staff consists of journalists, editors, and experts across iGaming & Technology. Over 20 years of combined experience and the latest in technology, AI,…
Pennsylvania businesses and nonprofits have reached the deadline to remove skill-game terminals after the state Supreme Court classified the machines as slot machines under state law and lawmakers failed to agree on a regulatory system.
The court’s June ruling said the games are unlawful unless licensed. It paused enforcement for 120 days to give lawmakers time to act, but businesses were required to remove the machines by Tuesday, October 13; enforcement was set to begin Wednesday, October 14, according to CDC Gaming.
The Supreme Court declined requests to extend the deadline, CDC Gaming reported. With the legislative window closed, an estimated 70,000 terminals across Pennsylvania were set to become officially illegal after October 13.
Law-enforcement agencies have said they will crack down on businesses hosting the machines beyond the deadline. The Pennsylvania State Police warned that terminals still operating on October 14 could be seized immediately and that business owners may face criminal prosecution.
The deadline follows roughly a decade in which skill-game terminals operated without a comprehensive statewide regulatory system. The 120-day stay offered lawmakers time to establish one, but they did not reach an agreement before the court’s deadline. ReadWrite has previously reported on the Supreme Court’s classification of skill games as slot machines and the consequences for operators.
Pace-O-Matic, which develops skill-game software and terminals, said it is complying with the law and has urged operators and locations to make sure the games are no longer available to play. The company also warned that lost machine revenue would affect small businesses and organizations including American Legions, VFWs, volunteer fire companies and emergency-service groups.
Joe Rocca, owner of Pittsburgh-based Rite Touch Distribution, said removing the terminals threatens his business. He expects to have 1,000 machines left after removals are complete; he said almost all of his company’s business involved leasing terminals in Pennsylvania or selling them to distributors who leased them to local businesses and nonprofits.
Penn Entertainment welcomed the end of what it characterized as an illegal gaming industry, citing concerns about responsible-gaming practices, consumer protections and the absence of tax revenue for the state. Casinos and the American Gaming Association had argued that the unregulated machines sidestepped legal and regulatory requirements.
Supporters of skill games, by contrast, say the terminals provide an important source of additional income for small businesses, bars, taverns and nonprofits. Rocca supported regulation but objected to machines being placed in settings such as hair salons and mini-casinos.
The Independent Fiscal Office estimates that each terminal can generate $47,000 a year, underlining the potential scale of the revenue businesses and community groups stand to lose. Some may turn to pull-tab machines or seek to become Pennsylvania Lottery retailers. Lottery spokeswoman Ewa Swope said the lottery has more than 9,900 retailers statewide and expects interest from players and businesses as skill games leave the market.
Two stalled measures in Harrisburg, House Bill 2213 and Senate Bill 1079, would set a $500 monthly fee for each terminal and cap the statewide total at 50,000. Their proposed framework was estimated to generate $300 million in annual state tax revenue.
A separate proposal in Democratic Gov. Josh Shapiro’s budget would have allowed licensed establishments to host up to five machines, taxed gross revenue at 52% and raised an estimated $487 million in fiscal year 2026-2027, according to the Independent Fiscal Office. That plan also failed to secure agreement. The proposals remain a possible legislative route, but no replacement regulatory system was in place when enforcement began; lawmakers’ proposed skill-game rules have not resolved the immediate operating question for businesses with machines still on site.
The ReadWrite Editorial policy involves closely monitoring the gambling and blockchain industries for major developments, new product and brand launches, game releases and other newsworthy events. Editors assign relevant stories to in-house staff writers with expertise in each particular topic area. Before publication, articles go through a rigorous round of editing for accuracy, clarity, and to ensure adherence to ReadWrite's style guidelines.

Samsung Android Tablets
The largest tablet on the market just got its annual update, and it's looking impressive! The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra features an updated chipset with enhanced cooling, an upgraded connectivity suite, and the latest Android OS packed with AI features.
The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is an ultra-slim metal tablet featuring a massive 14.6-inch AMOLED display with an 1848 x 2960 resolution, a 120Hz refresh rate, and HDR10+ support. It even comes bundled with an S Pen stylus in the box.
Furthermore, the Tab S12 Ultra remains one of the few tablets on the market with an IP68 rating for dust and water resistance.
At the heart of the Tab S12 Ultra is the Dimensity 9500, which outperforms the previous Dimensity 9400+ with a 19% faster CPU, 17% faster GPU, and a whopping 61% boost in NPU performance.
Camera-wise, the tablet features a dual setup on the back (a 13MP wide and an 8MP ultrawide) alongside a 12MP ultrawide front camera for video calls and selfies.
Powering the device is a massive 11,600mAh battery supporting 45W wired charging.
The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra is pitched as a versatile productivity powerhouse thanks to Samsung DeX, the optional Pro Keyboard, and One UI 9 built on Android 17.
The Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra looks like an excellent tablet, but Samsung has once again kept the display notch for the front camera. While it is relatively small, it remains an unfortunate eyesore on an otherwise sleek screen.
Inside the Galaxy Tab S12 Ultra's slim white packaging, you'll find the tablet, a 3A USB-C cable, and the hexagonal S Pen.
Although the tablet supports 45W wired charging, it does not include a power adapter in the box.
Another stone-age tablet generation with no removable battery or headphone jack.  
Worse audio, battery life is litte bad downgrade, display still ugly notch With bigger bezel, Slow charging, ONLY new CPU, but hight cost.
If it had pass-through charging, it would work perfectly with the DeX feature; the fact that they didn't even include it in a product at this price point suggests they're really keen on generating more electronic waste.

Microsoft is actively asking Xbox users with multiple consoles how they'd react if big franchises went fully console exclusive. Is this a hint at things to come?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

It's no secret that Microsoft's gaming division has struggled to keep pace with PlayStation and Nintendo for active console users, and this Xbox survey suggests the firm might be considering the nuclear option to fix things.
Yesterday, a new survey appeared for a subset of Xbox users on the Insider Program, presumably among those who Microsoft tracks as having multiple devices. A user sent us the below survey screens, which Windows Central has verified as real.
Microsoft's problematic Xbox multiplatform strategy has left many users who own multiple devices with fewer reasons to actually turn their consoles on. In that universe, it reduces Microsoft's chances to earn users in it's own ecosystem considerably, impacting both first party revenue and third party port viability. Microsoft sells mountains of games on PlayStation, but it has to pay a 30% cut for the privilege. Many PlayStation users already own an Xbox too, so Microsoft is potentially handing away potential users for no real reason.
These new surveys suggest Microsoft may be considering some drastic moves to change the situation.
View Original
View Original
The survey asks a variety of questions of users across multiple ecosystems, asking how likely they would be to invest in the Xbox platform if various games went timed, or even fully console exclusive.
Microsoft asks if users would switch to Xbox on a per game basis, including pitches of exclusivity for games like Fallout, The Elder Scrolls, DOOM, Hideo Kojima's PHYSINT, Diablo, and even Minecraft itself. The survey even stresses that existing titles wouldn't be removed from other platforms, but would no longer receive new content.
There's even a nuclear option question, asking users how they'd change their habits if every single Xbox game (except Call of Duty, likely owing to acquisition requirements) went fully console exclusive.
It asks users to consider the hypothetical scenario, offering multiple choice responses below it.
""Imagine it was announced that all future title releases from every XBOX-developed gaming franchise except Call of Duty will be console-exclusive to XBOX,"" the final survey question reads. ""For Minecraft only, existing titles would no longer be available for purchase on PlayStation or Nintendo consoles, and would not receive any new content updates. However, there is no impact to any of the existing titles or content you already own. Nothing changes on PC in this announcement."" 
It asks users if they would move to an existing Xbox console they already own, buy a new one, switch to PC, or skip it entirely in this scenario.
 



In the near term, even if Microsoft did create massive desire for its platform and was somehow able to make even GTA6 magically exclusive, the RAMpocalypse has driven hardware sales to historic lows. Even if there was Xbox stock available globally, they've become prohibitively expensive for many. But, that situation (hopefully) won't last forever.
If games like Fallout 5, The Elder Scrolls 6, and Diablo 5 were Xbox Helix exclusive, it would undoubtedly drive masses of sales for Xbox hardware. But would the stock even be available? Microsoft has historically struggled with global distribution even before the component crisis. As much as I think Xbox desperately needs exclusive content, the risks are undeniable on paper.
But sacrificing the entire platform is just as risky potentially. PlayStation itself just massively clawed back from Steam and Windows PC gaming too.
There's little point in maintaining Xbox as an entity if it was to become a simple publisher. Microsoft is more of a hardware company than it has ever been arguably, powered by data centers and increasingly home grown silicon, like its new Maia chips which has driven the stock to new highs recently. Microsoft has already sacrificed mobile as a platform endpoint, greatly limiting its ability to distribute and train its AI platforms. I can't imagine they'll seek to make the same mistake with Xbox.
Surveys are just surveys, and it all might amount to nothing. It could also be a hint around Xbox's thought processes. Perhaps there's math that exists that proves that the juice just isn't worth the squeeze. Halo Campaign Evolved on PlayStation likely did irreparable brand damage to Xbox as a hardware entity, even though the game itself wasn't spectacularly well-received.
The future of Xbox could end up being more interesting than expected.
