42,2026-10-14,"I think it’s sometimes easy for geeks to forget that we live in a tech bubble with our fellow geeks. Many of us here have been using smart home technology for more than a decade now, some even for many decades.
I’ve seen a lot of bemused comments on the Apple Home Collection, with people asking why anyone would want to pay significantly higher prices for devices which don’t seem to offer any added benefit. But I think this is absolutely a bubble effect …
In the very early days, smart home technology involved a great deal of soldering and programming. The tech gradually grew more accessible and fully wireless devices controlled by smartphones was the first major step change. All the same, configuring new devices was far from trivial, initially requiring a whole bunch of companion apps for products made by different manufacturers.
Apple’s attempt to change all this was HomeKit. The idea was that everything could be configured and controlled via a single app, with the HomeKit label guaranteeing compatibility. It also brought voice control to devices which previously required us to use an app.
The reality was still rather different from the vision, however. Some HomeKit devices still needed to be initially configured using a third-party app before they would appear in Apple’s Home app. Some of the setup processes made me feel like an unpaid network engineer.
Additionally, relatively few manufacturers bothered to make HomeKit-compatible products. Both the Amazon Alexa and Google Home ecosystems were far more commercially successful.
The launch of the Matter and Thread standards helped significantly. It meant manufacturers only had to support these new industry standards in order to ensure compatibility with each of the three big smart home ecosystems.
Matter also enabled a simpler setup process, again helping to make the technology more accessible to the mass market. Once more, however, the practical impact was limited.
Many techies have been wondering what all the fuss is about with the Apple Home Collection. There are no new product categories, and there are equivalent but lower-cost options for each of the new devices.
I think the Apple home will not be as good as you seem think. There are better cameras that are just as secure and as easy to set up for a fraction of the cost. For example for the cost of one indoor camera I could buy 5 blink cameras, or even ring (which I know isn't as secure but a popular choice) when in the sales can be gotten for a few quid.  
If I were to add cameras to my house, say 2 or 3 indoors and the same for the outside, it would cost about £1750. Way too much of a cost. Plus if the same people are incapable of adding a camera via an app that they need apple cameras to just pair like earphones, who is going to install the cameras for them? That's yet more cost. 
But for non-geeks, the setup process really is completely different. Essentially, Apple has made configuring one of these devices as simple as pairing a new set of AirPods. Just like AirPods, you need only bring one of the new devices close to your iPhone, with CNET’s Ajay Kumar commenting on just how simple it appears to be.
I watched an Apple representative instantly find, pair and register a new smart home device on both her phone and the HomeView as she carried on a casual conversation with me, not missing a beat. Everything was managed through the Apple Home app. It seemed to work like pairing new AirPods or a HomePod. I didn’t have time to dig into the process before being shepherded to the next demo, but what I saw was one of the most compelling things I’ve seen in the smart home world: an easy, intuitive setup.
We shouldn’t underestimate just what a big difference this will make to normal people. I have definitely had non-tech friends be impressed by my smart home, but none of them rushed out to emulate it. I really do think this is going to represent the next step in mass market adoption.
If you’re a normie who likes the idea of a smart home but has been put off by the apparent complexity, I think we may now have reached the point where many are willing to make the leap.
That’s my view; how about yours? Please share your thoughts in the comments.

For generations, a law firm's most valuable asset walked out the door every night.
It was the attorney who remembered the clause that saved a deal years ago. The trial lawyer who knew which arguments worked on a judge and which ones didn't. The partner who told an associate, ""We've seen this before.""
Now law firms are racing to turn that expertise into software.
After spending the past two years adopting tools from OpenAI, Harvey, Microsoft, Legora, and others, large law firms are building systems around something more valuable: what only they know. The bet is that artificial intelligence steeped in their deal histories, trial strategies, and client preferences will be more useful than whatever the firm across the street can buy.
Law firms are spending big to bottle their secret sauce. Earlier this year, Kirkland & Ellis agreed to pay Palantir $500 million to build technology for one of the firm's core businesses: advising private equity clients on raising money. Latham & Watkins bought its own Nvidia servers to start customizing large language models.
At Morgan & Morgan, America's largest personal injury law firm, lawyers have begun using an in-house artificial intelligence model trained to recognize patterns in their cases. A younger lawyer might miss that a patient showing signs of a pinched nerve near the spine could later require surgery. The firm says its software can flag that possibility.
""Our best and experienced people will spot it almost immediately,"" said Yath Ithayakumar, Morgan & Morgan's chief transformation officer. ""But if you're a first-year attorney, this is your first job, maybe that's not as obvious to you. Technology makes a huge difference.""
This story is part of a series called The AI Data Grab, an inside look at how tech giants are increasingly turning to workers, users, and the physical world to get data to train AI. Read more stories in this series here.
That is the promise behind Morgan & Morgan's push to build its own technology. The firm, which has said it will spend $1 billion on the effort over the next decade, isn't trying to compete with OpenAI. It wants to capture what its best lawyers already know.
Many law firms already use the same high-tech tools. Harvey says it has users at 80% of the country's 100 largest law firms by revenue. Legora says it covers a majority of the same firms.
When everyone can buy the same systems, the edge has to come from what those systems know, said Mary O'Carroll, chief executive of the LegalEng Consulting Group, a boutique legal consulting firm based in San Francisco.
If a firm has ""done every private equity deal for all of time and has a corner on the market,"" the prize is being able to use that history so lawyers can say, in effect, ""I have the knowledge of 50,000 deals in my back pocket,"" she said.
That helps explain why Morgan & Morgan is going so far as to build its own technology. The firm sits on decades of case files showing how its attorneys have handled injuries, discovery fights, settlements, and trials. Its history is not just a record of work done. It is the raw material these systems need to be useful.
""We have more data than anybody else,"" Ithayakumar said. ""We have to be good stewards of it.""
Responsible use starts with control. Morgan & Morgan has to be careful about who gets access to its data, what they're allowed to do with it, and whether outside vendors can retain or learn from it, Ithayakumar said. And before the firm can use its data to spot patterns across cases, it has to strip away any details that could identify the people behind them.
That need for control has opened a new lane for tech companies.
Want to discuss this story with someone?

                                  Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                
Harvey wants to do for law firms what Palantir is doing for Kirkland & Ellis. This year, Harvey took an open-weight model and trained it further for legal work. Eventually, it wants to help firms do something similar with their own matters.
LexisNexis and Thomson Reuters, owner of Westlaw, see a similar opening. Both built massive businesses by collecting cases, statutes, and regulations, then layering them with their own editorial analysis. Next, they pushed into the software lawyers use to draft, research, and review documents.
Now they are moving closer to the models themselves. Thomson Reuters is offering customers access to Thomson, a model trained on 150 years of its own content. LexisNexis says it's aiming for a similar future: models grounded in both its legal corpus and a firm's own data.
Law firms asked for it, said Sean Fitzpatrick, chief executive of global legal at LexisNexis. ""All of the models can do things in a similar way,"" he said. What sets them apart ""is the content and the context that those models have.""
For all the money flowing into these custom systems, it's too soon to tell whether the winners will be the firms with the most data or the software companies that help them use it.
What everyone seems to agree on is the direction of travel. The next fight in legal technology is not over who has the smartest software. It's over who gets to own what the software knows.

44,2026-10-14,"While the iPhone 18 Pro is out, the entry-level model has been held back until early next year. That’s likely because the company wanted to maximize early production capacity and memory availability for the Pro model, alongside the iPhone Duo.
But even this far ahead, one leaker has suggested that supply-chain orders indicate Apple is optimistic about demand for the base model …
Apple broke with tradition this year in launching only the Pro models of the new iPhone lineup, that is, the iPhone 18 Pro and Pro Max. The base model iPhone 18 was nowhere to be seen.
There are likely a couple of reasons for this. First, the memory shortage meant that the company probably wanted to ensure there was enough supply for both the two iPhone 18 Pro models and the iPhone Duo.
Second with the iPhone Duo a more complex device, production demands are likely greater, even for a model likely to sell in relatively modest numbers thanks to the high price. For that reason, Apple may have wanted to give its manufacturing partners some breathing space by not launching the standard iPhone 18 at the same time.
Apple begins its production runs significantly ahead of iPhone launches in order to build up stock ready to meet initial demand. Production of the iPhone 18 is expected to begin at the end of November.
Leaker Fixed Focus Digital (FFD) says that inventory levels indicate that the company is gearing up for a significant stockpile of devices. This presumably refers to component orders placed within Apple’s supply chain.
The standard version of iPhone 18 is scheduled for production at the end of November, and judging by the inventory levels, the stockpile is quite substantial.
Order levels within Apple’s supply chain are not necessarily a reliable indicator of production volumes. Apple prefers to have multiple suppliers for as many components as possible in order to negotiate the best possible pricing as well as protect against any issues experienced by individual manufacturers. It’s possible that FFD has visibility into a single supplier that happens to be favored by Apple at this point in the run-up to production. 
For this reason, we shouldn’t read too much into this report. However, the leaker does have a decent track record, so let’s call it an interesting indication that Apple may be expecting strong demand for the upcoming iPhone 18.

By Sari Moon 


Updated 6 hours ago,
October 14, 2026


Adam Rubin, a co-owner of Art of Play in Brooklyn, says the mechanical bird Kelly Clarkson wanted isn’t ordinary stock. Its maker, Bliss Kolb, lives in a house he built himself in the woods, isn’t on social media, and takes years to build these machines. Rubin says only 2 exist.
That’s why Rubin calls the dispute “an insane misunderstanding.” There was a price tag on the bird, which the shop calls Molly, but he says they added it only because so many people kept asking.
A serious buyer, he says, would need the shop to reach out to Kolb about commissioning an automaton, since it isn’t sitting in a warehouse. He compares it to a piece of fine-art sculpture.
Clarkson said on X on Oct. 10 that a salesperson treated her as though she couldn’t afford what she’d come to buy, likening it to Julia Roberts’ character in “Pretty Woman.” Rubin says the store was packed that day and that she was inside for less than 5 minutes. “We treated her like a normal person, but apparently that didn’t live up to her expectations,” he said.
What came after is where Rubin draws the line. “Kelly Clarkson has put us in danger,” he said. He describes threatening calls and emails, review-bombing across every platform, and hate speech popping up on the shop’s point-of-sale iPad while staff rang up customers.
People shouting accusations outside drew neighbors who called the police, and Rubin says those neighbors were then doxxed. The shop turned off its phone.
“If an actual customer tries to get in touch with us right now, they will never be able to,” he said.

Who doesn't want to own a fantastic pair of headphones? Killer sound quality and features like active noise-canceling (ANC) are staples of over-ear, on-ear, and in-ear audio, and one brand that makes all three is Bose. Unfortunately, Bose cans and buds are some of the more expensive audio products on the market (the same goes for the brand's Bluetooth speakers). Unless you're buying a used or refurbished item, it'll be tough to find Bose headphones that cost less than $200.
Now, 'tough' doesn't translate to 'impossible', and as luck would have it, we actually found a sub-$200 pair of Bose earbuds that can be purchased brand-new. We're talking about the $180 Bose QuietComfort Earbuds, which hit the market in 2024. Believe it or not, we'd actually recommend these over the $300 QuietComfort Ultra Earbuds (2nd Gen), Bose's in-ear flagship. Why, you may ask? For starters, the QC Earbuds beat the QC Ultra for battery life; the former delivers up to 8.5 hours on a full charge, while the latter is limited to 6 hours.
We also like the fact that the QC Earbuds have a dedicated 'Off' setting for ANC. With the Bose QC Ultra, you need to create a custom sound profile to turn ANC all the way down manually; there's no simple on/off toggle. We should also mention that the QC Earbuds are available in seven colors, whereas the QC Ultra is limited to five.
It's a little hard to get excited about calling a $180 pair of earbuds ""cheap,"" but Bose products often come at a premium price. So, what exactly are you giving up by not going with the more expensive QC Ultra (2nd Gen)? One suite of features you'll miss out on is Bose Immersive Audio – a dashboard of spatial audio settings and customizations available for free in the Bose app. While the QC Earbuds do include Quiet and Aware presets (also in the Bose app), the multi-dimensional soundstage and head-tracking tech of Bose Immersive Audio isn't an option.
The Bose QC Ultra also supports Bose CustomTune, another in-app feature that calibrates audio and ANC in real time to deliver the best sound quality and noise isolation for your ear shape. Side-by-side, the QC Ultra offers improved noise-canceling, too, but probably not enough difference in attenuation to warrant spending another $120. Oddly enough, there was also a second generation of the Bose QC Earbuds, but they're not available to purchase brand-new (we're not exactly sure why).
As for actual over-ear and on-ear Bose headphones, the cheapest set of cans you can currently get your hands on is the Bose QuietComfort Headphones (1st Gen), usually found for around the $250 mark."

A Chronicle Of Minor Things, from Indian filmmaker Tribeny Rai, picked up the APM Busan Award and the Sorfond Award at the close of this year’s Asian Project Market in Busan.

	The jury said the project stood out for its original story and narrative structure, and for the director’s ability to weave a social issue subtly into the story, while the project’s distinctive landscape and themes were also recognized for their potential to appeal to international audiences.

Casparina, from Indonesian filmmaker Reza Rahadian, received the CJ ENM Award for transforming the director’s most personal story into a family drama with universal emotional resonance.

Sign in to your designboom account.

the architect's former waterfront villa in saint-jean-cap-ferrat, france, is now on the market for €29.5 million, featuring a sail-inspired canopy, sweeping steel arches, and expansive views of the mediterranean.
  thomai tsimpou October 14, 2026 3 min        
[FEATURED]
 
Originally built in the 1950s and transformed between 1998 and 2002 under the direction of its former owner, architect Norman Foster, Villa La Voile occupies a waterfront hillside in Saint-Jean-Cap-Ferrat, France. The residence brings together sweeping steel arches, extensive glazing, and a retractable sail-like canopy, using its steep site to organize a series of interconnected living spaces around views of the Mediterranean. Now offered for sale, the 480-square-meter property provides a glimpse into Foster’s approach to residential architecture.
 
La Voile is arranged across seven levels, with reception rooms and terraces following the slope. Double-height spaces and a four-level atrium bring natural light into the interiors, while floor-to-ceiling windows frame views of the coastline. Suspended walkways connect the different levels, creating a sense of openness throughout the residence. Steel arches and glass facades define the architectural language of the villa, while a retractable canopy gives the house its maritime character. The canopy resembles a sail, a reference to the Mediterranean setting that also informs the name of the project, La Voile, which translates from French as ‘the sail’. 
 
 
 
Saint-Jean-Cap-Ferrat, between Nice and Monaco, is known for its waterfront villas and steep coastal landscape. La Voile takes advantage of this setting, with its different levels, terraces, and glazed facades opening the house to views of the Mediterranean. Originally built in the 1950s, the residence was extended and redesigned between 1998 and 2002. The renovation changed the layout and orientation of the house to make better use of the hillside and its waterfront position, transforming the existing building into the residence seen today. With its steel arches, retractable canopy, and multi-level interiors, La Voile reflects Foster’s interest in structure and light. Terraces at different levels extend the living spaces outdoors, offering changing views of the Mediterranean. A rooftop pool overlooks the coastline, while the landscaped grounds follow the hillside, giving the property a sense of privacy despite its open waterfront setting.
 
The two-story library and four-level atrium are among the defining features of the house. The atrium draws natural light into the interior, while the library creates a more intimate space within the otherwise open layout. A MudWork fresco by British land artist Richard Long brings an artistic element to the interiors, complementing the connection of the villa to the natural landscape. Alongside its main living spaces, the residence includes five bedrooms, a separate apartment, and garage facilities. 
aerial view of La Voile set between the rocky coastline and the landscaped hillsidecurved steel arches, stone walls, and glazed facades define the buildingterrace overlooking the Mediterraneanfloor-to-ceiling glazing frames sea views from the living areathe kitchen and dining area combine stainless steel finishes with expansive glazing overlooking the waterRichard Long’s MudWork fresco sits on the rear wall

The race for state insurance commissioner hinges on a pivotal question: can the private market help us adapt to climate disasters?
Insurance is a social contract. When you sign up with State Farm or Allstate for home insurance, you confirm your membership in a community — your money will go to fix someone else’s burned or flooded home, and their money will in turn support yours. We let insurance companies sell policies on a private market, and we elect or appoint government officials to oversee that market to protect the interests of homeowners.
That’s how it’s supposed to work, but that’s not how it panned out for Lynn McIntyre. 


When McIntyre’s mother died in 2022, she willed McIntyre her longtime house in the “Alphabets,” a charming neighborhood not far from the coast in western Los Angeles. McIntyre and her daughter had only lived there for a year when her insurance company, Liberty Mutual, withdrew her fire coverage. She couldn’t find another private company to cover the home’s fire risk, so she enrolled in the California FAIR Plan, a state-backed “insurer of last resort,” paying another $5,000 a year in addition to her regular home insurance from Liberty.
After the Palisades Fire raged through the Alphabets in early 2025, McIntyre’s small bungalow was still standing. But it was full of smoke and ash that had crept in through vents and windows, and her belongings were stained beyond recognition by smoke. When she filed a claim with the FAIR Plan, the plan adjusters told her she was out of luck. They would pay for a temporary apartment, but nothing else.
McIntyre, who had worked for years as a real estate broker, spent months battling with 10 different adjusters and dozens of FAIR Plan representatives. It didn’t work. The employees batted her away at every turn, hiring third-party adjusters failed to get her anywhere, and her attorney professed himself confounded by FAIR’s obstinance. Even as most of her neighbors cleared their debris and started building new fire-resilient homes, many of them much larger than their old properties, her old bungalow sat stained with smoke. 
It was only in late August, more than 18 months after the wildfire, that she was about to start repairing her home — not with insurance money, but with a nearly $200,000 loan from the federal government that she will someday have to pay back. To make matters worse, FAIR has been trying to cut off her apartment expenses, telling her that power and water have come back online in her area and that she needs to move back home — never mind that her house still has boarded-up windows and a scorched exterior. Meanwhile, Liberty Mutual informed her this summer that they would be canceling her non-fire coverage.
“The next time a fire comes through, I’m just going to light it on fire, because I’m not going to go through this again,” McIntyre said wryly as we stood in her doorway, construction machines rattling at the other houses around us. 
Ensuring that Californians like McIntyre have access to affordable insurance falls to an elected official that many Californians have likely never heard of: the insurance commissioner. The commissioner, one of eight elected state officials in California, regulates the companies that provide fire, home, and auto coverage in the Golden State. 
Over the last eight years, the incumbent insurance commissioner presided over what parties on all sides have called a “disaster,” a “crisis,” and a “collapse” in the state’s home insurance market. Now, as the current commissioner terms out, two Democratic candidates — Ben Allen and Jane Kim — have emerged claiming they can fix it. These candidates embody a debate in the Democratic Party about the best way to adapt to climate change. Allen, an established legislator representing Pacific Palisades, wants to rebuild the market, and Kim, a left-wing organizer from San Francisco, wants to ditch the idea of a private market altogether.
California State Senator Ben Allen, left, and insurance commissioner candidate​ Jane Kim each address the crowd during the California Democratic Party State Convention at the Moscone Center in San Francisco in February 2026. Christina House / Los Angeles Times via Getty Images​
Whoever wins in November will inherit an insurance system battered by a decade of devastating wildfires, insurers pulling out of the state, and other upheaval. Climate change and decades of poor forest management have turned California’s woodlands into tinderboxes, and the problem will only get worse as the planet continues to warm. The current crisis in the insurance market began with a series of unprecedented wildfires between 2015 and 2020. These fires scorched around 4 million acres and destroyed 35,000 homes, many in areas that had never been known to burn. Insurance companies like State Farm and Farmers had to pay out billions of dollars in claims, enough to negate the profits they had made from writing fire insurance policies over the previous two decades. 
The companies believed they were charging Californians far too little, but the price of insurance isn’t elastic like the price of gasoline or paper towels. Insurers have to seek permission from the state insurance commissioner to raise their prices. California was strict about those price hikes, and it blocked insurers’ attempts to pass their post-fire bill onto the state’s homeowners. The insurance industry’s response was to ditch California. They dropped tens of thousands of customers across the state, and companies like State Farm announced they would withdraw from the state altogether and cease writing any new policies. 
“There’s something broken that so many of us are facing the same issues,” McIntyre said, “and they just think they can just roughshod right on over all of us.” 
The lack of available insurance is starting to hurt the state’s economy: Around 1 in 7 California realtors said they had a home sale fall through in 2024 due to insurance issues, the state’s FAIR plan ran out of claims money last year, and average premiums could rise by 16 percent this year. The trend is now spreading out of the most flammable zones, and many homeowners even in low-risk areas are struggling to get insurance.
The outcome of the November election could determine the trajectory of insurance bills for millions of Californians and decide the fate of fire-prone areas around the state. The election will also serve as a bellwether for how voters in the United States want to approach the expensive and difficult process of adapting to a warming world, and whoever wins will have to turn that mandate into a policy that can offer short-term relief. This would be a daunting job under the best of times, and these are not the best of times. 
The current insurance crisis is new, but its origins are very old. Over the last century, California has built millions of homes in the “wildland-urban interface,” the flammable zone outside the state’s major cities. Developers rushed out into the mountains around Lake Tahoe and the desert hills east of San Diego and built without any thought for fire mitigation, throwing up homes with wooden sidings and flammable fences on crowded lots. These are the areas that are burning up in the state’s recent wildfires, and the areas that insurers are dropping en masse.
This history of unwise development has created a bizarre situation that the scholar Carolyn Kousky has dubbed “a tale of two markets.” Prices ticked up in places that weren’t risky, like wet San Francisco and low-lying Fresno, but the market went haywire in risky areas like Malibu and the High Sierras. Nearly half of all households in high-fire-risk areas lost their insurance between 2018 and 2024. The California FAIR Plan, which was intended as a fringe supplement, became the only option for thousands of homeowners. Since 2018, it has swallowed up a huge share of policies in risky areas, growing from less than 1 to 5 percent of the market today. In some places, it reached 80 percent of market share.
In theory, California voters have some influence over their haywire insurance market. Every state has an insurance commissioner, but California is one of around a dozen states where that is an elected position. The fact that it’s elected at all is a testament to the strength of the state’s pro-consumer slant — in 1988, voters who were incensed about high car insurance prices passed a measure that turned it from an appointed position into an elected one. 
In practice, this is a job no one should want. The commissioner has the power to approve or deny rate increases, and to police the industry’s compliance with state law. They also oversee the FAIR Plan, the state-backed insurer, which offers limited and expensive coverage to people like McIntyre who can’t get it on the private market. But the commissioner can’t do much else to make private insurers offer coverage or to reduce overall fire risk in wildland areas.
“The insurance commissioner has a lot of power and authority himself or herself that they should use, but the insurance commissioner alone cannot address these challenges,” said Dave Jones, who served as commissioner between 2011 and 2019.
The outgoing commissioner, Ricardo Lara, has been in office for almost the entirety of the state’s fire-induced market collapse. Lara vacillated between cozying up with the insurers or clamping down on them and running the risk that they might flee the state. First he pledged to reject all insurance industry campaign money, then accepted some of that money, then appeared to cover for it by stalling on rate increases, which led insurers like State Farm to drop more policies. 
Lara’s attempt at resolving the crisis was to strike a deal with the companies. He allowed them to pass on the cost of their reinsurance (or insurance for insurance companies) to customers, and he also allowed them to use forward-looking catastrophe models to predict future risk, something every other state had already allowed. That would translate to higher rates, and in return the insurers had to commit to writing more policies in the risky fire areas they wanted to abandon. But the deal turned out to be full of loopholes — insurers can meet their mandate without returning to the riskiest areas, and the state FAIR Plan has kept absorbing more market share after the Los Angeles fires in 2025. Lara said in an interview with The New York Times earlier this year that the industry had “bullied” him. (His office declined interview requests, and representatives for the FAIR Plan did not respond to requests for comment.)
Despite the entrenched nature of the state’s insurance problems, a dozen candidates piled into the election to replace Lara. This led to a crowded primary between four Democrats, five Republicans, and two independents; none of the candidates had the funding to run a full statewide campaign, and there was limited public polling. The results were surprising even to the candidates. The top two candidates who advanced to the November runoff were Democrats. In first place with around 27 percent was Kim, a former San Francisco supervisor and Bernie Sanders ally who had pitched a government-run single-payer program for disaster insurance. In second place was Allen, the state senator who represents the Palisades and is one of the legislature’s foremost experts on insurance. 
Allen and Kim agree on a lot. They both believe in simplifying typical insurance policies and forcing companies to be transparent about their spending and investment in fossil fuels. They both believe in beefing up the FAIR Plan so it would cover smoke and other damages, which would have saved McIntyre from months of confusion and delay. They have both vowed to crack down on insurers who delay and nitpick claim payments after disasters. And they have both rejected campaign contributions from the insurance industry.
Where they differ is on the question of how to get people off the FAIR Plan and stabilize the market — in other words, on the fundamental question of the state’s insurance system. Allen believes that if the state allows insurers to raise rates on risky homes, invests to harden homes in vulnerable areas, and takes a data-driven approach to measuring wildfire risk, it can entice insurers back and restore a healthy market. Kim would prefer to discard the market altogether. She proposes creating a state-run public insurance program for all homes and all disasters, replacing private companies and the FAIR Plan. Rather than doling out profits to investors, this program would spend its excess revenue to harden old homes against wildfire. Private insurance companies could still write coverage for burglary and other perils, but the state government would collect all wildfire insurance and pay out all claims, a model based on similar programs in New Zealand and France. 
The philosophical debate in the election is much deeper than a question of state policies or affordability — it’s about whether the private market can protect us against climate change at all.
Allen believes it can. He decided to run for commissioner after the Palisades Fire scorched a good portion of his district, plunging many of his close friends and acquaintances into a hell of displacement and insurance litigation. (Indeed, many of the Palisades homeowners I met knew him on a first-name basis, including McIntyre, who is supporting him in the election.) Even before that, he had already spent several years leading state legislative committees on insurance, energy, and wildfire risk, making him arguably the most qualified person in the state for the commissioner job.
“I think we need someone in this office who really understands climate science, who understands the markets, who understands the legislature,” he said. The Palisades Fire, he said, gave him a “level of understanding from going through the experience that is just different” — he heard about friends trying to itemize vaporized dining room furniture and saw phosphate pollution harming marine mammals in the water off his home in Santa Monica.
Rather than use the insurance commissioner post to push for a major statutory change like Kim’s public fund, Allen speaks about the commissioner as having a poder de convocatoria, a Spanish phrase that translates to “convening power.” He wants to bring together the state agencies and the insurance industry to have a real conversation about reducing fire risk and cajole the state’s market back into existence. He has just passed a bill that offers low-interest loans to homeowners who want to fire-harden their homes. He wants to pursue a sort of grand bargain with the industry, speeding up rate increase decisions while also disciplining the insurers who delay and deny claims.
Kim’s audacious proposal may be gaining more traction than Allen’s proposed compromise. She outperformed Allen in the primary in almost every county, including in his home county of Los Angeles, and given her progressive background, she did fairly well in the rural and red parts of the state. She admits her strong performance is partially “a mystery to me,” but attributes it to the fact that many voters are fed up with the insurance industry.
“There’s this sense of immense unfairness, and once we articulate the problem, and then on top of that, actually propose big policy ideas, I think that that resonates,” she told me. We were speaking at a home in San Francisco’s Presidio Heights, where Kim was attending a fundraiser for the radical climate activism group Climate Defiance. 
Some experts are skeptical of a government takeover in the style Kim is proposing. Jones, the former insurance commissioner, said the program has two key flaws. First, Kim’s promise to keep insurance affordable could mean keeping prices too low on mansions in fire-prone areas, where homeowners may currently pay premiums that are inadequate to their risk. Second, insurers in California are required to have tens of billions of dollars in extra cash to pay during big loss events, and Kim’s new fund would have to find that money in a state whose public budget is already strained.
“Not only does she not come clean as to where the money’s going to come from, if her thought is that it’s going to come from the general fund of the state of California, good luck,” said Jones. He pointed out that the model Kim cites in New Zealand is far smaller than California’s, has low coverage limits, and doesn’t cover fires. (Allen described the proposal as “red meat, sugar rush stuff,” and said taxpayers would end up footing the bill if the public plan lost money.)
Kim allows that the legislature might not jump for her public insurance fund, but she has a compromise in her back pocket — one not dissimilar from Allen’s own agenda. Instead of creating a single-payer insurance program that would use profits to fund fire resilience, the commissioner could turn the FAIR Plan into such a program. The plan already pools money from all the state’s big insurers, and Kim could force them to pay an extra fee that would fund hardening for FAIR Plan homes. She returned to this theme when she delivered a stump speech to the activists in the Presidio Heights living room, saying that private companies would never act on their own to reduce the state’s fire risk.
“What the insurance industry does is that they price risk, but they don’t actually address it,” she said. If that’s the case, she argues, then the industry shouldn’t be the one charged with keeping Californians safe from climate disasters.
For all their other points of alignment, this question represents the major philosophical disagreement between Allen and Kim. Allen agrees that the industry has not yet acted to reduce California’s enormous fire risk, but he believes that it can fill that role — and he can be the one to make that happen.
“The insurance commissioner is uniquely positioned to bring the insurance to the table … and actually say to the insurance industry, ‘OK, guys, you’ve got to be a part of the solution here,’” Allen said.
The problem for both Kim and Allen is that reducing risk is very difficult.
Take the case of Tony Byers. He is a retired electric utility worker and a longtime resident of Placerville, an old mining town high in the forest-covered hills east of Sacramento. He bought insurance from AAA for years, but the company dropped him after a 2022 fire burned a nearby stretch of the Sierras. State Farm gave him a quote, but then rejected him because it claimed he didn’t have a fire hydrant near his house, even though he did. Byers ended up going with Lemonade, the online insurer, and even paid to have some flammable trees removed at the company’s request, but last year they told him they were leaving California. He ended up on the FAIR Plan, and he now pays $3,400 a year for fire insurance alone, triple what he used to pay. Byers lives at the end of a narrow mountain road, and if he wants to get back on a private insurance plan, he’d likely have to spend tens of thousands of dollars to retrofit his property, which is a lot more than he can afford on a retiree’s budget.
The only people who can opt out of this gauntlet of risk reduction are those who can pay for elite coverage. That was the case for Kevin Roberts, a retired golf course owner who lives in Huntington Palisades, a wealthy neighborhood on the edge of the Palisades Fire burn scar. Roberts likely couldn’t get fire coverage for his two-story Spanish colonial from bigger insurers like State Farm, but years ago a former fraternity brother told him about a company called Chubb that offered boutique insurance at high prices. The company now charges him more than $30,000 a year for his coverage on a “surplus lines” policy that isn’t subject to state regulations. It’s an eye-watering payment, but the company came through with a $2.65 million payout for smoke damage after the fire. Many of the state’s wealthiest homeowners are already on this unregulated market: the number of surplus lines policies in California has doubled over the last four years to around 1.3 million.
“You’ll pay more, but if you need them, they’ll deliver,” said Roberts.
Neither Byers nor Roberts expressed strong feelings about the insurance commissioner’s election, and indeed, both candidates will face tremendous barriers before they can address the issues that someone like Byers faces in Placerville. That’s because scaling California’s efforts to reduce more than a century of fire risk is one of the most daunting adaptation efforts faced by any place in the United States. The challenge is not just in finding the money to help homeowners who can’t do it themselves, but in figuring out how to reduce risk for millions of old and ill-built homes.
The day after Kim’s fundraiser, I met two employees from North Coast Opportunities, a poverty-fighting organization that serves Lake County, north of San Francisco. The organization is the first to receive money from a $117 million state grant program designed to retrofit whole communities against wildfire. The organization chose to focus on a tight-packed community called Kelseyville, where hundreds of homes cluster on small lots against the side of a windy mountain ridge. As the employees from North Coast drove me through the area, it became clear just how hard it will be to achieve the scaled-up resilience that both Allen and Kim want.
Lake County has an urgent need for this. The mountainous area is among the state’s most vulnerable to wildfire — some 85 percent of its land area has burned since the turn of the century — and it is also one of its poorest counties. Insurers have abandoned the area en masse, forcing many people onto the FAIR Plan, or causing older homeowners to go without insurance altogether. 
The state program is the exact kind of intervention that insurers have long said will induce them to offer more coverage and stop raising rates. The nonprofit can give each participating home in Kelseyville about $70,000 to fund easy tweaks that will stop ignitions — such as removing trees from around homes, replacing vinyl siding with fiber cement, and deconstructing flammable wooden decks. The mitigated houses don’t look much different from the old ones, except for the emptier yards and fresh paint, but even these tiny fireproofing changes required painstaking work. North Coast has had to ask contractors to redo flawed retrofits and cajole local hardware stores into selling fireproof equipment. Many homeowners aren’t interested in participating at all.
“What creates an insurable community is for the whole community to have buy-in,” said Deanna Fernweh, the program manager. “It’s changing the mentality, because they’re like, ‘Well, we’ve always done it this way.’” She said she’s cautiously optimistic that companies will come back — insurers have been reaching out to learn more about her work, but they’ve also pushed for full neighborhoods to meet the strictest fire standards.
A pair of mitigated homes in Lake County, one of the first counties in California to participate in a state fire hardening grant program. The county paid to clear vegetation from around the base of the homes, which will reduce the potential for fire ignition. Jake Bittle / Grist
The project underscores the scale of the challenge the next commissioner will face in reducing fire risk. The project only touches around 300 of the 1,200 eligible homes in the neighborhood, and the grant typically isn’t even enough to do full mitigation on each home. The funding came from the federal government after years of delay. The best possible result is to bring insurers back to just a few streets, a fraction of the risk in Lake County alone. For millions of homeowners who don’t have access to grant funding, resilience is still out of reach.
“A home is only as strong as the weakest link,” said Steve Hawks, a longtime California fire official who is now the director of wildfire at the Insurance Institute for Business & Home Safety, which develops standards for fire hardening. “If you leave a weak link on the table, a fire of [significant] magnitude can exploit any vulnerability.”
Hawks describes the challenge as “daunting,” and that may be an understatement. Retrofitting a home for fire can cost several times as much as building a new fire-resilient home, and the bill for hardening the millions of homes in California’s fire zones would run to the double-digit billions at least. This money has to come from somewhere, and Allen and Kim both acknowledged that their biggest challenge is to find out where. 
This is the irony of the fact that both candidates are appealing to voters by talking about “affordability.” No matter which path one takes through the maze, one arrives at the same conclusion — to fix this crisis will take money, and much of that money will come from the people of California. As Allen says, “risk doesn’t disappear, it gets allocated.” The “affordability” challenge in this case is not to help voters keep up with inflation but to pay down a massive debt of risky development that has been accumulating for decades. The challenge is to remake a society that was not designed to be remade.
“I think we’re still at the beginning,” said Holly Sacks, the director of underwriting for Mercury, a California-based insurance company that has committed to remain in the state and to help incentivize risk mitigation. The company is selling in burned places like Paradise and Altadena, but only for hardened homes, and Sacks says it’s one of the only companies doing so. “We are out there, and we don’t hear about a lot of other carriers getting out there,” she said. “I think there’s still a ways to go … to really make the market more stable.”
The scale of this work is enormous, but the stakes are even larger. When we purchase insurance, we purchase not only the privilege of owning a home or driving a car, but also the privilege of belonging to a community in which all support all. Insurance, as Kim likes to say, is a prerequisite for participating in the economy. A healthy insurance market is also an indication that society can still hold together, that there is no home so vulnerable to the ravages of global warming that the rest of the community can’t afford to protect it.
Of all the things that climate change may take from us, this guarantee might be the most dear.

					By Jill Goldsmith 
Co-Business Editor

	With media M&A in the air, shareholders of both companies have approved Fox’s planned cash and stock acquisition of Roku at special meetings today. 

	The approvals were expected.

	The deal announced in June – which will turbocharge Fox’s streaming capabilities – calls for the company run by Lachlan Murdoch to acquire Roku for $160 a share through a combination of cash and Fox Class A stock, giving Roku an enterprise valuation of $22 billion. On closing, Fox shareholders will own about 73% of the merged business, with Roku shareholders taking the other 27%.

	Roku, founded in 2002, reaches around 100 million global streaming homes through its connected TV platform, and is in more than half of all U.S. broadband households. Fox operates its namesake broadcast network, a large local station portfolio, cable networks Fox News and Fox Business, subscription streamer Fox One, and a sports rights portfolio including the NFL, MLB, NASCAR, Big Ten and the FIFA World Cup.

	










			Watch on Deadline		






	Fox said at the announcement that the Roku purchase would “create a scaled next-generation media and technology company positioned at the intersection of two of the most important forces reshaping video consumption: the enduring primacy of live sports and news, and the continued rise of streaming.”

	Roku founder, chairman and CEO Anthony Wood will have an ongoing role at the combined company and will join the Fox board.

Fox Corp. President and Chief Operating Officer John Nallen told investors at a media conference last month that the deal is on track to close in the first half of next year despite a new request for information from regulators. He called the request by the U.S. Department of Justice “entirely expected.”

	It’s the latest transformative deal in a rapidly transforming media landscape that just saw Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery close their merger, creating the new Skydance. 

	In an SEC filing today, Fox noted nearly 100% of votes in favor of the deal. Roku’s tally will come in a filing Thursday.

52,2026-10-14,"It's four years since the start of the AI-fueled bull market, and Société Générale is urging investors to stick with all parts of it.
In a recent client note, the bank said that new legs of the trade are unfolding, which should continue to propel returns upward, even for the AI infrastructure and application trades that have already seen huge upside.
""After three years of massive infrastructure buildout, AI value creation is beginning to broaden downstream,"" Manish Kabra, SocGen's chief US equity strategist, wrote in the note. ""Rapidly evolving use cases and an expanding ecosystem of software, agents and physical AI applications are widening the opportunity set, from infrastructure providers to companies directly monetising AI products and, ultimately, those capturing productivity gains from adoption.""
There's perhaps no better illustration of Kabra's point than Meta's agentic AI model, Muse.
After worries that AI buildout stocks had run their course, the product has sparked new optimism that AI can indeed be monetized and that other firms are likely to keep spending to develop their own agents. The agentic AI boom also promises to boost productivity of firms across sectors of the economy.
To keep playing the AI theme, SocGen has released a three-part investing playbook. Here's what to know.
This is the AI infrastructure trade. Think chipmakers, data center stocks, and power companies. These stocks are the recipients of massive hyperscaler capex.

                Americans and Canadians have stopped traveling like one market. Whether a European destination runs North America from New York or has a team in Toronto, the ones that plan for each market separately stand to take the growth.            
Learn More
Europe's biggest tourism boards — from France and Britain to Ireland, Spain, Switzerland and Portugal — keep their own teams in Canada, Toronto, or Montreal. Many smaller ones can’t justify it, and run North America from one office or agency, often in New York. 
Whichever describes yours, the two markets on the other side of the ocean have been pulling apart, and your plan should reflect it.
Start with the Americans. They took close to 24 million trips to Europe in 2025, a record, and then eased off. Monthly growth slid from around 6% last summer to 2% by May, before departures fell 2.1% in June 2026 and 1.2% in July. We read that as travelers catching their breath after a record year.

Skift's analysis of U.S. and Canadian demand for Europe finds the two source markets moving in opposite directions, with implications for how European tourism boards staff and spend in North America. Americans took close to 24 million trips to Europe in 2025, a record, but growth cooled through the year and departures fell 2.1% in June 2026 and 1.2% in July. 55,2026-10-14,"TOPICS

                    on-prem
                

        Analyst predicts 80% of neoclouds will disappear as standalone businesses by 2030
    
AI services are unlikely to deliver enough revenue to cover the expected capital expenditure going into IT infrastructure between now and 2030, and it looks like neocloud operators will largely bow out or be bought out.
This is the conclusion from Gartner, which has added up the anticipated investments in AI datacenters over the next several years, estimated the revenue that can be expected from AI-driven applications over the same period, and found the numbers come up short.
Gartner's data comes from a report, ""Prepare for AI Market Consolidation: Capital Write-Downs Could Exceed $4 Trillion by 2032,"" which concludes that AI will not generate enough sales to profitably cover all of the planned AI capital spending before 2032 at the earliest. But forecasts typically focus on the end of the decade, so it ran the numbers for 2030 as well.
""In 2026, AI providers are expected to spend nearly $1 trillion on AI infrastructure, and are forecast to invest an additional $6 trillion between 2027 and 2030,"" said Gartner senior director analyst Will Sommer.
""However, our research indicates that enterprise and consumer AI revenue will be insufficient to support profitable returns on all planned AI capital expenditures during this period.""
Assuming a 12 percent return on invested capital (ROIC), which many companies are said to regard as the bar for acceptability, Gartner estimates AI datacenter operators would need to generate a total of $8.5 trillion in revenue to cover capital spending and financing costs while achieving that return.
Unfortunately, it forecasts that the total enterprise and consumer AI revenue that can be attributed to AI datacenters during this period will add up to no more than $3.6 trillion.
Returns below expectations could force operators to write down assets whose value they can no longer recover, particularly as newer GPUs displace older hardware.
The result will be a shake-out of the companies providing AI infrastructure services, and Gartner believes that neocloud operators – ""GPU-as-a-service"" providers specializing in AI compute – will be the hardest hit. It forecasts that 80 percent of them won't survive as independent companies beyond 2030.
""Not everyone is in trouble,"" Sommer told The Register. ""Some will outperform, some will underperform, and some will have to exit the market.""
Gartner expects the hyperscalers to weather the shake-out better because their businesses extend beyond AI services.
Last year, McKinsey & Company warned that neocloud companies need to move up the AI stack and diversify to avoid being commoditized.
There has been growing unease in the financial markets over the amount of capex being poured into AI investment, with some money lenders starting to get cold feet.
Last month, SoftBank Group found itself facing higher borrowing costs as the tech investment biz sought to drum up further funding for OpenAI.
Financial biz Jefferies issued a report this week saying that developers such as OpenAI and Anthropic are facing threats to their profitability from open source and Chinese models that undercut them on price.
Management consultant Bain & Company also published a report last month estimating that the AI industry needs to generate $6 trillion in annual revenue by 2031 to keep up funding for the infrastructure needed to meet anticipated demand. Bain also forecast a revenue shortfall unless the industry develops products and use cases not yet identified. 
Both the IMF and the Bank for International Settlements have voiced concerns about the AI bubble bursting and the effects rippling out across the global economy. ®

Oct 14 : The London Stock Exchange Group said on Wednesday it has partnered with Nvidia to help financial institutions process and analyse large volumes of historical market data faster and at scale using the US chipmaker's technology.
LSEG, which operates the London Stock Exchange and provides data and analytics, has been expanding its offerings to support diverse demand whilst retaining licenses and control as AI also affects how businesses trade and consume data.
Analysis showed that computing on Nvidia's specialised processors without creating copies of the dataset could help study every trade and price quote in seconds for insights, the company said. This would make research faster and closer to real-time as equity trading moves to 24-hour cycles, it added.
""This collaboration expands how customers can draw insight from LSEG's trusted, governed Tick History. Customers want to work with detailed historical data at speed,"" said Ron Lefferts, co-head of data & analytics at LSEG.
According to the company, its Tick History has about 110 trillion rows of data across 100 million instruments spanning 30 years and 580 exchanges, trading venues and contributors. 
LSEG is the largest customer of Reuters, which provides news for its Workspace news and data platform and other products. It plans next year to launch LSE 24, a night-time trading site for some exchange-traded products.

57,2026-10-14,"Today's Nice Price or No Dice Be-1 is one of a clutch of boutique cars Nissan built at its specialty Pike factory. Looking cute as a bug and now old enough to bring that cuteness to the U.S., we'll have to decide what such a cutie-pie should rightfully cost.
With the holidays coming up and Canadian Thanksgiving only this week, a familiar refrain will echo in households across the land: ""Save room for dessert!"" Pacing oneself and not getting caught with no room to spare when a big meal's final act arrives is an important skill for any gastronome. A similar discipline applies to buyers of certain cars, who know not to blow their entire wad on the purchase, but to negotiate a sale price that leaves enough behind for any unexpected maintenance and repair bills. 
That was the general consensus regarding the 2011 Mercedes-Benz E 63 AMG we considered on Tuesday. Offered at $23,900, the car appeared to be in excellent condition. The mileage and the model's history of engines eating themselves alive made that amount feel like too high an initial outlay for many of you, leaving little buffer for when something expensive might decide to let its freak flag fly. In the end, the judgment was 'No Dice' in a 66% loss.
Back in the 1970s, Nissan, then going by the assumed name ""Datsun"" here in the U.S., sold a stripped-down version of its B-210 compact as a price leader, calling the model the Honey Bee. As the cheapest of the cheap, few have survived to the present day. This 1987 Nissan Be-1 is also extremely rare, especially since Nissan built only 10,000, and getting one in the U.S. means privately importing it from its home market of Japan.
The Be-1 was Nissan's first Pike Factory specialty car. It was followed by the Figaro, Pao, and S-Cargo van. All of these specialty retro-style cars were based on Nissan's sub-compact March platform featuring FWD and fairly mundane mechanicals under the hood. According to its Nissan Heritage Collection page, the Be-1's name comes from ""Plan B-1,"" but it doesn't explain what that means.
The car's styling pulls from several classic European mini cars, but arguably the most obvious influence is Italy's Autobianchi A110, echoed in the Be-1's greenhouse and gently curving sides. Nissan says the Be-1's factory name—Pike—means ""edgy,"" but I think we can all agree ""adorable"" is a better descriptor for this particular model.
In every way, this is a very small car, though not Kei-car small, so it's a bit less terrifying to drive in a land of giant pickups and SUVs. The wheelbase is a relatively generous 90 inches, while the car overall stretches to just over eleven and a half feet long. Inside, there's room for four close friends, although it's tight enough that hopefully they've all recently showered. Extra room is afforded by appreciably thin doors and a basic, scooped-out dashboard.
Under the forward-opening hood lies the March's MA 10S inline four. That SOHC engine makes 51 horsepower, which on this car is routed through a simple three-speed automatic to the front wheels. And before you poo-poo that paltry output and dearth of gearbox cogs, remember the Be-1 tips the scales at a mere 1,500 pounds, so there's not much metal to move around. There's also no frippery like A/C or power steering to rob the engine of what little it has to give.
According to the seller, this Be-1 has a clean Washington State title, and its import paperwork has already been handled. It also has had recent maintenance, including a new timing belt and water pump. Other notable aspects include a suspension shift to coil-overs, which has lowered the car—but not too much—and a handsome set of Michelotti-designed wheels off of another Pike car, the Figaro.
Everything about the car looks to be in great condition. The ""Resale Red"" paint shines nicely and is accented by black trim and bumpers that don't seem to have gotten chalky with age. In the cabin, the seller touts a new Pioneer double-DIN stereo with Apple CarPlay and Bluetooth capability. It's mounted low ahead of the shifter, but at least it's usable in the U.S. 
Naturally, since this was originally intended as a Japanese home-market car, it's RHD and has gauges that read in metric rather than miles. That means the 87,359 on the odometer is in kilometers, which converts to around 54,000 American miles. The only noteworthy issue in the cabin is some peeling plastic on the dashboard behind the retro instrument cluster. That seems to be the only issue on the car overall, so while easily dismissed, it's a frustrating blemish considering it's right in the driver's line of sight.
So, this is an appreciably rare and arguably cute car that will stand out at any car meet, or just while driving down the street. It has another notable aspect: at $9,500, it's likely the cheapest of the small handful of Be-1s imported to the U.S. and currently for sale. Based on a quick look around, there seem to be only about three others on the market right now, and none of those are under ten grand.
Ah, but does that automatically make this one a bargain? Perhaps they're all just overvalued. I think it's time for you all to weigh in on that question. 
What's your take on this Be-1 and that $9,500 price tag? Does that feel fair given the car's exclusivity, clean, clear import paperwork and title, and the fact that it's so cheerful-looking you just want to hug it? Or for that much, is this not the be-all and end-all of bargains?
You decide!


Portland, Oregon, Craigslist, or go here if the ad disappears.
Help me out with Nice Price or No Dice. Hit me up at robemslie@gmail.com and send a fixed-price tip. Remember to include your commenter handle."
58,2026-10-14,"I jailbroke my Kindle a few months ago to use it for more than reading, like sideloading games and playing music. I figured kTerm would be enough to reach my self-hosted services and my Linux home server. But kTerm just opens a shell into a stripped-down BusyBox environment. It’s there to run the e-reader, not to act as a general Linux machine, and there’s no apt to pull in the tools I’d normally reach for on a server.
Running real Linux software on the Kindle meant waiting for someone else to cross-compile it first. I didn’t want just a terminal window into my home server, though. I wanted the Kindle itself to run Linux while still functioning as an e-reader. That sounded trickier, but a chroot made it happen.
E-ink monitors cost $500+, so I built one from a spare Kindle with just Python and a weekend
I used a chroot, which gives Debian its own root filesystem inside a single .ext3 image file. The Kindle doesn’t have to dual-boot into it, and I don't have to flash anything. A KUAL (Kindle Unified Application Launcher) extension mounts the image and chroots into it, while the Kindle’s own kernel keeps running underneath. The stock software stays untouched, so you don’t risk losing the e-reading experience. If I ever want Debian gone, I just delete the image file.
I found a tool on GitHub called kindle-linux-chroot that makes this straightforward. It builds ARMv7 images for Debian, Alpine, and Arch Linux ARM, and Void Linux should work too, even though it’s untested. It also includes a KUAL extension that launches the chroot, which saves me from stitching the mount commands together by hand. You do need a jailbroken Kindle with KUAL and kTerm first.
The project also offers a one-line script that pulls down a minimal Linux filesystem and packages it into a single file. I ran it on a Linux server at home. The script needs root access, and I had to install debootstrap and qemu-user-static first so the server could build an ARM image.
I copied that Debian image to the Kindle’s root storage folder and moved the Linux Chroot extension into KUAL’s extension folder. Then I opened KUAL on my Kindle, tapped Linux Chroot, and tapped Start Terminal. In a few seconds, I saw a plain bash prompt on the display. I updated everything, installed screenfetch, and ran it to display the hardware and software information.
Because this is a full Debian install, the apt-get command gives me tools the stock Kindle never had, like curl, git, wget, and htop. That’s the real case for running Linux on an e-reader, because I can install most of what I need from Debian’s ARM repos instead of waiting for a Kindle-specific port.
Typing long commands on the Kindle’s on-screen keyboard isn't fun. The first thing I did inside Debian was to run the /ssh command, which starts a lightweight SSH server on port 2222. Whenever I need it, I open the terminal on my laptop and connect over Wi-Fi, just like I do with other machines on my home network.
The project's docs list a default root password of “kindle,” so run passwd before you connect the Kindle to a network you don’t trust.
I also run Tailscale on my Kindle so I can SSH out to my home server, not just SSH into the e-reader itself. That turns it into a pocketable terminal I can use to reach my home network from anywhere, without needing a laptop. Initially, the Kindle felt like an odd fit, but once I added it to my tailnet, it behaved like any other node.
I won’t pretend that the Kindle replaces a proper machine or a home server. It’s good at small, constant tasks, though. I’ve often used it to serve a couple of files over my home network, run quick scripts, pull stats, and poke at other devices to troubleshoot. With Python 3 installed, python3 -m http.server command shares the folder in one line. None of those tasks are demanding, and that’s the point.
The combination of e-ink and low power draw helps here. Even when I leave it plugged in for days, it doesn’t get warm or noisy. I often leave the same terminal output on the screen, since e-ink doesn’t need power to hold an image. I use the Linux server on my mini PC for the heavier jobs.
The Linux build runs on an old kernel, has no desktop environment, and has tight RAM. The project’s notes say most models stay on Linux 4.1.15. There’s no systemd, so you have to start services manually. You’ll be running everything headless and shouldn’t plan to compile anything heavy or run real workloads on the Kindle.
None of that bothered me because I never intended to use the Kindle as another workstation. I wanted a proper terminal to reach my home network from anywhere for maintenance jobs and a local Debian shell for quick checks.
A jailbreak opened it up for several practical uses.
If you’ve got a jailbroken Kindle for reading books, trying this experiment is far more interesting than keeping it a single-purpose device. It won’t replace your server, but it does offer a window into it.
I built a 1GB image on a Linux server on my mini PC and ran it on a Kindle Paperwhite (12th gen) on firmware version 5.19.6.
The Kindle Paperwhite Signature Edition is a premium e-reader that packs 32GB of storage and has wireless charging support alongside a bevy of luxury features like an ultra-thin design, an auto-adjusting front light, weeks of battery life, and a store with over 15 million titles.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

61,2026-10-14,"Reporter, Infosecurity Magazine

	While many supermarkets in France are still grappling with food stock disruptions due to a cyber-attack that targeted refrigeration controllers on October 11, the scope of the incident could be larger than previously observed.

	Recent developments suggest that the incident does not only impact Danfoss refrigeration controllers, but also temperature regulation devices from Dixell Electronics, an Italian brand owned by American heating and cooling equipment provider Copeland.

	Notably, French local media France 3 reported that an E. Leclerc supermarket in the Tarn region, in the south-west of France, experienced disruptions to its refrigeration systems, despite using Dixell equipment instead of Danfoss.

	French national newspaper Le Monde also reported a supermarket near Bordeaux, where attackers reportedly changed freezer settings from −22°C to 150°C.  A technician observed several settings being altered per second, suggesting automation.

	A security advisory published by the French national Computer emergency response team (CERT-FR) on October 12 specifically named Danfoss AK-SC and AK-SM series as well as Dixell xweb and Copeland xweb as “affected systems.”

	Speaking to Infosecurity, Amir Preminger, CTO of industrial security provider Claroty, said refrigeration controllers are a prime target, not just for supermarkets but also cold-storage facilities, warehouses, pharmaceuticals, chemical manufacturing and more, that often go overlooked by security teams.

	“Industrial control systems such as refrigeration controllers are often assumed to be on safely isolated networks, however, our research found many of these devices are directly exposed to the public internet. While this convenience significantly reduces maintenance costs by allowing engineers to troubleshoot issues remotely, it also lowers the barrier to entry for attackers,” he said.

	Preminger assessed that while the attack did not necessarily require sophisticated skills, the targeting was a clear deliberate attempt to cause damage.

	“I don’t think it’s sophisticated,” he said. “However, you cannot cause a defrost by mistake by typing the wrong parameter. You need to initiate the defrost. Somebody needed to guide the exploit, the campaign to have that impact.”

	He compared the apparent tactic with an incident at a food-processing facility in Israelin 2025. In that case, an attacker inside the facility’s network manipulated a refrigeration compressor and caused it to defrost. While the impact was similar, Preminger did not claim the incidents involved the same group.

	He argued that the broader concern was that refrigeration controllers were exposed to the internet. Preminger said such exposed Danfoss devices could be found outside of France too, including in Israel and the US, but he did not know what software versions those devices were running or whether they were vulnerable.

	He also cautioned that patching alone does not make internet exposure safe. His advice was to keep critical devices off the public internet and restrict access through measures such as corporate virtual private networks (VPNs) or even access point name (APN) gateways.

	He also urged operators to segment their networks, inventory their equipment, and know who can access each device. “Gate your devices, segment your devices. That’s the first frontier,” he said.

	Supermarket fridges are generally connected to the internet to comply with legislation, which, in Europe, requires them to maintain timestamped monitoring of temperatures to strengthen food safety and to install an alarm system in case of an abnormal rise in temperature.

	This connection also allows for remote maintenance.

	“This is an essential system for the proper preservation of food products,” emphasized Dominique Schelcher, CEO of Coopérative U, in a post on X.

The American Black Film Festival (ABFF) is expanding into theatrical distribution with the launch of ABFF97, a new initiative created to bring select independent films to audiences in major U.S. markets through curated theatrical releases.
AMC Theatres will serve as the initiative’s inaugural theatrical provider, with releases planned across key markets including Los Angeles, New York, Atlanta, Washington, D.C., Chicago, Detroit, Dallas, Houston, Charlotte, and Philadelphia.
Named for the festival’s founding year, ABFF97 builds on ABFF’s 30-year history of discovering and championing emerging filmmakers by extending that work beyond the festival circuit and into theatrical release.

ABFF97 extends our role from discovery to distribution, drawing on the audience relationships and cultural reach we have built over 30 years. With AMC as our initial theatrical provider, we have a strong foundation to bring these films to market.”
The initiative also builds on the success of the ongoing 2026 ABFF Pop-Up Tour, which has completed stops in Washington, D.C. and New York, with Atlanta and Houston to follow in the coming weeks. The audience response has reinforced interest in experiencing independent films on the big screen and helped inform ABFF’s expansion into theatrical distribution.

 









The launch establishes ABFF97 as a new theatrical pathway for independent film and a year-round expansion of ABFF’s work. ABFF97 will curate select films, develop theatrical release strategies, and lead marketing, publicity and audience engagement. These releases are designed to build visibility, demonstrate audience interest and strengthen opportunities for subsequent licensing and distribution across other platforms.
