TROPICANA FIELD (St.. Petersburg, Fla.) — After their first loss of the postseason, the Rays were in the throes of their first real test of October adversity. They had to find a way to stop the bleeding and prevent the ALCS against the Guardians from getting out of hand. 
Most of all, they needed a stopper. A few months ago, no one could’ve imagined that veteran right-hander Freddy Peralta would be the answer.
(Photo by Mary DeCicco/MLB Photos via Getty Images) 
Peralta, the fastball-reliant Tampa-Bay transplant, was spectacular in the Rays’ 3-1 win against Cleveland on Tuesday night at Tropicana FIeld. He twirled six scoreless innings — the longest start of his playoff career — and threw the fastest pitch of his career on a 99.4 mph fastball to Guardians right fielder Jo Adell in the fifth inning of his electric outing. With Peralta setting the tone, the Rays evened the series at one game apiece. 
""It's no secret,"" Peralta said, ""That I trust in my fastball a lot, and it's my best pitch.""
Peralta dotted the strike zone with high heat all night, which befuddled the Guardians lineup over and over again. It was somewhat surprising that the Guardians couldn’t do anything against Peralta’s four-seamer, because the team plainly advertised his biggest strength by throwing up ""Fastball Freddy"" graphics all over Tropicana Field before and during his outing.
(Photo by Mike Carlson/MLB Photos via Getty Images) 
The Guardians knew it was coming, and they still couldn’t hit it. Peralta’s confidence grew every time he collected a whiff with his unhittable weapon. 
""I thought his fastball was really good tonight,"" Guardians manager Stephen Vogt said. ""It was really good. And he was hitting that top rail. We talked about needing to get him down, but he was putting it right above, right at the top rail. And when he's doing that with the good ride and cross-fire extension, he's going to be tough. And he landed enough change-ups and curveballs to keep us honest. Freddy threw a really good game tonight.""
Peralta’s lights-out performance on Tuesday night was the culmination of tons of hard work from both the right-hander and the Rays organization since he joined the club in the summer. With the way the ball was gliding out of his hand and painting the black against Cleveland, it was easy to forget that this has actually been an emotional roller-coaster of a season for Peralta. 
He had a miserable first half with the Mets, posting a career-worst 4.99 ERA in 22 starts in New York. That experience was very hard for Peralta and his family because he was used to having success throughout his career. In 2025, Peralta finished fifth in National League Cy Young voting after he recorded a 2.70 ERA and led the league with 17 wins. He earned the reputation as one of the game’s most elite active starting pitchers when the Mets traded for him last offseason. 
""It was the first time that I was struggling like that,"" Peralta said of his time as a Met. ""And also in New York, an organization that traded for me, an organization that trusted me, and I wasn't able to give the best of myself. It was a little disappointing for me. I didn't feel good about it. 
""But when I got traded here, I just felt different. For some reason I got here, and I felt like myself again.""
(Photo by Julio Aguilar/Getty Images) 
After beginning his Rays tenure with a seven-run clunker against the Rockies, the Rays coaching staff got to work. They helped Peralta fix his mechanics, ditch his windup, and pitch exclusively from the stretch. Removing his windup allowed the righty to get to the mound faster, which freed ""Fastball Freddy"" up to use his favorite weapon with ease. 
The mechanics have been repeatable, but most of all, they’ve been simple. Peralta credits Rays pitching coach Kyle Snyder, bullpen coach Jorge Moncada and bullpen catcher Charlie Valerio for being in the trenches with him since he joined Tampa Bay in August. They gave him a plan, one that didn’t overload him with too much information, and he followed it. The Rays wanted Peralta to focus on regaining confidence in his fastball and throw more changeups. Always the team player, ready to follow orders, Peralta went to work.
(Photo by Ray Bahner/MLB Photos via Getty Images) 
He won American League Pitcher of the Month in September after recording a 0.96 ERA in five elite starts. Less than two months after joining the Rays, Peralta was back. 
""I don't think we'd ever expect, 'Hey, we're going to acquire a guy, and he's going to win Pitcher of the Month three weeks later,'"" Cash said. ""We were very fortunate to get him. I give our scouts a ton of credit for recognizing and appreciating that Freddy Peralta wasn't that far removed from the Freddy Peralta in Milwaukee. And we have certainly reaped the benefits of him. 
""The type of teammate, it feels like he's been here for four or five years, the way he carries himself, the way he interacts. The pitching and the numbers he's thrown up speak for themselves.""
Peralta is lauded for being a terrific teammate everywhere he goes, in large part because of his infectious positive outlook on life. When he’s not on the mound, he’s always smiling. When he was on the Mets, he hosted team get-togethers and organized off-day outings. When he was on the Brewers, he hosted barbecues at his house. Already, in a matter of weeks, Rays players have bonded with Peralta in what has been a seamless transition to his third team in 13 months. 
This winter, Peralta might be heading to his fourth.
(Photo by Mike Stobe/Getty Images) 
The 30-year-old is set to enter free agency this offseason, and the Rays have helped ensure that he’ll get a big pay day. Peralta is earning just $8 million this season in the final year of his previous contract. Next year’s market, particularly for free-agent starters, is particularly thin. With the way Peralta bounced back this year, and the way he’s showing up in the postseason, he should garner plenty of interest around the league. 
Perhaps, even a reunion with the Mets is on the table. 
""I had no idea,"" Peralta said when asked if he was aware that Mets owner Steven Cohen was in attendance at Tropicana Field on Tuesday. ""I don't even know what to say because I don't know if he came to watch just the game or maybe me, too. What I can say is that we have a good relationship. When he traded for me, he texted me. We spoke. Also, when we were in spring training, we had really good communication. He spoke with me when I got traded back to the Rays.
""I think he's awesome. And I'm happy that he was here watching, not watching me, watching the Rays."""

65,2026-10-14,"A Nasdaq-listed fintech company led by a former FTX Europe executive is planning to bring tokenized U.S. stocks trading onto blockchain rails under the Securities and Exchange Commission's (SEC) new regulatory framework.
Perpetuals.com (PDC), headed by CEO Patrick Gruhn, filed a notice on Wednesday that it plans to operate a tokenized securities venue under the SEC's five-year ""innovation exemption,"" which allows certain marketplaces to trade U.S. stocks on public blockchains without registering as traditional securities exchanges.
The move makes Perpetuals.com the second company to pursue a marketplace under the exemption, following OKXICE, a joint venture between crypto exchange OKX and New York Stock Exchange (NYSE) parent Intercontinental Exchange (ICE), which last week filed plans for round-the-clock trading of more than 60 tokenized U.S. stocks.
The exemption, introduced in September, allows certain marketplaces to trade U.S. stocks on public blockchains without registering as traditional securities exchanges. It requires tokens to carry the same shareholder rights as ordinary shares, including dividends and voting rights, while giving companies 30 days to object to having their shares traded on a tokenized securities venue.
The move marks a return to tokenized equities for Gruhn and his team, who helped develop some of the earliest stock-linked tokens offered by crypto exchanges including Binance and FTX in 2021.
Gruhn co-founded Digital Assets AG, a Swiss-based tokenization company that worked with German investment firm CM-Equity to bring digital tokens linked to stocks such as Tesla and Coinbase to crypto traders. Digital Assets AG was later acquired by FTX Group, becoming part of FTX Europe, which Gruhn led before the crypto exchange group's collapse in November 2022.
Those early products differed from the securities envisioned under the SEC's new framework. While they offered price exposure to underlying shares, Binance's stock tokens did not provide voting rights. Binance discontinued the offering in July 2021 amid regulatory scrutiny, including concerns raised by German regulator BaFin over the lack of a published securities prospectus.
""The Perpetuals team has long been trailblazers in tokenization, and this expansion into the U.S. allows us to further utilize our platform and deep expertise,"" Gruhn said in a statement.
The company already provides trading technology for Perpetual Markets MTF, a Cyprus-based marketplace licensed under Europe's MiFID II framework that also received authorization under the EU's Markets in Crypto-Assets regulation in August.
The firm reported $65.2 million in total assets at the end of April, while posting a $17.6 million net loss during its fiscal year.
It also announced an agreement with Datavault AI earlier this year to support trading infrastructure for planned tokenized commodity offerings involving copper, gold and other physical assets, with more than $300 million in targeted issuance.
According to the public notice shared with CoinDesk, Perpetuals' U.S. marketplace will be operated by Kephas Corporation, a Delaware-based subsidiary. Trading would take place through permissioned automated market maker (AMM) liquidity pools, initially on Kronos X Public, an Ethereum-compatible layer-2 blockchain developed by the company.
AMMs use smart contracts and liquidity pools to facilitate trades between buyers and sellers instead of traditional exchange order books. Each pool would pair a tokenized U.S. stock with a digital asset, initially expected to be a form of USDC USDC$1.0003, allowing eligible investors to trade shares against the Circle-issued U.S. dollar-pegged stablecoin.
Perpetuals has not set a firm launch date yet. Trading could begin in mid-November at the earliest following the required 30-day period after the public notice, a company spokesperson told CoinDesk, depending on the completion of market-data and compliance arrangements, smart-contract security reviews and other preparations.
The company also plans to publish updated disclosures, including live smart-contract addresses, before trading begins, the spokesperson added.

66,2026-10-14,"Linux has forever been considered above Windows when it comes to security. Part of the reason for that, and what people usually assume, is that Linux doesn't have a meaningful market share for attackers to target with sophisticated malware or attacks. While that holds true when compared to Windows's market share, it definitely is not the full picture. Linux also has an advanced permission management system that usually prevents files from being accessed or executed, which provides additional security. I tested this by setting up WSL and an Ubuntu installation, then letting it scan my data, including a file infected with make-believe malware, to see how it responded.
 Let's look at what happened earlier this year when hackers decided that Linux had enough users to launch an attack. In June 2026, Arch User Repository (AUR) was compromised by hackers who injected malware into over 400 packages. The attackers adopted abandoned packages and edited their build instructions, so installing them also pulled in an npm package called atomic-lockfile to deliver rootkit-like malware. Sonatype later noted that an estimated roughly 1,500 packages across AUR were impacted and were modified to steal credentials. Since then, AI has made it easier for people with limited coding knowledge to build and execute targeted attacks aimed at stealing or harvestingcredentials.
 Linux permission setup does not allow files to be executable by default without explicit user permission. This ensures that malware that was downloaded or transferred to a Linux system is not executed automatically. I tested this by asking my Ubuntu CLI to run a simple one-line script, which it denied because the system didn't have enough permissions to run it. System folders work in a similar way, and running a command to create a new file simply failed.
One caveat, of course, was that my account on Ubuntu is set up in the sudo group, so the system safety was more or less fully in my hands. Good terminal habits can be a life-saver when it comes to Linux, especially if you are logged in as admin or using sudo. One of the reasons for the AUR attack was that users probably ran packages without reviewing changes, allowing malicious code to run on the system.
These neat tricks make my Linux experiments a lot easier
 I installed ClamAV on my copy of Ubuntu and downloaded its database of about 3.6 million entries. Once done, I created an EICAR test file on my system. For those unaware, an EICAR test file is a harmless 68-byte to 128-byte dummy malware file created by the European Institute for Computer Antivirus Research (EICAR) and the Computer Antivirus Research Organization to test antivirus. ClamAV was quick to flag the file as infected when I ran the virus scan and reported EICAR-signature FOUND straight away.
However, this is half the story. Antivirus like ClamAV is great at recognizing known threats and malware but falls short with new malware, or malware that isn't obvious or is embedded in the executable. That's where things differ for Linux: having antivirus, while good practice, doesn't make you fully secure against threats.
I don't need an antivirus, but if I did, I would install ClamAV. It's a free, open-source tool that can run on multiple platforms seamlessly.
 As mentioned above, antivirus is only half the story on Linux, and Linux permissions limit the damage if you accidentally download malware. This is why people often don't need antivirus on Linux as long as they follow good habits, like not giving every file or folder full permissions and ensuring packages come from trusted sources.
Another tip that works wonders is to always check what changed when a package was recently updated. Also, ensure the package is coming from a trusted source and is not modified by a middleman.
The obvious counterpoint is that something like ClamAV has no real downside while providing an extra layer of protection, helping you scan and vet files downloaded online or in email attachments.
Linux has different security permissions, and each one impacts what can be done with a file and a directory.
Linux is generally safer than Windows because of its permission system, how it handles packages, and its rapid patching of bugs and vulnerabilities, plus a smaller user base than Windows or macOS, which makes it less lucrative for attackers looking to do maximum damage.
Overall, this makes a case for Linux not needing an antivirus in the traditional sense, like one would for Windows or a Mac. However, tools like ClamAV can provide an extra layer of security against known threats.
The threat landscape is changing, and it is changing rapidly thanks to AI tools that can comb through data and lines of code to find vulnerabilities that can be targeted. So the notion that Linux doesn't require any security is outdated and, frankly, dangerous. However, the good news is that if you follow good security hygiene, keep your system updated, and exercise caution when downloading or running unverified scripts, you should be in a good place.
We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

It is a complete myth that Windows has more market share than Linux. It has more in the desktop computer OS space, but *far* *less* of the valued target space.  Linux powers most servers as well as most of the other Internet space such as routers, switches, wireless access points, etc. On top of that Android is Linux based so that is a *huge* amount of people that use Linux systems and don't even realize it. This is especially true now that a lot of people just use their phone rather than a computer these days. Even I Phone users often connect to Linux powered WiFi devices. Furthermore, Chromebooks are Linux powered.

Hackers are far more motivated to crack into Linux systems, but not only is the security model better out of the box / by design, there are additional tools like Security Enhanced Linux (SE Linux) that simply aren't available in the Windows world. (Yes, I know Windows has UAC capabilities, but most Windows people are incapable of using those effectively). Basically I am saying the author is right that the reason viruses are much less common is not that Linux has less market share, but the reason that is a myth is because the claim it has less market share is ill-informed / false.
As I have written here before, having a Linux AV is a good precaution - and ClamUI makes ClamAV easy to use without the clunky and outdated terminal.
Yes, Linux and Windows are configured differently, but that doesn't mean that Linux is totally safe.  Type in ""Linus Anti-Virus"" and you'll see commercial AV products for industry - and up until 2024, they were offered for individual users as well.
In conclusion, you wouldn't practice unsafe sex; nor should you engage in unsafe computing either.
Ever since I got interested in Linux last year, I was disappointed that there wasn't an easy to use AV program.  ClamUI changed all that.  So let's just bury the hatchet, smoke the peace pipe and move on.
Live long and prosper.

67,2026-10-14,"The ESA – not, not the cool one – a lobby group made up of various videogame and entertainment companies, has asked the US Trade Representative (USTR) to add Anbernic to its list of companies that are supposedly notorious for engaging in and facilitating piracy. If you’re unaware, Anbernic is one of the bigger names in Android and Linux-based handheld gaming devices targeted at the retrocomputing market.
While many online sellers do include an SD card full of ROMs alongside emulation hardware of all types, Anbernic itself does not appear to do the same when selling directly from its website. Regardless, the ESA argues that these ROM-laden Anbernic devices selling through “normal retail channels” help to “normalize hardware-based piracy by delivering illicit catalogues through a polished consumer product.” This, the ESA says, meets the threshold for a “superfake,” an ill-defined designation that applies to “high-quality counterfeit trademark goods … on a commercial scale.”
There’s a lot of murkiness surrounding whether or not Anbernic sells their devices with preloaded ROMs or not. The number of reports from people who say they did get a ton of ROMs on their Anbernic devices, bought from Anbernic’s own store, is large enough that it’s clearly something they do. However, there’s an equal number of people who say their Anbernic devices were completely free of ROMs, and I’m one of them – I’ve bought like three or so devices from their store over the years, and they never came with any ROMs. My most recent purchase was an RG 35XXPro Linux-based model, and there were no ROMs on it (I replaced the stock operating system with Knulli, which has been a great experience).
If Anbernic does indeed ship at least some of their devices with ROMs preinstalled, then it’s their own damn fault if that lands them in legal trouble. It’s not like downloading ROMs is particularly difficult (just do an internet search for your preferred system and something like “ROM pack” and you’ll get the console’s complete library in a single download), so they really have no need to do so.
There’s one extra layer here though. Third-party resellers like Amazon offer loads of Anbernic devices preloaded with SD cards full of ROMs, all from third parties not affiliated with Anbernic. And wouldn’t you know it – Amazon is a member of the ESA lobby group. In other words, the problem also lies with lax retailers not properly vetting what they’re offering, but instead of going after places like Amazon, they’re going after Anbernic instead.
Amazon is a haven for illegal and counterfeit products, but they’re so wealthy and powerful they just get away with it.

Follow me on Mastodon @[email protected]
However, there’s an equal number of people who say their Anbernic devices were completely free of ROMs, and I’m one of them – I’ve bought like three or so devices from their store over the years, and they never came with any ROMs.
I’ve bought two Anbernic handhelds from Amazon in the past, both came from third parties and both had an SD card installed that was preloaded with tons of commercial ROMs. Of course I removed those cards and wiped them immediately since I couldn’t be sure they were free from malware. My latest Anbernic handheld, the RG DS, I ordered directly from Anbernic. It had no commercial ROMs installed, only a few hobbyist ones that I was able to verify as open source and legitimate copies. I run an alternative Linux-based OS on it via the SD slot, but I kept the Android based native OS on the internal storage in case the SD card flakes out.
I hope Anbernic is able to work this out in whatever way that allows them to continue shipping to the US; their handhelds in my experience are the best on the market, especially if the Steam Deck or similar x86-based handhelds are too big and/or too expensive. I sold my Steam Deck a few months back (for more than I paid for it new in 2023, thanks RAMpocalypse!) and I don’t even miss it; the RG DS fits in my pocket and plays 99% of the games I played on the Deck.

68,2026-10-14,"
  By Darragh McDonald |
  at October 14, 2026 4:51pm CDT  
With the regular season in the books, MLBTR is taking a position-by-position look at the 2026-27 free agent market. For the final entry in the series, we head to the right-handed relief market.
Player ages are for the 2027 season, and names within each tier are listed alphabetically, not “ranked.” Only players who appeared in MLB (or on major league 60-day injured lists) this year are included.
Previous entries in this series: Catcher, First Base, Second Base, Third Base, Shortstop, Center Field, Corner Outfield, Designated Hitter, Starting Pitcher, Lefty Relief
Proven Closers
Bednar has had some ups and downs but has been a lockdown closer for most of the past five years. He racked up 19 saves for the Pirates in 2022 with a 2.61 earned run average. The following year, he posted a flat ERA of 2.00 and notched 39 more saves.
A big setback came in 2024. Bednar’s strikeout rate had been hovering around 30% in previous seasons but dipped to 22.1% while his ERA shot up to 5.77. He got out to a slow start in 2025 and was optioned to the minors in April. He was back less than three weeks later and has been quite consistent since then. He has thrown 131 innings since being recalled from that brief optional assignment with a 2.20 ERA, a 30.8% strikeout rate, 7.1% walk rate, 48.1% ground ball rate and 61 saves.
The big ERA spike in 2024 isn’t likely to be a huge issue for Bednar, as there’s some precedent working in his favor. Josh Hader had a 5.22 ERA in 2022 but bounced back with a 1.28 ERA in 2023. He then signed a five-year, $95MM deal with the Astros. Edwin Díaz had a 5.59 ERA in 2019. He corrected in subsequent years and eventually nabbed a five-year, $102MM deal with the Mets, with an opt-out after three.
The Yankees will first have to decide whether or not to give Bednar a qualifying offer. It’s somewhat rare for a reliever to receive a QO but the top closers usually do. In the past decade, Díaz, Hader, Raisel Iglesias, Will Smith, Craig Kimbrel, Greg Holland, Wade Davis and Kenley Jansen have received QOs. They all declined. Holland eventually settled for a one-year, $14MM deal but everyone else in that group landed a deal of at least three years and $40MM.
Bednar could accept the QO, stick in the Bronx for another year and return to free agency after the lockout but he’s already 32 years old and will probably look to cash in. He shouldn’t be able to get to the Hader/Díaz level of guarantee since he’s older than those guys and has never flashed the same kind of 40-50% strikeout rate upside, but a strong three- or four-year deal with an eight-figure salary should be attainable.
Iglesias keeps putting up numbers as he pushes towards his late 30s. He just tossed 60 innings with a 2.55 ERA. That’s actually a slight improvement from 2025, when he had a 3.21 ERA. Under the hood, some things trended in the wrong direction, though only slightly. The velocity on his four-seamer and sinker dropped by about half a mile per hour. His 26.8% strikeout rate and 7.1% walk rate were both slightly worse than last year’s 27.4% and 6% clips. On the other hand, he increased his ground ball rate from 30.5% to 37.6%.
It has now been six straight full seasons of posting an ERA of 3.21 or lower for Iglesias. He racked up at least 29 saves in five of those six. Despite the consistency, his age will prevent him from getting a long-term offer. He was a free agent last offseason and signed a one-year, $16MM deal with Atlanta.
He has a case for two years, even at this age. In the past five years, Mark Melancon, Adam Ottavino, Chris Martin and Blake Treinen all signed two-year deals beginning in their age-37 seasons. But since Iglesias just returned to Atlanta on a one-year deal last time, it wouldn’t be a shock for that to happen again.
Solid Setup Arms
Abreu has been one of the better setup arms in baseball for a few years. Unfortunately, he is coming off his worst season since he became established. From 2022 to 2025, he logged 281 2/3 innings with a 2.30 ERA, 34.3% strikeout rate, 10.4% walk rate and 39.9% ground ball rate. In 2026, he had a 3.73 ERA. His 39.9% grounder rate was in line with expectations. His 29.9% strikeout rate was down for him but still well above average. Most concerningly, his walk rate shot up to 15.3%.
He at least finished with some positive momentum, as his worst stretch of the year was right out of the gate. After 7 1/3 innings, he had a 14.73 ERA and horrendous 30.2% walk rate. In his final 55 1/3 innings, he had a 2.28 ERA. His 12.4% walk rate in that time was still concerning but much closer to season-opening expectations.
His velocity was down a bit for the year but was lowest in May and bounced back after that. He was mostly around 97 mph with his four-seamer from 2022 to 2025. He was at 95.7 in 2026 but with a nadir of 94.4 in May and then 96.4 from July to September.
Based on his track record, it’s possible he secures a multi-year deal with an eight-figure salary. If teams are scared off by this year’s control woes and slight velo drop, perhaps he will be limited to one-year offers or he could try to get two years with an opt-out halfway through.
It’s been quite a journey for Adam but he has finally made it to six years of service time. A late bloomer, he didn’t really break out until he his age-30 season, but he then went on to have several great campaigns.
Unfortunately, he is hitting the market at an unfortunate time. From 2022 to 2025, he threw 256 2/3 innings with a 2.07 ERA, 29.2% strikeout rate, 8.4% walk rate and 43.1% ground ball rate. This year, he missed almost three months with a shoulder strain and was limited to 34 1/3 innings. His 2.62 ERA doesn’t look bad but was mostly due to a fortunate .245 batting average on balls in play and 91.7% strand rate. His 15.5% strikeout rate was well shy of his previous benchmark and the 22.1% league average. He tossed 3 1/3 innings in the postseason without notching a strikeout.
Adam’s track record is strong but he will likely be limited to one-year offers because of his age, his recent injury and lack of punchouts this year.
Blackburn has been a starter for most of his career. He opened the 2026 season in a long relief role for the Yankees. He may have earned a rotation role if some injuries popped up but the Yanks were relatively healthy for most of the year. Blackburn was kept in the bullpen, apart from two starts as an opener.
He slid from long relief to a more typical setup role as the season went along. In total, he tossed 85 innings with a 1.59 ERA. His 20.5% strikeout rate and 9.8% walk rate weren’t too impressive but he induced grounders on 54.7% of balls in play. He racked up a save and 11 holds before the season was done. He added another six innings in the postseason without allowing a run.
It wasn’t quite as dominant as the ERA would suggest. As mentioned, the strikeout and walk profile was middling. Blackburn also got help from a .230 BABIP and 88.9% strand rate. His 3.75 FIP and 3.92 SIERA don’t provide confidence for him keeping the ERA down. Even when he was pitching shorter stints in the second half, his 22.4% strikeout rate was only modestly better than his season-long rate. Even that slight uptick was offset by a 12.6% walk rate after the break. In his six scoreless playoff innings, he didn’t strike anyone out.
Caveats aside, an ERA under 2.00 is hard to ignore and Blackburn did well at limiting damage. Statcast pegged his average exit velocity, barrel rate and hard hit rate in the 74th percentile or better. He could get a setup job somewhere but it’s also possible his past experience as a starter helps him. Steven Matz had a somewhat similar 2025 season and parlayed that into a two-year, $15MM deal with the Rays for 2026.
Ginkel just wrapped up a nice bounceback season. Over 2023 and 2024, he posted a 2.86 ERA in 135 1/3 innings. He struck out 27% of batters faced, limited walks to a 7% clip and induced grounders on 46.7% of balls in play. In the first of those two seasons, he added 11 2/3 scoreless innings as part of Arizona’s World Series run.
Last year was a step back. Right shoulder issues limited him to 25 2/3 innings with an awful 7.36 ERA, 24% strikeout rate and 10.7% walk rate. In 2026, he was mostly back in his previous form, tossing 67 2/3 innings. His 4.12 ERA doesn’t look too impressive but his 66.8% strand rate was to the unfortunate side. His 26.2% strikeout rate and 6.2% walk rate were back to his previous range. His 39.8% ground ball rate was his lowest since his 2019 debut but this year’s 3.50 FIP and 3.13 SIERA suggest he was pretty close to his 2023-24 performance.
Ginkel may be limited to one-year deals on account of his age and 2025 struggles. His 2026 was a nice return to form but more with the underlying numbers than the surface-level stats. His trajectory is somewhat similar to Andrew Kittredge. For Kittredge, injuries wiped out most of his 2023. He had a solid 2024 in his age-34 season and then secured a one-year, $10MM deal with a club option from the Orioles. Ginkel is younger than Kittredge was, but his walk-year ERA is higher than the 2.80 mark that Kittredge put up.
Speaking of bounceback years, Phillips is having a great one. From 2022 to 2024, he gave the Dodgers 179 frames with a 2.21 ERA, 29.6% strikeout rate, 6.5% walk rate and 42.6% ground ball rate. Tommy John surgery in May of 2025 wiped out most of that season. He hit free agency and signed a one-year, $6.5MM deal with the Dodgers while still recovering. He came off the IL in July and has essentially returned to form. He tossed 26 innings this year with a 3.46 ERA, 31.1% strikeout rate, 5.7% walk rate and 31.8% ground ball rate. He has made three scoreless appearances in the postseason so far.
Phillips isn’t closing for the Dodgers now because he’s behind Edwin Díaz and Tanner Scott on the bullpen chart but he does have previous experience. He saved 24 games for the Dodgers in 2023 and 18 more in 2024. He should be able to fit on almost any club, whether they need a closer or just an extra setup arm. A strong multi-year deal with an eight-figure salary feels attainable.
Specialists/Middle Relief
Armstrong was a free agent last winter and netted a one-year, $5.5MM deal with the Guardians. He missed a couple of months with a calf strain but threw 35 1/3 innings with a 4.58 ERA. His 22.1% strikeout rate and 42.7% ground ball rate were around average but he walked 12.3% of batters faced. It wasn’t a great season but he did notch eight holds and cracked Cleveland’s postseason roster. He has thrown 5 1/3 scoreless innings in the playoffs so far.
The Diamondbacks gave Clarke a one-year deal with a $1.5MM guarantee last offseason. That was a nice investment, as he gave them 73 innings with a 2.96 ERA. His 15.6% strikeout rate was well below average but he filled up the strike zone and limited damage. He only walked 4.9% of batters faced. He induced grounders on 45% of balls in play. Statcast put his average exit velocity and hard hit rate in the 73rd and 78th percentile of qualified pitchers, respectively. He was credited with 14 holds for the Snakes. The low strikeout rate won’t help him with modern front offices but his overall performance should net him another major league deal and maybe even a bit of a raise.
De Los Santos has bounced around the league in recent years, generally posting passable numbers. Dating back to the start of 2022, he has pitched for six different clubs, logging 320 1/3 innings in the process with a 4.19 ERA. His 23.8% strikeout rate and 8.3% walk rate in that time are around average. He was scooped up by Houston in August of last year after Atlanta let him go. The Astros then kept him around for 2026 with a $1.6MM salary. He posted a 4.79 ERA while earning five saves and ten holds. He should be able to get another major league deal with a similar salary to what he made this year.
Johnson has a strong track record but is trending down. After a stint in Japan in 2019, he returned to North America and posted a strikeout rate above 30% from 2020 to 2023. That ticked down to 28.4% in 2024, 24.8% last year and 19.9% this year. He finished 2026 with a 4.62 ERA, his highest since his return from Japan, apart from his injury-marred 2022. He secured a $6.5MM deal last winter. He should have less earning power this time but his velocity mostly held steady and his reputation is good, so perhaps he can get another big league deal.
Junis has worked as a starter and long reliever in his career but his more of a traditional reliever lately. He just tossed 54 1/3 innings for the Rangers with a solid 3.64 ERA. His 16.2% strikeout rate was below par but his 7.7% walk rate and 41.6% ground ball rate were close to average. He did a good job limiting damage, as Statcast pegged his average exit velocity, barrel rate and hard hit rate in the 72nd percentile or better among qualified pitchers. He continues to be effective but the low-strikeout, low-velo profile isn’t en vogue and his age doesn’t help him. Junis got a $4MM deal last winter and his earning power should be fairly comparable going into 2027.
Kittredge posted a 3.54 ERA this year, just barely higher than the 3.40 he had the year prior. But under the hood, things moved more drastically. His strikeout rate dropped from 30.8% to 18.7% and his ground ball rate dropped from 49.2% to 42.1%. His 3.81 SIERA this year was more than a run higher than last year’s 2.49. One year ago, Kittredge had enough appeal for the Orioles to send cash considerations to the Cubs just for the opportunity to trigger a $9MM club option. But at the 2026 deadline, he didn’t get moved despite the O’s selling other players. He should be able to secure another big league deal but at a salary well below this year’s.
Injuries limited Loáisiga to about 50 total big league innings from 2023 to 2025. He had to settle for a minor league deal with the Diamondbacks coming into 2026. That turned into a nice pick-up for the Snakes, as Loáisiga gave them 67 innings with a 2.42 ERA. His 18.4% strikeout rate was a few ticks shy of average but his 5% walk rate and 56.4% ground ball rate were both quite good. He got some help from a .270 BABIP and 76% strand rate. His 3.43 SIERA was a full run north of his ERA. Still, Loáisiga has good velo and a long track record of results as a groundballer. He should be able to get a big league deal this time.
Romano earned saves for both the Angels and Rockies this year but the results weren’t pretty. He finished the season with a 5.61 ERA. Pitching at Coors Field surely contributed to that but it doesn’t explain everything. Romano’s velocity is about four ticks below his peak. This year’s 25.5% strikeout rate was above league average but he also walked 12.4% of opponents. His 4.11 FIP and 4.01 SIERA suggest he was better than his ERA but still not great. A return to a closer’s role in Colorado is possible but he shouldn’t have a prominent role on a competitive team.
Santana has been up-and-down lately. He had a career ERA of 5.17 at the end of 2023. He posted a 3.89 ERA in 2024 and then 2.18 last year. He then posted a 5.56 ERA in 2026. Last year’s ERA was a mirage but he at least had a decent 22.2% strikeout rate and 6.3% walk rate. Those numbers ended up at 19.6% and 10% in 2026. He’ll be looking to bounce back in 2027. He was released by the Pirates this year but his past success was enough for him to finish the season with a spot on Arizona’s big league roster.
Schreiber is going into free agency on a down note. From 2022 to 2025, he threw 227 1/3 innings with a 3.33 ERA, 24.7% strikeout rate, 8.7% walk rate and 47.1% ground ball rate. This year’s 3.28 ERA doesn’t look bad but his 16.3% strikeout rate and 10.6% walk rate were way off his norms. The Royals sold at the deadline but seemingly didn’t find a taker for Schreiber. He was placed on waivers in August. His reputation is strong enough that the Yanks claimed him. He had a 1.64 ERA after that claim with a tiny 9.3% strikeout rate in the small sample of 11 innings. He made $3.715MM in 2026 and may have to settle for less next year.
Sewald was once a lockdown closer. In 2026, he still racked up 24 saves but his performance was all over the place. It was his third straight year with an ERA in the mid-4.00s. With Arizona, he had an awful 6.23 ERA but good numbers under the hood and awful luck. He was put on waivers in August and landed with the Blue Jays. Then everything flipped. He had a 0.63 ERA, poor peripherals and great luck. The end result was 53 1/3 innings with a 4.73 ERA, 24.2% strikeout rate and 5.9% walk rate. He got $1.5MM last winter and might have similar earning power this offseason.
Injured Wild Cards
Fairbanks was one of the better relievers in the league from 2020 to 2025. He signed a $13MM deal with the Marlins for 2026 but had the worst season of his career. Nerve issues in his throwing arm put him on the injured list multiple times and he posted a 6.31 ERA in 41 1/3 innings. It was reported in mid-September that he would require surgery for thoracic outlet syndrome, though he didn’t go under the knife until the following month. On October 2, he told Foul Territory that he still had not yet had the procedure but that it was scheduled for the following week.
It’s not known which version of TOS he was diagnosed with, so his timeline is unclear. He’ll be looking for a bounceback opportunity but might not sign until after the lockout when there’s more clarity on his health.
Garcia is in basically the same spot to Fairbanks but he should have a slight head-start on the recovery process as García underwent TOS surgery in August. From 2019 to 2025, he tossed 322 innings with a 3.58 ERA, 27.1% strikeout rate and 7% walk rate. He should have opportunities based on his track record but his age and health will obviously impact his market.
Graterol was an effective setup arm for the Dodgers from 2020 to 2023. His big velocity didn’t translate into strikeouts but he was elite at generating ground balls. Injuries have wiped out essentially three whole seasons. He made just seven appearances in 2024 and then missed all of the two subsequent campaigns, mostly due to right shoulder issues. He underwent surgery in November of 2024. He began a rehab assignment in May of 2026 but that lasted just four appearances. He required back surgery in June. There haven’t been any updates on his status since then.
From 2022 to 2024, Harvey tossed 150 2/3 innings with a 3.29 ERA, 27.3% strikeout rate, 6.7% walk rate and 42.7% ground ball rate. A teres major strain and an adductor strain limited him to just 10 2/3 innings in 2025. The Cubs gave him a $6MM deal, hoping for a bounceback. Instead, he made just four appearances before hitting the IL with triceps inflammation. A stress reaction was discovered in May. He threw a bullpen in August, per Meghan Montemurro of the Chicago Tribune, but he never came off the IL.
Over 2022 and 2023, Jiménez threw 113 innings with a 3.27 ERA, 32% strikeout rate and 5.8% walk rate. That was impressive enough for Atlanta to give him a three-year, $26MM deal. The first year was basically more of the same, as Jiménez posted a 2.62 ERA in 2024 with a 29.6% strikeout rate and 8.3% walk rate. He had surgery in November of 2024 to address cartilage damage in his left knee. He had another procedure on that knee the following November. He spent the entire year on the IL, though he did make three minor league rehab appearances in September. It’s encouraging that Jiménez was back on the mound but he is a question mark after missing two entire seasons.
Leiter battled right hip issues this year and underwent season-ending surgery in July. He has generally posted strong peripherals but poor ERAs thanks to his BABIPs. That may be luck but it keeps happening, so it’s becoming harder to believe he’ll regress to league average. In 140 innings since the start of 2024, he has a 28.8% strikeout rate, 8.1% walk rate and 44.7% ground ball rate, which are all good numbers. But his .356 BABIP in that stretch was almost 70 points north of league average, pushing his ERA to 4.56. He was able to get $2.85MM from the A’s last winter but is now a year older and coming off the aforementioned hip surgery.
Treinen signed a two-year, $22MM deal with the Dodgers going into the 2025 season, despite his age and injury history. Over those two seasons, he logged 56 1/3 innings with a 4.47 ERA. His 25.9% strikeout rate was still above average but a drop from his previous levels. His 12.4% walk rate was a few ticks worse than average. He missed time due to forearm, elbow and shoulder issues. He finished this season on the IL due to that shoulder and hasn’t been on the Dodgers’ postseason roster. There’s a chance he could be activated later if the Dodgers stay alive.
Long Reliever/Swing Options
Canning posted a 3.77 ERA for the Mets in 2025 but his nice season was ended by an Achilles rupture. He was still recovering when the Padres gave him a $2.5MM deal for 2026. He was reinstated from the IL in May and struggled in the rotation. He was moved to a long relief role and fared better. He had a 6.97 ERA in his first 62 innings this year and a 2.70 ERA in his final 26 2/3 but there was some batted-ball and sequencing luck pushing those numbers apart. For the whole season, he threw 88 2/3 innings with a 22.3% strikeout rate, 10.5% walk rate, 46.9% ground ball rate, 5.68 ERA, 4.75 FIP and 4.20 SIERA.
The A’s gave Civale $6MM and a rotation job to start the season. He posted a 5.42 ERA and was designated for assignment in July. The Cubs traded for him and put him in a long relief role. He posted a 1.72 ERA the rest of the way. His true talent is somewhere in the middle. On the whole, he tossed 111 1/3 innings with a 4.20 ERA, 18.5% strikeout rate and 7.1% walk rate.
Fedde struggled in 2025, posting a 5.49 ERA. He had to settle for a $1.5MM deal with the White Sox. He bounced back with a 3.83 ERA, though that oversells him a bit. His 16.3% strikeout rate and 38.1% ground ball rate were subpar, while his 8.6% walk rate was around average. A .271 BABIP and 78% strand rate helped him out a lot, hence his 5.01 FIP and 4.76 SIERA. He officially made 15 starts and 18 relief appearances, though he was mostly pitching multiple innings regardless of when he entered.
Quantrill had a 6.04 ERA in 2025 and had to settle for a minor league deal with the Rangers going into 2026. He eventually got a roster spot and had a good season, initially as a long reliever before getting a proper rotation role. He made 15 official starts and 17 relief appearances with a 2.86 ERA in 110 innings. His 5.6% walk rate was strong but his 18.3% strikeout rate a few ticks worse than average. A .239 BABIP and 77.6% strand rate helped him out. His 4.01 FIP and 4.25 SIERA are perhaps better baseline expectations for what he can provide. That’s still great return on investment for a minor league deal and he may have earned himself a major league gig for 2027 but a lat strain put him on the IL with a week left in the season, clouding his status somewhat.
Richards settled for a minor league deal with the Phillies coming into this year. He got called up in April and made two appearances before he was traded to the White Sox. He ended up logging 83 innings over 54 appearances on the year with a 3.69 ERA, 21.2% strikeout rate and 7.5% walk rate.
Williams has been a swingman for most of his career, with 179 starts and 55 relief appearances. He began 2026 recovering from an internal brace procedure. He came off the IL in July and pitched for the Nationals and Rangers down the stretch. He threw 28 2/3 innings over 15 appearances with a 3.14 ERA, 28.3% strikeout rate, 8.3% walk rate and 47.3% ground ball rate.
Player Options
After a down year in 2025, Helsley signed a two-year, $28MM deal with the Orioles. The second year was a player option, giving Helsley the chance to return to the open market if he bounced back in 2026. There were some encouraging signs as he got some of his strikeouts back and cranked up his ground ball rate but his control wasn’t good and he made multiple trips to the IL with right elbow issues. He should stick with the O’s for 2027, collect the remaining $14MM and make another attempt for a bounceback.
Pagán’s case is more borderline. He struggled early on and hit the IL with a hamstring strain. He was dominant after he came back. Before hitting the IL, he had a 6.43 ERA in 14 innings. After the IL stint, it was 28 2/3 innings with a 1.88 ERA. He got a lot of help from a .227 BABIP and 98.1% strand rate but he struck out 30.4% of batters faced and only walked 4.5%. His 3.38 FIP and 2.78 SIERA in that stretch suggest he would have been quite good even with neutral luck. Beating $10MM should be doable but his age won’t let him get too far past that number. Perhaps he would decide to stay in Cincinnati and skip the headache of the lockout.
Club Options
Anderson spent 2022 to 2025 pitching in Asia. He was able to secure a $7MM deal with the Tigers for 2026. He started in a long relief role but got a rotation gig after Tarik Skubal and Casey Mize were traded at the deadline. On the whole, he tossed 108 innings with a 3.67 ERA, 25% strikeout rate, 9.4% walk rate and 48.4% ground ball rate. He had a 2.61 ERA as a starter and a 4.63 ERA as a reliever but those are misleading numbers. From the rotation, his 22.9% strikeout rate and 11% walk rate were lackluster. A .246 BABIP and 88.6% strand rate were the main reasons for his success. As a reliever, his 26.8% strikeout rate and 8.1% walk rate were far more impressive but his .313 BABIP was to the unlucky side. This is one of the most borderline option decisions of the year. MLBTR’s Anthony Franco took a more detailed look at Anderson earlier this week.
Clase would be a bargain at this price based on talent but he hasn’t pitched since July of 2025 due to a gambling/pitch-fixing investigation. It’s unknown if he’ll ever pitch in the majors again. He hasn’t been collecting salary since he was put on the restricted list. It’s unclear if the Guardians would have to pay the buyout if they turn down this option.
This is an easy call for the Royals. Estévez only made one appearance this year and it wasn’t good. He allowed six earned runs in a third of an inning. He then landed on the IL and never came back. He had shoulder surgery in September and has an uncertain timeline. Even before this injury-marred year, his strikeout rate was trending down, going from 27.8% in 2023 to 23.6% and 20.1% in the next two seasons. He’ll be back in free agency soon. His market will depend on his surgery recovery.
Jansen signed an $11MM deal with the Tigers last winter, in the form of a $9MM salary and $2MM buyout on a $12MM club option. It’s now a net $10MM decision for Detroit. Jansen had another good year, in a sense, posting a 3.20 ERA. However, it feels like he’s walking a tightrope, as he has had a BABIP below .200 in two straight years now. If some more balls find grass, things could turn quickly. He had a 4.46 FIP and 3.96 SIERA this year. The Tigers may want to move on but it’s also possible Jansen could be traded at this price point.
Muñoz had a down year if you look at his 4.24 ERA. However, a big home run spike played a role in that. He allowed nine this year, after only allowing ten over the previous three seasons combined. His 37.1% strikeout rate was his best since 2022. His 10% walk rate was a bit above average but normal for him. His 49.6% ground ball rate was lower than his previous seasons but still better than average. His 3.34 FIP and 2.41 SIERA suggest he’s still a lockdown closer. A pitcher this good can get paid even coming off a down year. As mentioned earlier, Helsley got $28MM over two years with an opt-out. Muñoz is still a bargain at $9MM, especially since there’s another club option for 2028.
Rea has been a solid swingman for four straight years now. This time a year ago, the Cubs were deciding on his club option but just worked out a new deal instead, giving him a slight bump on his guarantee. He went on to post a 4.34 ERA over 19 starts and 15 relief appearances, a pretty similar season to 2025. The deal feels about right for Rea’s value, so it’s a borderline call. The Cubs need arms and could bring him back. If they want to change things up, they could buy him out or perhaps trade him to another club.
Senzatela’s deal was signed way back when he was a starter. He’s now a reliever and had a decent season, though it hasn’t trended in the right direction. He gave the Rockies 52 1/3 innings with a 3.61 ERA. He was traded to the Brewers and posted a 5.14 ERA after the deal, though he did crack Milwaukee’s playoff roster and has made two scoreless appearances so far. It’s been a good year but the option price is usually reserved for elite relievers. Senzatela doesn’t qualify as he only struck out 19.3% of opponents this year. He should be able to get a major league deal but with a salary lower than this.
Stanek is basically the same pitcher he was a year ago. Despite strong velocity, his strikeout rates have always been good but not great, as his walk rates have been too high. This year, he had a 4.02 ERA. His 24.3% strikeout rate was just a bit above average but his 12% walk rate was a few ticks worse than par. He got a $3.5MM guarantee from the Cards last winter. Since his profile is the same but he’s a year older, his earning power should be about the same or slightly less. The option should therefore be bought out.
Stephan put up some good numbers for the Guards from 2021-23. Tommy John surgery wiped out his 2024 and he hasn’t been able to get back on track. In 2025-26, the Guardians kept him in the minors. He threw 48 2/3 innings on the farm in that time with a 9.99 ERA. His fastball averaged 92.8 mph in Triple-A this year, almost four miles below the 96.5 mph he averaged in 2022. Stephan is clearly going to be bought out.
The Angels gave Stephenson a three-year, $33MM deal going into 2024 but injuries basically wiped out that whole contract. He missed all of 2024, made ten appearances in 2025, and then missed all of 2026. The one upside for the Angels is that all the time missed unlocked this club option for them. But after so much missed time, they might prefer to just walk away. Stephenson had elbow surgery in April, a ligament and flexor repair surgery. It’s unknown what his expected timeline is from here.
Webb continues to put up good results but the market keeps spitting on him. He has thrown 320 career innings with a 3.07 ERA, 23.6% strikeout rate, 9.8% walk rate and 36.6% ground ball rate. His 2026 numbers were all fairly close to that. The Orioles non-tendered him going into 2025 instead of paying Webb a projected $1.7MM arbitration salary. The Rangers scooped him up, got a good season out of him but wouldn’t pay him a projected $2MM arb salary for 2026. $2.5MM feels like a fine price for a guy with Webb’s skills but the precedent suggests he could be sent back to the market.
Weiss has a nice showing in the KBO over a couple of seasons and the Astros thought he could be a low-cost rotation upgrade for them. They gave him a $2.6MM guarantee to cross the Pacific and return to affiliated ball. It didn’t work out. Weiss posted a 7.62 ERA in 26 innings before being outrighted. He had a 5.42 ERA in Triple-A. General manager Dana Brown is now gone. It’s not exactly clear who will be making these decisions for the Astros but it should be an easy call to walk away from this option.
This is one of the easiest decisions, as the Sox will definitely pick this up. UCL surgery wiped out most of his 2024 but Whitlock has a 2.03 ERA in 124 1/3 innings since coming back. His strikeout, walk and ground ball rates are all better than average. He has 50 holds and five saves over the past two seasons. There’s another club option for 2028, which only enhances the appeal of picking this one up.
Wick had good seasons in Japan in 2024 and 2025 but required Tommy John surgery in November of 2025. The Giants signed him knowing he would miss all of 2026, hoping that Wick could help them in 2027. His health status is unclear. The option price is barely above this year’s league minimum. Next year’s league minimum is still to be determined. Unless Wick’s health outlook is worse than expected, the Giants will presumably pick this up as planned.
Minor League Deal/Reclamation Types
Photo courtesy of Brad Penner, Imagn Images

69,2026-10-14,"Exports grew by 36.3 percent in September, the biggest leap since the COVID-19 pandemic.
Save
Share
China’s exports surged by more than one-third in September, a boost to the world’s second-largest economy as it faces slowing growth.
Exports grew by 36.3 percent last month year-on-year to reach $447.18bn, accelerating from a 25 percent gain in August, government figures released on Wednesday show.
The leap marked the biggest monthly rise in exports since 2021, when China’s trade figures were skewed by the effects of the COVID-19 pandemic and stringent lockdowns.
Tech-related trade led the growth, with semiconductor exports surging more than 108 percent and shipments of automatic data processing machines jumping nearly 55 percent.
Imports increased 31 percent year-on-year, up from 28.2 percent in August, according to the figures from the General Administration of Customs.
“High-tech demand, supported by the global and domestic AI upcycle, remained the key driver, while China’s pricing and supply-chain competitiveness in other major export categories also helped sustain the momentum,” Taylor Wang, an economist at HSBC in Hong Kong, said in a note to clients.
China’s total exports in the first nine months of the year rose 21.3 percent to $3.37 trillion, while imports gained 27.5 percent to $2.43 trillion.
The gap between exports and imports in the first three quarters gave China a trade surplus of $938.1bn.
The Association of Southeast Asian Nations (ASEAN) was China’s biggest export market by a wide margin, with exports rising more than 52 percent in September to $81.7bn.
Exports to the European Union rose 25.3 percent in September to $60.15bn, while shipments to the United States jumped 39.6 percent to $47.88bn.
After decades of relying on state-led investment and exports to power its economy, China is striving to find new avenues for growth amid a falling population, deflating property market, and weak domestic consumption, which contributes far less to gross domestic product (GDP) than in advanced economies.
China’s economy is expected to grow 4.5 percent in 2026, according to the International Monetary Fund’s latest growth outlook, which would be the slowest expansion since 1990, excluding the outlier years of the COVID pandemic.
“The strong export growth should keep China’s growth on track to achieve its growth target of 4.5-5 percent for 2026,” Lynn Song, chief economist for Greater China at ING, told Al Jazeera.
“Nonetheless, growth remains quite unbalanced, with domestic data quite soft in comparison,” Song said.
“It will be important to rebalance growth over the medium term if China is to achieve its longterm growth targets.”

70,2026-10-14,"This 1999 Volkswagen New Beetle is a Japanese-market example that has been modified with flared wheel arches, aftermarket side skirts, and a tea tray-style rear spoiler, and it was imported to the US in August 2026. The car has 65k kilometers (~40k miles) and is powered by a 2.0-liter inline-four paired with a four-speed automatic transaxle. Finished in Silver Metallic over black leather, the vehicle is equipped with 18″ five-spoke wheels, a sunroof, heated front seats, cruise control, air conditioning, and an aftermarket stereo. This New Beetle is now offered by the selling dealer at no reserve with a clean Florida title.

The car is finished in Silver Metallic (LG9R) and has been modified with aftermarket flared wheel arches, front and rear lip spoilers, side skirts, and a tea tray-style rear spoiler. Headlight and taillight covers have been fitted, and additional details include a sunroof, fog lights, power-adjustable mirrors, and projector-style headlights.

The 18″ five-spoke wheels show blemishes and are wrapped in a mix of Dunlop and Goodyear tires. Stopping power is provided by anti-lock front ventilated and rear solid discs.

The heated front seats and folding rear bench are upholstered in black leather, and the headliner has been reupholstered in black fabric. Bright trim has been added to the cabin, and additional interior appointments include a dashboard-mounted tray, cruise control, air conditioning, and an aftermarket stereo.

The steering wheel fronts a 220-km/h speedometer, a tachometer with a 6,500-rpm redline, and a fuel-level gauge. The digital odometer indicates 65k kilometers (~40k miles).

The car is equipped with a 2.0-liter inline-four, and an oil change was performed in preparation for the sale.

Power is routed to the front wheels through a four-speed automatic transaxle.
The Carfax report shows no accidents or damage, with an initial entry in August 2026.

AMD and iBUYPOWER stuff 16 Zen 5 cores and 40 compute units into a mini PC

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

iBuyPower has entered the compact AI workstation market with a $3,999 mini PC built around AMD’s Ryzen AI Max+ PRO 495 processor.
The Pro AMD AI Halo Mini PC combines 128GB of memory, integrated Radeon graphics, and is designed for professional computing and local AI workloads.
According to the company, this device measures 70 × 198 × 200 mm, runs Windows 11 Pro, and occupies relatively little desk space.
Its product images also show a leather strap against the compact enclosure, giving the system a distinctive visual detail beyond its technical specifications.
The workstation uses a 16-core Zen 5 processor, Radeon 8065S integrated graphics based on RDNA 3.5, and a neural processing unit rated at 55 TOPS.
Its graphics processor includes 40 compute units, while the system’s 128GB of LPDDR5X memory runs at 8,000 MHz for demanding shared-memory workloads.
iBuyPower says the hardware can run AI models locally, support ray tracing and interactive simulations, and handle creative, engineering and data-intensive tasks.
Sign up to the TechRadar Pro newsletter to get all the top news, opinion, features and guidance your business needs to succeed!
Connectivity includes Wi-Fi 7, Bluetooth 5.4 and two 10Gb Ethernet ports, alongside USB4 connections capable of transferring data at up to 40 Gbps.
The front panel includes two USB 3.2 Gen 2 Type A ports, a USB4 Type C connection, an audio jack and an SD card reader.
Rear connections add DisplayPort 1.4, HDMI 2.1, another USB4 port, additional USB ports, and the second Ethernet connection.
The Pro AMD AI Halo Mini PC is equipped with two 10Gb Ethernet ports, which provide fast wired networking.
However, their presence alone does not confirm that multiple units can operate together as an AI cluster.
That requires a compatible software stack and configuration for distributing model workloads across machines, details iBuyPower has not announced for this particular system.
The storage design offers three M.2 positions, including two 2280 slots and another supporting 2242 or 2280 drives, with PCIe 4.0 connectivity.
The 128GB memory capacity may appeal to developers running large models locally, although usable model size and speed will depend on software and workload demands.
We reached out to iBuyPower, and the company confirmed that the mini PC comes with an SSD. The starting configuration comes with a 1TB SSD, and it costs $3,999.
When this device launches on the company’s website, potential buyers will be able to select the capacity they’d like the system to configure.
This mobile workstation is scheduled to become available in Q4 2026, but higher capacities will incur more cost.

73,2026-10-14,"Triumph’s liquid-cooled Bonneville T120 is a powerhouse of low-end torque wrapped in a classically handsome package. It’s a great bike, but for people like us, it’s a bike that begs for certain enhancements. French workshop FCR Original caters exactly to this demographic. 
Roughly 40 custom motorcycles roll out of FCR’s Chauvigny facility every year, each embodying their mastered in-house style and loaded with custom parts from their extensive catalog. They do not hack up tired donor bikes. Instead, they supply brand-new, untouched platforms to ensure every bespoke build retains absolute reliability and a two-year manufacturer warranty. 
Their latest creation—commissioned by a client named Stéphane—strips the polite veneer off a fresh T120 Black to expose a sharper, significantly more aggressive scrambler underneath.
FCR kicked things off by reworking the rear subframe to tighten the proportions while retaining the structural integrity needed for the occasional passenger. The new saddle is trimmed in a rich black faux leather, and the original Triumph passenger pegs were retained. Digging through their catalog, FCR ditched the Bonneville’s fenders for proprietary aluminum mudguards, complete with a Bates taillight and license plate holder. 
Moving up front, FCR rebuilt the factory forks and treated the stanchions to a black Diamond-Like Carbon (DLC) coating. This isn't merely a cosmetic blackout; DLC drastically reduces stiction to improve small-bump compliance under heavy braking. The front end is secured by a modified satin black top yoke with a Bates headlight, while fully adjustable Shock Factory 2Win dampers cure the factory pogo effect out back.
To shed unsprung weight, FCR laced up a set of custom black rims utilizing an 18-inch front hoop and a wider 17-inch rear. Pirelli Scorpion Rally STR rubber is the thinking builder’s dual-sport tire, as it’s aggressive enough in the dirt but engineered with a continuous center block for high-speed tarmac stability. Plus, it just has the right look. Stopping duties get a vital upgrade via three Moto-Master Wave rotors, improving heat dissipation and initial bite over the OE Triumph discs.
The cockpit highlights FCR’s electrical expertise. Ditching the bulky factory dials and switchgear requires serious integration to keep the modern CAN bus system happy. FCR successfully wired in a Motogadget Tiny speedometer on a bespoke bracket, alongside Motone two-by-three button blocks without triggering a dash full of error codes. They also dropped the factory one-inch bars for a 22-millimeter black bobber handlebar fitted with Dakar grips, Motogadget mo.view mirrors and mo.blaze bar-end indicators to keep everything seamless.
The 1200 cc parallel-twin remains internally stock but breathes through a custom FCR Original exhaust. It’s slim and much shorter than the factory setup, so it won’t interfere when things get rough. A proprietary engine guard protects the sump while SW-Motech footpegs replace the slippery stock rubber units.
Visually, the build sticks rigidly to Stéphane’s brief: “understated at first glance, yet rich in detail.” To accomplish that with an all-black machine, varying shades are your best friend. The fuel tank and mudguards wear deep gloss black, contrasting sharply against the satin side panels and gunmetal grey FCR logos. A yellow PIAA LP530 spot lamp mounted to the right-side crash bar intentionally breaks the blackout rule and reinforces the bike’s newfound purpose.
Ultimately, this T120 is not a radical, grinder-heavy reinvention. It is a highly calculated, purposeful restyling that fundamentally alters how the machine feels and presents. This exact formula is how FCR Original has carved out its niche in a saturated custom market. By executing a comprehensive aesthetic and performance overhaul without compromising the core architecture, they deliver a tailored build you would choose over a stock Bonneville every single day. 
Best of all, this dark scrambler rolls out of the Chauvigny workshop retaining its full manufacturer warranty. And for a custom that’s built to be enjoyed, what could be better than that? 
FCR Original | Facebook | Instagram | Photos by FCR Original

74,2026-10-14,"Foot-and-mouth disease threatens livestock farmers’ livelihoods and disrupts meat supplies as authorities impose curbs.
Save
Share
Windhoek, Namibia – “We had to close the market for several days. It was just before the weekend and month’s end. That’s when we need the money the most,” says Francisco, a vendor at Single Quarter, Windhoek’s largest open meat market.
He pours vinegar and cooking oil into a tomato and onion salsa, a traditional accompaniment to kapana, a popular grilled-meat street food. Around him, many stalls and grilling stations sit empty. Instead of the crowds that typically fill the market during business hours, only a handful of customers move between the stalls.




Recent closures and concerns about meat supplies have disrupted business.
“Our business is slow. We are struggling to buy meat, but without it, we cannot survive,” says another kapana vendor, who asked to remain anonymous. “This is our livelihood. The meat is much more expensive now, so we are making less money altogether. If this continues for three months, it is going to be very bad for us.”
In Katutura, Windhoek’s largest township, uncertainty over beef supplies is adding to residents’ financial concerns.
“If we don’t eat meat, what are we going to eat? We are Namibians,” one resident says, describing meat as a staple of the local diet. Another worries about rising prices and the wider consequences for Namibia, where livestock exports are an important part of the economy.
At a supermarket in the Okuryangava suburb, the butchery has already run out of some premium beef products. Store manager Enrico De Azevedo, 23, says consumer behaviour has also contributed to the pressure.
“We saw people panic-buying like they did during the COVID-19 pandemic with toilet paper. Now, they can go and stack their meat next to their toilet paper,” he says.
De Azevedo also recognises the wider economic risks. “For those in the export business, this will have grave implications.”
Namibia’s Directorate of Veterinary Services (DVS) confirmed an outbreak of foot-and-mouth disease (FMD) on a commercial farm in the Karasburg State Veterinary District, in the southern //Kharas Region, on September 23, 2026.
In an address on October 5, President Netumbo Nandi-Ndaitwah said surveillance teams had recorded 127 positive cases across 15 farms. Teams had inspected 29,313 animals across 68 farms. The government had also established 20 surveillance teams and 10 roadblocks in the region as part of its response.
In an update covering the situation as of October 6, the Ministry of Agriculture, Fisheries, Water and Land Reform reported that 129 animal samples had tested positive and 17 farms had been confirmed infected. Veterinary teams had inspected 72 of 81 targeted farms.
The outbreak was subsequently confirmed in a second region. On October 7, laboratory tests confirmed FMD in cattle at Kalala Island, in the Kabbe constituency of the Zambezi Region, in northern Namibia.
Veterinary officials from the Katima Mulilo State Veterinary Office investigated the suspected outbreak on October 5 after observing clinical signs in 20 cattle. Twelve samples were collected and submitted to the Central Veterinary Laboratory in Windhoek on October 6. All 12 tested positive for the virus the following day.
In a public notification issued on October 10, the ministry outlined restrictions across the Zambezi Region following confirmation of the outbreak.
These include suspending the movement of cloven-hoofed animals and their raw products, cancelling previously issued movement permits, and halting livestock auctions, informal markets and other animal-gathering events. Slaughter at local abattoirs, informal markets and social events has also been suspended.
Slaughter at export abattoirs may continue for eligible cattle under the commodity-based trade system, provided the animals meet quarantine requirements and pass traceability checks.
Emergency vaccination has begun in the affected area, while roadblocks and intensive surveillance are intended to prevent further spread.
FMD is a highly contagious viral disease affecting cloven-hoofed animals, including cattle, sheep, goats and pigs. It can disrupt livestock production and trade, particularly when it affects areas recognised as free of the disease.
Following confirmation of the //Kharas outbreak, Namibian authorities notified the World Organisation for Animal Health (WOAH) and introduced restrictions on the movement and export of cloven-hoofed animals and their products.
The European Union subsequently amended its import rules for Namibia’s previously authorised export zone, known as NA-1, following the outbreak.
The Zambezi detection has added to the challenge facing veterinary authorities, who are working to contain the disease and prevent further infections. The available information confirms outbreaks in both regions but does not establish that the disease spread directly from //Kharas to Zambezi.
“The outbreak has been a heavy blow to our country,” Marianne Pretorius, head of communications at the Namibia Agricultural Union (NAU), told Al Jazeera by email. “The restrictions are painful, but they are necessary, and the NAU supports them.”
On October 5, the European Union announced a temporary suspension of entry into the bloc of specified fresh meat products from cattle, sheep and goats, as well as certain products from other farmed and wild hoofed animals, from Namibia’s previously authorised export zone. The restrictions also cover specified processed meat products, including biltong and jerky.
The measure is not a blanket suspension of all Namibian meat imports. According to the EU, certain processed products that undergo the prescribed risk-mitigating treatment remain eligible for entry. Qualifying fresh-meat consignments certified by Namibian authorities before September 23 may also enter during a 90-day transitional period, subject to the applicable EU rules and border-control requirements.
The restrictions apply to the specific products covered by the EU measure. It remains unclear whether the Zambezi outbreak will affect exports to other markets or under other trade arrangements.
For the country’s livestock industry, the restrictions add to the uncertainty created by domestic movement controls and the loss of access to some export markets.
“For farmers in the //Kharas region, the impact is immediate and severe, but this is not only a //Kharas problem,” Pretorius said. “When export markets close, every livestock producer in the country feels it.”
She warned that the consequences could continue even after domestic trading resumes.
“Even when domestic trade resumes, prices may remain under pressure until export markets reopen,” she wrote. “That is why we are calling for market recovery to be treated with the same determination as disease control.”
Namibia has stepped up efforts to contain the disease, including plans to strengthen veterinary fencing in high-risk areas.
Construction of a veterinary cordon fence along the Orange River in the //Kharas Region began in October. The barrier is intended to strengthen controls along the border with South Africa. The government has also pursued additional fencing and containment measures in high-risk areas.
The containment measures are intended to limit animal movement and reduce the risk of further infections. Their effectiveness will depend on surveillance, compliance with movement restrictions and the ability of veterinary authorities to identify and contain new cases.
The outbreak is also affecting the country’s meat-processing sector. The Namibian reported on October 12 that Meat Corporation of Namibia (Meatco) had placed about 400 production workers on three weeks of special leave with full pay. The situation was due to be reviewed on October 23, with workers’ return depending on guidance from veterinary authorities.
The Namibian also reported that Meatco had paid farmers N$31 million (about US$1.8 million) for cattle delivered to its Windhoek abattoir between August 29 and September 11. The payments cover cattle already delivered, while uncertainty remains over future sales and export access.
For livestock producers, the outbreak threatens immediate income and the longer-term viability of their businesses. Restrictions on moving and selling animals can interrupt cash flow even for farmers whose herds have not been infected.
Gert Engelbrecht, a 40-year-old farmer from Aranos, said the restrictions had created financial and emotional strain for producers.
“Our livestock is not simply a business to us; it is our livelihood and something we have invested our lives in,” he told Al Jazeera. “Being able to move and sell livestock is fundamental to keeping the farming operation going. It affects farmers across Namibia when movement and markets are restricted. Ultimately, we need to regain our disease-free status.”
The DVS has intensified surveillance and is tracing livestock movements that took place before the outbreaks were detected.
For farmers, restoring market access is essential to keeping their operations running. Meat vendors, meanwhile, depend on reliable supplies to sustain their businesses.
“Farmers are fighters,” Engelbrecht said. “We understand why restrictions are necessary, but we are not sitting back and waiting for somebody else to solve this. We are working with the veterinary authorities to see this disease controlled, regain our status, and restore access to our markets.”
