        Amazon, Google, Meta among members of lobby group funding dire warnings over cloud sovereignty plans
    
Some of America's biggest tech companies have a warning for Europe: curb your reliance on US cloud providers, and it could cost you €115 billion.
That's the finding of a new study commissioned by CCIA Europe, a lobbying group whose members include Amazon, Google, and Meta, companies that have plenty of reasons to want European governments to keep buying American cloud services.
The research, conducted by the European Centre for International Political Economy (ECIPE), claims the European Commission's proposed Cloud and AI Development Act (CADA) could saddle Europe with €115 billion in costs over the next decade, more than six times the Commission's upper estimate of €18 billion.
The report argues that restrictions favoring European cloud providers would leave governments and businesses facing hefty bills to migrate workloads and rewrite software. It puts direct public-sector costs at €33.3 billion, with a further €80 billion potentially falling on private companies in sectors including banking, healthcare, energy, and transport.
However, a rather large asterisk is attached to that €80 billion figure. It assumes the Commission extends sovereignty requirements beyond public procurement to cover 25 percent of cloud workloads in essential private-sector industries. In other words, it's a modeled scenario, not a bill Brussels has already handed European businesses.
The study also comes with some baggage of its own. CCIA represents several US technology giants that dominate the European cloud market, including Amazon and Google. Both have spent years selling European customers cloud services while more recently developing sovereign cloud offerings designed to address concerns about foreign ownership, data access, and regulatory control.
Amazon, for example, has been developing its AWS European Sovereign Cloud, while Google markets a range of sovereign cloud solutions. Apparently, sovereignty is an attractive proposition when it comes with a hyperscaler logo attached. Meta, another CCIA member, has its own interests in European AI infrastructure, although it is not a commercial cloud provider on the scale of AWS or Google Cloud.
The report specifically targets Article 31 of the proposed legislation, which it says could allow the Commission to extend sovereignty requirements into essential private-sector services. CCIA argues that forcing businesses to move away from existing cloud infrastructure could trigger expensive software redevelopment projects, with code rewriting and re-architecting accounting for 69 percent of its estimated costs.
Luxembourg would apparently take the biggest hit, with the study putting the cost at €1,711 per worker over ten years. Denmark (€905), Austria (€900), and Finland (€864) also face hefty bills, as does Ireland (€856), which hosts the European headquarters of several US tech giants.
CCIA is urging EU governments to overhaul the proposed legislation, limit sovereignty restrictions to sensitive workloads, and abandon requirements based on corporate origin or personnel nationality in favor of what it calls risk-based technical safeguards.
""Member States cannot rely on the Commission's incomplete estimates when deciding CADA's strict sovereignty requirements for cloud and AI providers,"" said Mitchell Rutledge, CCIA Europe's policy manager. ""Taxpayers and businesses will foot the bill, yet the official assessment missed 70 percent of CADA's true impact.""
For AWS and Google, offering European customers greater control over their data also helps retain business that might otherwise migrate to European competitors. It's a rather convenient arrangement: sell customers sovereignty on one hand, while an industry association representing your interests warns governments against imposing too much of it on the other.
Neither company responded to The Register's questions. 
Whether Europe's sovereignty ambitions will prove as expensive as CCIA predicts is unclear. What's easier to establish is that the companies warning Brussels about the cost of reducing its dependence on US technology have plenty to gain if Europe decides to keep buying American. ®

76,2026-10-14,"TOPICS

                    columnists
                

        New models arrive so fast, we already have more new tech than it's possible to understand in a career
    
Why are we in such a hurry? Are we in a race? Where's the finish line? Are we in the lead? Far behind?
I increasingly hear these questions about AI from people who feel a gritty sense of being unpleasantly overstimulated – like a three-year-old allowed to stay awake too long and now unable to sleep.
That feeling has become worse since last November when AI tools suddenly became ""good enough"" to be broadly useful. A whole cohort of engineers – myself included – found themselves infused with manic energy, bursting with ideas on how to prompt up tools and processes with this wonderful new toy.
Usage exploded – as has revenue. The AI firms, finally on a path to something vaguely suggestive of profitability, leaned in and increased their release cadence. Every new model felt like Christmas.
We've had a lot of Christmases these past ten months or so.
Almost every engineer I know has accrued a massive ""toy debt,"" unable to stop experimenting with more tools than any of us could ever hope to master across an entire career.
Each new model delivers a quick dopamine hit to users, but the high wears off and the slump arrives.
That's where we are now. Engineers feel exhausted. The race to ""Super Intelligence"" (whatever that is) left us with something else entirely: call it ""stupor intelligence"" – the feeling that we've absorbed more than we can digest. More AI would just be pointless excess.
If that's the view from inside the AI bubble, how does it look on the outside? Not so great. The Wall Street Journal confidently reports that 63 percent of Americans favor an immediate pause or slowdown in AI development. They can see this technology is moving too fast, in too manic a rush, and without control. It's making them uneasy.
That shapeless unease takes form as a widespread anxiety that the machines will soon rise up to kill us all. It's reflected within the industry as people spectacularly burn out, posting long screeds about the imminent human extinction event. Former Anthropic staffer Jacob Coxon's resignation post went viral because it gave folks a focus for their anxieties.
Would it kill us to pause and catch our breath?
Here's the raw truth: businesses already have more technological change to digest than they can work through for years to come. We've a wealth of ""good enough"" AI – American, Chinese, and European – to completely transform business operations. That will happen, but on a timescale of years, not days.
Bringing new, better, shinier AI models to market has become a project of diminishing returns. Not because the models have plateaued, but because the culture has. We simply can't process any more AI.
So how do we stop? The profitable loop between the model makers and their customers won't pause on its own. It will only go faster and harder. At the same time, we're seeing the stirrings of a Luddite 2.0 movement, intent on destroying the machines before they replace all the workers.
Resistance to datacenters is the friendly face of something that could turn ugly if we simply keep charging ahead.
One thing could slow us: the cost of keeping these systems under control.
The smarter models become, the more difficult they are to manage. Every day brings more reports of ""rogue agents"" reaching external systems from labs that seem to have avoided the hard work of designing secure test environments. More powerful models could prove increasingly costly to contain. Eventually, costs outweigh benefits. We could be one lawsuit away from reaching a pause, when the frontier labs and their products become uninsurable risks.
Here's hoping we pause before some real mischief occurs. Otherwise, plugs may be pulled, and all the pretty Christmas lights will go out. Better by far to act with agency, clear-eyed and humble, than confront force majeure. ®

Polymarket brought on Academy Award-winning director Kathryn Bigelow, known for The Hurt Locker and Zero Dark Thirty, to helm two ads in its new national campaign, which debuts today (Oct. 14), three weeks before Election Day. It’s another milestone for “The History of Concrete.” The off-beat documentary about… well, the title says it all, has crossed the $2 million mark 25 days into its run. That’s a strong result for a non-fiction film, as well as a testament to the appeal of its director John Wilson, who has built a passionate following thanks to his cult favorite HBO series “How To with John Wilson.”
Magnolia, the indie studio behind the documentary, picked up the film at this year’s Sundance Film Festival, where it was a hit with critics. The total gross now stands at $2,042,000. “The History of Concrete” is currently playing on 172 screens in North America, which includes 11 screens in Canada. It will shed some venues this weekend, dropping to 125 screens. 

 









The film, which touches on issues of urbanism and architecture with Wilson’s absurdist flair, has been a huge hit at the IFC Center, surpassing $300,000, making it one of the indie theater’s highest-grossing titles ever.
“‘The History of Concrete’ has worked in every market we’ve opened,” said Neal Block, Magnolia’s head of distribution and marketing. “I don’t think in my 20 years at Magnolia that I’ve ever received more emails from exhibitors expressing delight at how a film is performing, and asking to book return engagements and encore screenings. It’s not playing like a documentary; it’s not playing like a comedy — it’s sort of charting its own path theatrically, since it’s very much its own singular piece of art.”

 









“The History of Concrete” also picked up four Critic’s Choice nominations this week, including nods for best documentary feature and best first documentary feature. It was also nominated for its editing and narration.

Most of us have that one friend who always talks too much about themselves. In many cases, we might actually, unfortunately, be that friend. Research shows that around 40% of a person’s speech is about themselves. And while that can make us seem quite narcissistic, it doesn’t mean we don’t listen to what our friends say.

This person, however, was accused by their friends of focusing too much on themselves during their latest catch-up sesh. Apparently, the person talked too much about themselves and asked too little about their daughter and parenthood. The parents even timed it with their nanny cam, and once the friend found out, they went online to ask whether this is a normal part of friendship or a violation of boundaries and privacy.

Getty Images / Unsplash (not the actual photo)






Drazen Zigic / magnific (not the actual photo)






etonastenka / magnific (not the actual photo)





Stock-Act1304

anna_grant / magnific (not the actual photo)
Who hasn’t met a person who likes the sound of their voice a little bit too much? Of course, we can’t know if OP is really that kind of person without hearing what both sides of this argument have to say.
It’s nice when the conversation revolves around you, when people are engaged in what you’re saying, and want to hear more of what you think. The same researchers who found that around 40% of a person’s speech is about themselves also noticed that answering questions about oneself floods our brains with dopamine. So, it’s pleasant when someone is interested in you during a conversation, and it’s similar to having a good meal, receiving money, and even moments of intimacy.
However, it is true that some people are more narcissistic than others when it comes to conversations. These types of people are actually referred to as conversational narcissists. Sometimes, they might dominate the conversation without realizing they’re doing it. Other times, their goal might be to manipulate, gaslight, or dismiss the other person.
There are different reasons why some people are more narcissistic during conversations than others. As psychotherapist Leanna Stockard, LMFT, explained to VeryWell Mind, there are four main ones:
In the case of the first three, the person might not even notice they’re doing it. This might be the case for the OP — perhaps they tend to talk about themselves more than they realize. However, some people are just plainly ego-driven and think that what they have to say is more interesting and valuable than their friends’.

DC Studio / magnific (not the actual photo)
Even those who are conversational narcissists can change their ways. It’s not a medical diagnosis — it’s just a tendency to talk over people, listen to them less, and not engage during conversations. That, of course, doesn’t feel great for friends, but there are ways conversational narcissists can be more present and engaged in conversations and become better listeners.
Experts say that self-reflection is the number one step: realizing that you’re taking over conversations and not participating in them the way your friends might want you to. “Overcoming these patterns starts with building a person’s own emotional literacy and then utilizing skills to help them find a deeper connection within interactions with others,” Hannah Schlueter, MA, LAC, explains.
Conversational narcissists need to hone their active listening skills. That means listening to what a person is saying and repeating back what they heard, and only offering their perspective once the person is done.
Non-verbal cues are also important. Words aren’t the only way of communicating between people, so looking for microexpressions, a frown, a smile, and different breathing patterns can also be helpful when engaging in a conversation.
If you know you tend to hijack conversations and are bad at spotting non-verbal cues and active listening, try preparing questions and topics before meeting up with a friend. “Before meeting up with people, go in with a mindset and intention to not talk about yourself, to not share a story where you’ve experienced something similar, or share your perspective or opinion unless the other person asks,” Stockard added for VeryWell Mind.

80,2026-10-14,"At a Lucia end.
Cyberleek, the leaker who's been releasing footage from Grand Theft Auto 6 for weeks now, is seemingly done posting leaks. Cyberleek released a new - seemingly final - video today which supposedly shows parts of the game's ending.
The leaker has indicated they may be done sharing footage as their goal of pumping their crypto coin had failed. Cyberleek recently said they'd release a 200GB build of the game if the coin hits $30m market cap, which it's nowhere near doing.
The coin has crashed in recent weeks after a major bump that coincided with the release of leaked footage showing co-protagonist Lucia fully naked, shortly after they released footage of co-protagonist Jason also nude.
Eurogamer has seen the latest footage, and it's not as dramatic as Cyberleek initially made it out to be. The footage does not appear to be of the game's ending sequence or final cutscene, though the location of where the action takes place could be a spoiler. The four-minute video ends with a montage of various locations and flyover footage of a city, set to Journey's Don't Stop Believin'.
There may be something there, but when viewed without proper context, it's hard to say what's actually being spoiled. Still, the footage is best avoided if you want to jump into the game fresh.
""The 30m was not reached, so no build,"" Cyberleek reportedly said in a message posted on their website. ""Uploading 200+ GB anonymously is a serious risk - worth it only if the milestone funded the secret project.""
At the time of writing, Cyberleek's memecoin's market cap has dropped to $1.2m just three days after reaching its peak of $7.8m. It also seems they've abandoned the initial plan to tie certain gameplay leaks to the coin's market cap.
""The leeks stop here. For now,"" Cyberleek allegedly wrote, though it's worth keeping in mind that the leaker reappeared last week after going to ground following their initial leaks in August, seemingly in response to Rockstar parent company Take-Two initiating a massive investigation and issuing subpoenas to Discord and Microsoft.
Given recent history, Cyberleek could simply re-emerge yet again with a new scam and more footage. GTA 6's 19th November official launch is not far off, however, so their window is closing.

14 October 2026


AT&T

AT&T has been one of Mexico's biggest carriers for over a decade, but that's about to come to an end. The company has announced that it has reached an agreement to sell AT&T Mexico to Grupo Televisa and Fintech Holdings for $2 billion in cash at closing, ""subject to certain adjustments"", plus ""additional contractual and contingent benefits"".
The buyer is a consortium owned 50/50 by Televisa and Fintech, which plans to pay with a combination of cash on hand and debt financing. What it's getting is Mexico's second-largest mobile operator, with about 24.7 million wireless subscribers at the end of last year. Televisa already has a fixed-line broadband business in the country, so the deal will let it offer mobile service alongside that.
AT&T got into Mexico back in 2015, when it bought Iusacell and Nextel Mexico and merged them under the AT&T brand. Now, it says the sale is part of its long-term strategy of focusing its spending on growth opportunities in what it calls the ""vibrant US market"" - which in practice means fiber and wireless networks at home. CEO John Stankey also took the opportunity to point out the company's belief that the AI future will run on converged networks and dense fiber.
The transaction is expected to close in the first quarter of 2027, subject to regulatory approvals in Mexico. If you're an AT&T customer in the US, the company says nothing will change for you.
Source

83,2026-10-14,"Not-Ship
Charting the age of uncertainty.
Amanda Shendruk
Not-Ship turns one next week!! To mark it, I'll be kicking off a campaign for new members. I won't spam you; free subscribers will only get three extra emails on top of the weekly edition. 
But first, can I ask for a small birthday present? Hit reply and finish this: I read Not-Ship because...
💙 Amanda
Last week I wrote about how Amazon's search page is designed to screw you over: the top row trap, the swamp of ads, the discount-washing. Today, I'll explain what's going on behind the scenes, and how Amazon's rules make sure prices stay high not just at Amazon, but everywhere you shop.
In a nutshell, here's what's happening:
This isn't just my theory. This system is at the heart of the US Federal Trade Commission's (FTC) upcoming antitrust case against Amazon.
Let's get into it.
Amazon relies on independent sellers to populate its online marketplace, and it charges them to be on the site. Amazon takes a big cut. In 2014, this was about 19% of sales. But today, many sellers hand close to half of every sale to Amazon. Half!
Amazon's basic commission (15% of the sale price in most categories) is in line with what eBay and Walmart charge. What pushes Amazon's cut higher are the other fees stacked on top.
Based on an apparel seller that opts for Amazon fulfillment, storage, returns and advertising.
There's the referral fee, which is the commission Amazon takes on every sale. The fulfillment fee is the cost to have Amazon pack and ship items from its warehouses. Then there's advertising: those sponsored spots in the top row we saw in last week's article.
Of these costs, only the referral fee is mandatory. Sellers can choose to ship orders themselves, for example, or forgo advertising. But in practice, the choice isn't actually much of a choice. 
Here's what it looks like for a seller considering these so-called options:
In other words, the real choice for sellers isn't between paying for extras or not; it's between making sales or not. The FTC calls Prime eligibility, which effectively requires Amazon's fulfillment service, ""a virtual necessity for doing business on Amazon."" It says the same of advertising. 
Here's where the fees are passed on to us consumers. When sellers give roughly half of their sales to Amazon, the other half isn't profit. That money has to cover making the product, shipping it to Amazon's warehouses, paying staff and everything else. So even if they wanted to absorb Amazon's fees on your behalf, most companies can't afford to. They have to raise their prices.
Don't forget, Amazon's biggest single fee — the referral fee — is a percentage of the price. So when prices go up, Amazon's cut goes up, too.
Now here's where it gets really crazy. Amazon's Fair Pricing Policy lets it remove the Buy Box (the ""Add to Cart"" button), or even suspend a seller, if they're offering their item cheaper elsewhere. So, a seller who raises prices on Amazon to cover the fees has to raise them everywhere else too — even on their own website! — or risk losing their Amazon sales.
The consequence is clear: We pay Amazon prices, even when we're not shopping on Amazon.
It's been documented again and again. In 2024, Amazon sellers told CNBC they lost the Buy Box after Target put their products on sale. In an ongoing lawsuit, California's attorney general alleges Amazon pushed brands to get Walmart, Target and other retailers to raise their prices. And when progressive media outlet More Perfect Union reported on this, they even found an Amazon seller who was suspended over a one-cent price gap with Target. 
Ideally, market competition would sort this out. High prices send shoppers elsewhere and force fees down. But when sellers can't charge less anywhere else, there's nowhere cheaper to go. 
For sellers, this is frustrating. So, why don't they just leave? Because Amazon is where the shoppers are: it accounts for about 40% of all US online retail sales. For many sellers, not being on Amazon isn't an option.
At least regulators have noticed. In 2023, the FTC and 17 US states sued Amazon, arguing that it illegally maintains its monopoly power by, among other things, punishing sellers who offer lower prices elsewhere, effectively forcing them to use its warehouses and charging ever-higher fees. Amazon denies any wrongdoing, and the case is set to go to trial next year. In Europe, Germany ordered Amazon to stop controlling its sellers' prices earlier this year, though the decision isn't yet final.
After last week's piece, a Not-Ship reader wrote to say none of this is new: plenty of online stores push ads and fake discounts. That is true. But what's different about Amazon is its reach. When prices increase on Amazon, they tend to increase elsewhere too. You can, of course, choose not to shop on Amazon. But if the FTC is right, you may be paying Amazon's prices anyway.
Not-Ship's data dispatch. In your inbox, weekly. 
No spam. Just lots of charts. Unsubscribe anytime.
I spent two years working at the Washington Post, which Amazon founder Jeff Bezos owns. During that time, he was busy dismantling the publication, so he wasn't well loved in the newsroom.
In August, I saw Cory Doctorow speak at the Edinburgh International Book Festival. His enshittification concept — the process by which online platforms shift from serving their users to squeezing them — inspired this two-part series on Amazon. If you want to understand it all a lot better, I highly recommend his book Enshittification: Why Everything Suddenly Got Worse and What to Do About It. Somehow it makes antitrust issues both accessible and entertaining! 
Here's what I found interesting, important or delightful this week:
Life after death? Alvin Chang's latest masterpiece has dropped! It’s about what people around the world believe happens after we die, drawn from surveys of nearly 130,000 people in 23 countries. (Proud to count Alvin as a Not-Ship subscriber 💙.) 
Stunning embroidery. If you haven't seen the work of Irene Saputra, get yourself over to Instagram. Endlessly inspirational.
Instant knowledge. The Ars Notoria was a medieval magic book that promised scholars university learning through prayers, rituals and diagrams. No studying required!

Is this a bad omen for the U.S. gamers?

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

A Redditor has shared a photograph of the prices of Nvidia RTX 50-series GPUs in Japan, and it doesn’t bode well for gamers. According to u/Chaise91’s post on the r/pcmasterrace subreddit, retail prices for some of the best gaming GPUs are somewhere between 5.66% and 41.1% higher compared to Amazon U.S. listings.
The memory chip shortage and massive demand for AI compute have been affecting the supply of gaming GPUs, with reports suggesting Nvidia is planning to halt RTX 5090 production in favor of AI data center and professional GPUs. Because of this, the supply of high-end GPUs has vanished online, with third-party sellers now demanding almost $10,000 if you want to buy one. The image shared on the social media platform does not show the RTX 5090, so we fear that retailers in the market no longer have the high-end graphics card in stock, leading to supply pressure on the lower-tier cards.
We built a table comparing the prices from the Japanese retailer to listings we found on Amazon based on the current exchange rate of JPY 153.38 for every USD 1, and the hikes vary between 12.71% on average for the RTX 5080 to a jaw-dropping 41.10% for the RTX 5060 Ti 8GB. Note that these are pre-tax prices, though, so your mileage may vary depending on which state you live in. We added a representative 8.5% tax rate to see the price difference for gamers in states with high sales taxes, and the difference falls between 3.88% and 30.04%.

While the Japanese market is typically more expensive compared to the U.S., especially in GPU pricing, you would still feel the price hikes on these graphics cards, especially for the mainstream RTX 5070 Ti and below. While this may be bad news for Japanese gamers, it could also serve as a harbinger for the United States, as these pricing trends tend to travel westward. We saw this late last year, when Japanese shops started limiting SSD, HDD, and RAM purchases due to shortages, and GPUs with 16GB VRAM and up had to be rationed because resupply shipments failed to arrive on time.
These price increases are indeed plausible given the chip shortage pummeling the PC gaming industry, but we’re hoping that this is just a fluke in the local Japanese market and not a preview of things to come for the global GPU market. But in case you’re on the market for a new graphics card and have been following our GPU price tracker to get the best possible deal, you shouldn’t delay your purchase any longer, as there’s a chance that these price increases will eventually land on American shores.

85,2026-10-14,"Netflix is laying off about 1,000 employees, roughly 6% of its staffers, in its biggest round of cuts since 2022, a person familiar with the plans confirmed.
Co-CEOs Ted Sarandos and Greg Peters notified employees about the downsizing in a memo on Wednesday. Netflix is contending with slowing engagement growth and a lagging stock price that's down more than 30% over the last six months and over 40% over the past year.
The streaming giant is letting go of staffers primarily across the product, tech, HR, and legal teams, Sarandos and Peters said in their memo. The Wall Street Journal first reported the news.
Netflix's mission to entertain the world ""requires us to be disciplined about where we prioritize our people and resources,"" Sarandos and Peters said. They said the streaming giant's business is ""strong and growing"" and added that the company is on pace to hit this year's financial goals.
Other media giants have made cuts in recent weeks. NBCUniversal said in early October that it's parting with a few hundred global streaming tech employees, while Disney did its third round of layoffs in six months in late September.
Netflix's cuts come a week before it reports third-quarter earnings, which will be after the closing bell on October 20. Its last major round of layoffs was in 2022, after the company lost subscribers for the first time in more than a decade.
Sarandos said at a Bloomberg event in late September that ""we're not growing as fast as I want us to, and we're working on making that move faster.""
Paid streamers have faced pressure overall as free services like YouTube and Tubi have gained popularity. Netflix and its rivals are turning to podcasts and creator content to win over viewers. Disney+ and Paramount+ are planning to add free tiers, and Netflix's Peters said this summer that ""a free offering could make sense in some markets.""
Netflix met Wall Street's earnings expectations last quarter but said it would start posting its detailed engagement report once a year, instead of twice a year. The company told shareholders that ""engagement is not just the quantity of view hours, but also refers to the quality and variety of our offering.""
Take a smarter break in your day - and see how far you get.

                                    Add BI in Google so our reporting is easier to find when you’re searching for what matters.
                                  
The reporting change troubled investors, as engagement is generally correlated with subscribers' willingness to stick around and pay higher prices. (Netflix hiked prices in March to $19.99 a month from $17.99 for its standard ad-free plan and to $8.99 for its ad-supported plan, up from $7.99.)
Netflix's viewership grew 2% in the first half of the year. Its 97 billion viewing hours are likely far higher than any of its paid streaming peers, which don't disclose engagement in the same way.
Netflix generated 7.8% of viewership on US TVs in July, according to the latest data from Nielsen, below YouTube's 14.2% share but well above third-place Disney's streamers at 4.7%.
However, Netflix's streaming viewership share is down from 9% in December, which was its highest mark in more than five years, thanks to hit shows like ""Stranger Things.""

86,2026-10-14,"Join this webinar for a keep-or-cut list of off-page tactics and a step-by-step plan for running the ones that still work on your brand.
Invalid traffic on AI Max search doubled to 5.28%. Get the channel-by-channel retail benchmarks before Black Friday.

Calls from ChatGPT, Claude, and Perplexity land in 'direct/other.' This checklist shows six steps to track them as their own source.
Ask the SEJ experts you already read what AI, agents, and algorithm changes mean for your traffic. Private, human to human. $97/mo.
Webinar: The Link Building Graveyard: What to Bury & What Still Wins
From testing top Shopify stores, Bill Hunt found agents can build a checkout, but can’t answer basic delivery or cancellation questions.
In September 2026, Slobodan Manic argued that the text-only versions of websites now being served to AI “strip out the wrong layer.” A markdown mirror describes the page but drops everything a visitor could press, so it becomes “a brochure … served to the user most eager to act.”
He ends with a brilliant three-part list that every ecommerce team should adopt: expose what can be done, make it callable, and report what happened.
I had been running an audit of the top 122 Shopify storefronts with a related question: not whether AI can find or cite these stores, but whether an agent can actually resolve a purchase decision on them. That means having enough information, context, rules, and the capability to execute to answer the buyer’s questions, commit to the purchase, and deal with what comes after.
His article prompted me to go further. Instead of counting which tools the stores declared, I called them. This article reports what we found, confirms his argument, shows where our results differ, and outlines what both merchants and Shopify can do about it.
The platform installed the actions, not the merchants. Manic reports that Shopify switched on WebMCP tools for every Liquid storefront on August 5, and that “the merchants did not build it.” Our data shows the same thing at scale:
If you count adoption of agents.md as a sign of merchant readiness, you are really measuring Shopify’s rollout schedule.
GEO does not address the action. Manic writes that GEO “has absolutely nothing to do with the action.” Our data backs this up. Most stores publish the facts an agent needs, and Shopify’s checkout works. What breaks is the step in between: turning a published policy into an answer the agent can use when making its decision. The return window appears as prose on a policy page, but the agent can’t retrieve it when asked. Being cited or ranked doesn’t fix that.
The semantic floor is broken, including on product pages. He cites WebAIM’s 2026 finding that unlabelled form inputs appear on 51% of home pages, empty links on 46.3%, and empty buttons on 30.6%. We ran the same checks on the product page, where the purchase action actually lives, for the 68 stores we could retrieve:
The buy button itself is usually fine. When it is present, it is a native, named <button> 92% of the time. The problem is around it. If two-thirds of size selectors have no label, an agent cannot reliably tell which dropdown sets the size and which sets the color.
This is where UX, accessibility, and agent readiness must work together because they depend on the same markup. A labeled size selector, a named buy button, and an announced “added to cart” message serve a screen-reader user, a keyboard user, and a browser agent at once. The conflicts stem from a visual-only design: swatches with no names and confirmations that only appear on screen. Fix the markup once and all three benefit.
Huge props to Shopify as nobody else has deployed an agent-callable commerce surface at this scale, and that deserves recognition before any criticism.
Across our sample, 89 of 122 stores answered a standard MCP tool listing. Shopify says its catalog now spans “billions of products across millions of merchants,” and that orders from AI-powered searches grew nearly 13 times year over year in Q1 2026. Merchants on the platform received catalog search, cart, checkout, and policy lookup tools without writing a line of code.
The quality is also better than I expected. We ran a live, non-completing checkout on every store that exposed the Universal Commerce Protocol (UCP) endpoint: create a checkout for one in-stock item going to a Boston address, read it back, cancel it, then cancel it again. We never completed a purchase or sent payment details.
 This bears directly on the third item on Manic’s list. He describes an agent that re-submitted a form because the only confirmation was “rendered for eyes.” On Shopify’s tool layer, that failure is handled: the second cancel is refused with a machine-readable error instead of being silently repeated. On that test, Shopify’s tools already report back to the agent properly.
These are differences of timing and scope, not disagreements.
1. We Tested A Different Door, And Shopify Has Since Finished His
Shopify offers agents two routes into checkout. The first is a server endpoint that an agent calls directly, with no browser (UCP over MCP). The second is WebMCP: tools that register inside the shopper’s own browser tab. Manic tested the browser route on August 6 and found that “the buying path died on an internal error.” At that point, the browser tools covered the storefront and cart, and the checkout step mostly moved the tab to the checkout page.
Our updated September test used the server route. Checkout creation succeeded on every store we gave a real, in-stock item, with a median response time of about 1.1 seconds. The only failures came from our own stale data. We had collected item IDs a few weeks earlier. By test day, five of those items had sold out, and two IDs no longer existed. The stores rejected them with clean out_of_stock or invalid codes (“The merchandise with id … does not exist”). So our result does not show that his defect was fixed. We were testing a different route.
That small failure is still a lesson for merchants, because agents work the same way we did. An assistant may hold an item ID from a product feed, an earlier conversation, or a shared cart, and try to buy it days later. A sold-out item is normal commerce, as long as every surface an agent reads reports availability promptly. A vanished ID is avoidable. It usually happens when a product is deleted and re-created, or re-imported, instead of edited, and the agent then has to start its search over. Keep variant IDs stable, and keep inventory current across your feeds and catalog.
Then, on September 28, Shopify announced support for WebMCP in checkout. Browser agents can now “read and update Shopify checkouts,” covering “the full shopping journey… through checkout and order confirmation,” with no merchant configuration. On September 30, we ran a new test and opened live checkouts at a sample of sites in a browser, with WebMCP exposed to the page. Both registered the four announced tools: get_checkout, update_checkout, complete_checkout, and navigate_to_storefront. A read-only get_checkout call returned the full checkout: line items, totals with tax, fulfillment method, and coded messages showing which fields were still missing. We did not submit an order, but the browser route that failed for Manic now reaches and reads the checkout properly.
The tool descriptions also answer his point about agents repeating actions. They tell the agent to “use get_checkout to verify the outcome” and “do not resubmit while status is complete_in_progress or completed.” That’s the “report what happened” requirement, written directly into the tools.
Every one of the 74 checkouts came back with the status requires_escalation and a message flagged requires_buyer_input. This matches the rule in Shopify’s default agents.md that checkout requires human approval. That is a reasonable safety choice, and it changes what “agent-ready” means. The new browser tools keep the same rule: complete_checkout is to be used “only after the buyer authorizes the purchase,” and control goes back to the buyer for steps such as 3D Secure. On Shopify today, the agent builds a priced cart, and a person commits the money.
An agent can act on a decision only after making one. Manic’s framework covers the doing. Our audit asked whether the agent has what it needs to make a decision and whether it can undo the result. On both, the answer was mostly no, and the September 28 release doesn’t change that. In the browser checkout, the refund terms still reach the agent only as a link to the refund policy page, not as values. get_checkout reads “post-completion order details,” but no tool cancels or changes the order. At one site, the checkout also contained a line item the shopper hadn’t chosen, “Free Returns Coverage” for $2.98, added by the store. The agent can see it, but nothing in the checkout data marks it as optional or explains how to remove it. That’s a rule the buyer needs before committing, and it’s exactly the kind of thing the tools don’t explain.
This is the part I most want ecommerce leaders to take away. It is easy to look at a Shopify store today and conclude it is agent-ready: the agents.md file exists, the UCP profile exists, the MCP tools answer, and many readiness checkers will score the store well.
What the defaults don’t give you: declared is not the same as done.
The agent can’t find the answers to buyers’ questions. Every store with Shopify’s policy tool was asked six questions that a real buyer would ask. We then ran a control test with broad, policy-style questions.
Return windows are the one clear success. For everything else, the content is there, and the tool can reach it when the question sounds like a policy page. It cannot reach it when the question sounds like a buyer. One store’s FAQ says “free standard ground shipping on U.S. orders over $75,” and that answer never comes back for a question about what shipping costs.
Worse, when asked about cancellation, two stores instead returned their shipping FAQ. A wrong answer that looks confident is more dangerous than no answer, because an agent has no reliable way to tell it doesn’t address the question.
Delivery date is missing everywhere. A live checkout priced shipping to Boston for 36 of 74 stores, so for half the sample you can get the cost, but only by starting a purchase. None of the 74 returned an arrival estimate or a carrier, even though the protocol has fields for both. “When will it arrive?” could not be answered through any agent path on any store we tested.
Pickup is invisible. All 93 stores with a UCP fulfillment declaration list shipping as the only method; none declares pickup. That includes retailers that clearly offer it. A store that documents Pickup in Store, yet its agent endpoint answers “No pickup locations available.” Another offers Click & Collect and declares shipping only. Here, the agent gets a clear, machine-readable answer that is wrong, and it will send the customer to delivery when same-day collection was available.
Most policy information isn’t machine-readable. 70% of the stores whose policy text we could read state a return window in prose. Only 7% of product pages expose it as structured merchantReturnDays.
Nothing can be undone. Not one of the 122 stores exposes a tool to cancel, change, or return an order after it is placed. This is not the merchants’ fault. The UCP order specification defines two operations, reading an order and an order-event webhook, and no operation to cancel or amend one (UCP). It even defines a permission scope for “cancellation, returns, and other modifications” without defining how to perform any of them.
Across all nine checks we used to decide whether an agent could complete a purchase decision, zero of 122 stores passed. The best were three stores that reached seven of nine. All three failed the same two: no stated cancellation window, and no way to cancel.
When we sorted the failures by the team that owns the missing information, a pattern appeared:
None of these teams owns the website. The checks nearly every store passes are the ones the platform generates automatically: catalog, variants, price, checkout. The ones nearly every store fails to meet need someone outside the web team to write a commercial rule and a system to enforce it. That is an organizational problem, and the search team cannot fix it alone.
Because Shopify is deployed at this scale, it can also fix much of this at scale. Several of the gaps involve data Shopify already holds in structured form.
You do not need a full audit to find out where your store stands. Most of this takes an afternoon.
Ask your own policy tool the questions buyers ask. This one call shows you what an agent gets back:
Repeat it for the return window, shipping cost, arrival time at a real address, pickup today, and used or open-box returns. If you get an empty list, a link, or the wrong topic, that’s what the agent gets too.
Then work through this list:
Manic’s closing line is that a text-only version of a site is a brochure. Our data adds a second half. Shopify has given its merchants a working checkout that an agent can operate. What it hasn’t yet given them, and what most haven’t provided themselves, is the rulebook an agent needs to use it responsibly: how much, how soon, can I collect it, and can I change my mind?
Having the information isn’t enough. An agent needs information, context, rules, and operational capabilities to resolve a customer’s decision. For now, most stores that look ready by default are only ready until the buyer asks a real question.
One thing our test did not measure, and that surprised me most in hindsight: we told each store exactly which item to buy. A real buyer rarely arrives with a variant ID. They need help choosing between sizes, models, and related products, and that decision logic is merchant knowledge no platform can install by default. Shopify can carry it; only the merchant can write it. That’s the next test, and the subject of a future column.
Methodology: 122 leading Shopify storefronts, selected from published rankings of top Shopify merchants, probed between August and September 2026. The layers tested were: agents.md, llms.txt, UCP profiles and MCP tool listings; product-page structured data; the visible text of policy pages; the buyer questions and control questions put to each store’s own policy tool; a non-completing checkout lifecycle test (never calling complete_checkout or sending payment); a static-HTML accessibility check of each product page; and, on September 30, a browser check of the new WebMCP checkout tools at two stores, reading the checkout without submitting it. Requests were rate-limited, and responses that were throttled or blocked were recorded as unknown rather than absent. Coverage varies by test: policy text for 56 stores, policy tool for 88, live checkout for 74, product-page accessibility for 68. Shipping was tested to one Boston address. The accessibility check reads server HTML only, so it understates what JavaScript may add.

87,2026-10-14,"New Balance’s 983 looks like it took a wrong turn somewhere on the way to Earth. With its bulbous overlays and oddly sculptural shape, it’s giving serious space-age energy, and not in the usual “slap some silver on it and call it futuristic” kind of way.
There’s a certain weirdness to this sneaker that makes it hard to ignore. The upper is busy without being chaotic, the oversized N logo looks almost inflated, and the sole has enough curves to give an architect something to think about. It’s a lot of shoe, but that’s precisely the point.
New Balance has never been short on chunky dad shoes, yet the 983 feels like it came from a slightly different corner of the brand’s universe. It’s less concerned with looking like a vintage running shoe and more interested in seeing how far that familiar formula can stretch.
The proportions are what really sell it. Between the layered upper and the exaggerated sole, the 983 has a strange, almost cartoonish quality that makes it stand out from the sea of mesh runners currently doing the rounds. It’s not exactly subtle, but then subtlety was never going to be the assignment here.
The result is a sneaker that feels a little alien, a little futuristic, and refreshingly difficult to compare with everything else on the market. Earthbound sneaker rotation? This one might be ready for a change of scenery.

88,2026-10-14,"in Film |   	October 14th, 2026  1 Comment


 


Cleopa­tra cost Twen­ti­eth Cen­tu­ry Fox $44 mil­lion, by the stu­dio’s own esti­mate. That may sound like the bud­get of one of those genre pic­tures that winds up going straight to stream­ing, but if we con­vert those 1963 dol­lars into cur­rent ones, it comes out to $480 mil­lion. The most expen­sive film ever made up to that point, it remained an indus­try sym­bol of near-par­o­d­i­cal­ly trou­bled pro­duc­tion and grotesque cost infla­tion for decades. Like The Wiz­ard of Oz, it failed to recoup its invest­ment dur­ing its ini­tial the­atri­cal run, even­tu­al­ly turn­ing a prof­it thanks only to re-releas­es and tele­vi­sion rights sales. And yet, these six decades lat­er, the invest­ment seems to have proven sound, at least accord­ing to the video above from YouTube chan­nel Wolfcrow, “Why Cleopa­tra Still Looks Like a Tril­lion Bucks.”
Unlike some infa­mous­ly expen­sive movies, Cleopa­tra does­n’t leave you won­der­ing where all the mon­ey went. Wolfcrow cre­ator Sareesh Sud­hakaran points out some of the myr­i­ad ways it man­i­fests onscreen, from the 70-foot-long giant sphinx to the full-scale Alexan­dria har­bor built on the coast to the Roman Forum set designed to be three times larg­er than the actu­al Roman Forum to the 65 or so out­fits Eliz­a­beth Tay­lor wears in the title role (to say noth­ing of the 26,000 oth­er cos­tumes made for all the oth­er char­ac­ters). Even the mat­te paint­ings and opti­cal cam­era tricks still look con­vinc­ing. The film’s pro­duc­tion design­ers knew that noth­ing could be done by half mea­sures, giv­en that the 65mm Todd-AO stock used for film­ing would expose any flim­si­ness on the big screen.
 


Just as unfor­giv­ing was the ultra-wide aspect ratio used for the 1959 ver­sion of Ben-Hur, which neces­si­tat­ed painstak­ing labor on every­thing that went before the cam­era, but also a new approach to the com­po­si­tion of the shots them­selves. Even those of us who’ve nev­er seen the movie can call to mind images of its cli­mac­tic char­i­ot race, a sequence that earned its cul­tur­al impact the hard way.
It took five months to film on the largest set ever built at that time, with 1,500 extras in its stands. A few years lat­er, Lawrence of Ara­bia took Hol­ly­wood grandeur up to the lev­el of sub­lim­i­ty with its real desert land­scapes. David Lean “was obsessed with scale and detail,” Sud­hakaran explains. “He want­ed the desert to be more than just a back­drop. He want­ed it to be a char­ac­ter in the sto­ry,” which required metic­u­lous plan­ning of all the ways its nat­ur­al light could pos­si­bly be used to dra­mat­ic effect.
 


Though Sud­hakaran clear­ly has an inter­est in spec­ta­cles from the gold­en age of the Hol­ly­wood his­tor­i­cal epics, he’s also made videos on last­ing­ly impres­sive movies from oth­er times and gen­res. When was the last time you sat down to enjoy the satir­i­cal urban tech­no-dystopia Robo­Cop, the com­ic-strip noir fan­ta­sia of Dick Tra­cy, or the flam­boy­ant de-famil­iar­ized twen­ty-third cen­tu­ry of The Fifth Ele­ment — or bet­ter yet, saw them prop­er­ly screened at a revival house? When they came out, those films met with vary­ing degrees of box-office suc­cess and crit­i­cal praise, if not regard­ed as the absolute peak of cin­e­mat­ic sophis­ti­ca­tion. In our time, their ambi­tion and craft look noth­ing short of aston­ish­ing, espe­cial­ly when we remem­ber that they built their worlds in phys­i­cal real­i­ty with real human beings, most­ly ana­log tech­niques, and a whole lot of labor and imag­i­na­tion. Could even a tril­lion dol­lars buy the same results today?

IMF warns of widespread disruption

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

The International Monetary Fund has warned global financial markets are becoming increasingly vulnerable to the volatility of AI investments.
While AI-related investments have becoming an increasingly important driver of economy activity, even helping to offset some of the economic damage associated with ongoing geopolitical upheaval, AI holds the potential of causing major market disruption.
Specifically, the IMF warned of three potential developments – a correction in AI-related investments and company valuations, an increase in government bond yields, or a major cyberattack targeting critical national infrastructure, including financial institutions.
While the IMF acknowledged the AI market has helped sustain large parts of the world's economy, but pointed toward high company valuations and exceptionally strong revenues. The report also warns of two-way investments with tech companies both giving and taking from each other and becoming increasingly reliant on each other.
To quantify this, the IMF wrote: ""Our review of 14 such transactions since 2023 found about $300 billion in equity investments and $650 billion in debt, in exchange for $1.4 trillion in future revenue commitments.""
Also involving the tech sector, the IMF worries that a potentially catastrophic cybersecurity incident could spell disaster for the world's economy. With financial systems more digital, automated and interconnected than ever before, cybercriminals now have the opportunity to attack multiple institutions and organizations simultaneously.
Ultimately, the IMF's conclusion is that isolated incidents are no longer so isolated – complex dependencies mean that one single incident could have much broader impacts.

Waiting for lower prices and worthwhile upgrades? We don't blame you

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

This year seemingly hasn’t been a vintage one for phone companies. According to Counterpoint Research, global shipments are 20% lower this quarter than in the third quarter (Q3) of 2025, making this the lowest Q3 in 14 years.
You can probably guess the main reason for that, as the ongoing RAM crisis is both pushing up prices and leading to component shortages. That means disrupted shipments for phone manufacturers — causing some models to be in shorter supply — and higher prices for consumers, which can make the prospect of an upgrade less appealing or affordable.
Still, this 14-year low might not be purely down to the RAM crisis, as Counterpoint additionally claims that consumers are “holding on to their devices longer than expected.”
Now obviously, high prices could cause people to hold off on upgrading, but presumably the RAM crisis was factored into consumer behavior, so if people are holding onto their devices for longer than expected even with that factored in, then there might be something else at play too.
What could that be? The report doesn’t take a guess, but it might be that phones are simply less interesting or flawed than they once were. These days, even quite low-end phones are often highly capable, and run well for years — which wasn’t always the case.
At the high end, it’s increasingly hard for manufacturers to offer meaningful upgrades and differentiation when the best phones already have more power than most people need, along with superb screens and cameras.
 



In any case, according to this report, Apple and Samsung seem the most resilient to these challenging times, with Samsung actually increasing its market share to 23%, despite a 3% decline in shipments compared to this period last year. That’s in large part apparently due to the success of the Samsung Galaxy Z Fold 8.
Sign up for breaking news, reviews, opinion, top tech deals, and more.
Apple also grew its market share, rising to 22% despite also selling fewer phones than this time last year. It’s likely that the brand would have done even better had it shipped a base iPhone 18, rather than waiting until next year for that.
Other brands like Xiaomi, Oppo, and Vivo meanwhile saw sharp declines in shipments and a drop in market share alongside that, allowing Apple and Samsung to increase their own market shares in turn.
But across the whole industry it seems fewer phones are being sold — and while things might improve once the RAM crisis is over, they might not completely return to ‘normal’ if there are other reasons for this change in customer behavior, too.

91,2026-10-14,"(Oct 14): Paper losses on Bank of America Corp’s bond portfolio rose past US$100 billion (RM408.2 billion), bringing the spotlight back on a pain point that was at the heart of the 2023 regional-banking crisis.
Yields on seven-year and 10-year Treasuries are now above 5%, pushing down the market value of those bonds. That drove its unrealised losses on its total debt securities to more than US$109 billion in the third quarter, up 28% from the end of June, according to filings.
JPMorgan Chase & Co, Citigroup Inc and Goldman Sachs Group Inc “are sitting pretty while we all kind of forget that Bank of America is a mess”, Bill Moreland, founder of BankRegData, wrote in a newsletter this month.
Still, the US banks that have reported so far haven’t been immune. The impact of market swings on these securities’ portfolios filters through earnings via the firm’s so-called accumulated other comprehensive income. The AOCI losses more than doubled in the third quarter at JPMorgan and Wells Fargo & Co.
“We all knew rate marks were coming, still there is some sticker shock seeing AOCI nearly double in the quarter,” Truist Securities analysts including Brian Foran wrote in a note to clients Tuesday. As AOCI dragged down banks’ capital levels, it made investors wonder if banks will slow down share buybacks, according to Foran.
As of August, analysts predicted that large banks would repurchase US$207 billion of shares by the end of 2027, Foran noted.
On Tuesday, JPMorgan said it cut repurchases by almost 90% in the third quarter from a year earlier. JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon downplayed the impact of seasonal market swings on its decision to reduce share buybacks.
“So the positive news is the main reason the buyback is down — and it’s got nothing to do with AOCI or guessing about the environment or even Basel III — we believe now, which is different than we told you in the past, that we can redeploy that capital at very good returns over time,” Dimon said on a conference call Tuesday.
Those unrealised losses eased earlier this year as rates fell. But the recent reversal in rates has brought the issue back to the fore. While the portfolio isn’t an existential threat for Bank of America, which has average deposit balances of about US$2 trillion, the swings in the portfolio have been a headache for the bank.
“It’s hard to avoid it, but if you are really underwater, then I think you might get more negative attention,” said Nathan Stovall, a managing director at Performance Trust Capital Partners, speaking broadly about investors’ expectations on bank stocks. Investors will want to know, ""Did you learn from that experience?”
The issue has lingered over Bank of America since the Federal Reserve began its latest rate-hiking cycle in early 2022. The company has been the worst performer among the half-dozen biggest US banks in that period, with shares returning about 21% while the stocks of Goldman, Citigroup and JPMorgan more than doubled.
Bank of America’s current securities portfolio is still the biggest among peers and yields the least. The firm’s executives have said that as the low-yielding bonds approach maturity and roll off, the bank can reinvest those into securities with higher yields.
Those portfolios were at the centre of the turmoil that brought down the likes of Silicon Valley Bank and First Republic Bank in 2023. What’s different this time is that banks aren’t facing the same liquidity crunch, making industry experts believe it will be manageable.
The bank failures have also prompted banks across the industry to shore up their defences against swings in interest rates. Many have sold underwater bonds at a loss and reinvested the proceeds in higher-yielding securities to boost what they can earn on those portfolios. More banks are now exploring similar moves.
JPMorgan booked US$658 million of investment securities losses in the third quarter to replace lower-yielding securities. Also in July, Banc of California said it sold off US$2.3 billion of low-yielding securities and redeployed some of those proceeds into higher-yielding assets.
“I would say it is absolutely a concern, again,” said Scott Hildenbrand, chief balance-sheet strategist at Piper Sandler Cos. “I’ve been exploring in a lot more conversations with banks around restructurings.”

92,2026-10-14,"Trade body claims Anbernic ""normalises hardware-based piracy by delivering illicit catalogues through a polished consumer product""
The Entertainment Software Association (ESA) has recommended that retro console maker Anbernic be added to the US government's Notorious Market List.
The Notorious Market List is an  annual report published by the Office of the United States Trade Representative (USTR), which identifies online and physical markets ""that reportedly engage in or facilitate substantial trademark counterfeiting or copyright piracy.""
Anbernic, based in China, manufactures handheld devices that emulate retro gaming systems from platform holders including Nintendo, PlayStation, and Sega. Last year, Anbernic suspended shipments to the US  following the implementation of tariffs.
According to  the filing (via  VGC), the ESA claims that Anbernic ""normalises hardware-based piracy by delivering illicit catalogues through a polished consumer product [...] By marketing the devices through ordinary retail channels, the operation also shifts monitoring, investigation, notice, and remediation costs to publishers and marketplaces.""
The trade body stated that Anbernic devices are ""routinely offered or shipped with removable memory cards preloaded with hundreds or thousands of unauthorised copies of copyright-protected video games published by ESA members. Those copies enable immediate play without a lawful purchase or license and reflect the unlawful circumvention of technological protection measures used to control access to or copying of member games.""
In general, the ESA highlighted ""illicit plug-and-play consoles and handheld devices"" as being ""the functional equivalent of superfakes.""
""A single device can substitute for lawful purchases across multiple video game publishers and console generations, divert sales from authorised games, compilations, subscriptions, remasters, and licensed retro hardware""
""Sellers commonly use the illicit library as a key selling point, and the commercial price often reflects this. The same catalogue can be duplicated at negligible marginal cost across large production runs and then distributed through branded websites, legitimate online marketplaces, social media, and physical electronics markets. This combination of professional presentation, enormous embedded catalogues, and mainstream distribution permits piracy to scale as an ordinary consumer purchase.""
The ESA also highlighted that these devices can ""create security, privacy, and financial risks""; ""bypass important player and child-safety protections""; and ""distort competition and burden governments as well as rights holders.""
Alongside ""piracy devices,"" the ESA said it should add ""cracked game sources, repackers, and linking sites""; ""torrent and piracy-coordination markets""; ""unauthorised digital goods marketplaces""; and ""commercial cheat markets"" to the Notorious Markets List.
Commercial marketplace G2A is mentioned under the ""unauthorised digital goods marketplaces"" subheading, with the ESA noting that ""G2A previously represented that it did not permit sales of in-game accounts or items, but the marketplace facilitates account and digital item sales at scale.""
To address these issues, the ESA has asked the USTR to set out seven steps for governments and market operators to take, which include:

Topic:Personal Investing
The ASX estimates 950,000 non-investors plan to start in the next year. (Reuters: Hollie Adams)
The stock exchange operator says market participation is at a record high among Australians.
An ASX survey found 54 per cent of Australian adults hold investments outside their super and primary home.
The ASX estimates 950,000 Australians who do not currently invest plan to start within the next 12 months.
A record 11.5 million Australian adults now hold investments outside their superannuation and their main home, a new survey by the stock exchange operator finds.
The ASX Australian Investor Study 2026 has revealed an estimated 2.5 million Australians have joined the investor population since 2020.
Investors, the survey showed, now represent 54 per cent of the adult population.
""Australia is a nation of investors,"" ASX chief executive Anthony Attia said.
Market participation in Australia is at a record high, the ASX says. (ABC News: Darryl Torpy)
Most investors were middle-income earners and middle-aged, with a median gross income of $110,000 and an average age of 46.
Younger people, aged 18–29, made up 21 per cent of investors.
For the first time, the ASX survey looked at how investors were using AI tools for investment information and advice.
The bourse said investors continued to use professional advisers, brokers, online investment platforms, family and friends, social media, podcasts and other digital sources.
It found AI uptake was strongest among younger investors, used by 14 per cent of investors aged 18 to 29.
Mr Attia said Australia's public participation in share trading was among the highest in developed markets.
Anthony Attia took over the top job at the ASX last month. (Supplied)
The survey, which polled roughly 4,500 people in January and February this year, found shares were the most widely held assets, followed by residential property, exchange-traded funds (ETFs), term deposits and cryptocurrency.
""Almost 1 million more Australians are looking to start investing.
""The rapid growth of ETFs, with ownership more than doubling since 2020, shows how innovation is opening new opportunities for investors.""
Australian ETF monthly inflows hit $7 billion for the first time in August, says Betashares. (Reuters: Dado Ruvic/Illustration)
Since the ASX survey, money has continued to increasingly flow into ETFs.
Data from investment firm Betashares showed the Australian ETF industry experienced $7 billion in cash flowing in during September after monthly inflows surpassed $7 billion for the first time in August.
The industry hit a record $388 billion in assets under management.
If you've avoided investing because you've found the terminology intimidating, you're not alone.
Recent tax changes appear to be behind the enthusiasm for stocks in general, but ETFs in particular.
The government announced in the May budget that it planned to replace the current 50 per cent capital gains tax (CGT) discount with a discount based on the actual rate of inflation.
It also said it would introduce a minimum 30 per cent tax on gains from July 1, 2027.
Forager Funds chief investment officer Steve Johnson said ETFs were popular under the new settings as net gains and losses were contained inside the investment, so a surge in an individual company that might attract capital gains tax could be offset by another loss-making stock.
Betashares agreed investors had paid more attention to the relative tax effectiveness of index-based investment strategies and ETFs following the CGT changes.
Some young Australians locked out of the property market due to unaffordable housing have pursued ETFs and ""rentvesting"". Here's what they make of the budget.
The firm also said the continued fall in Australian house prices was giving investors more reason to look at other asset classes like shares, bonds and commodities.
Global X ETFs senior strategist Marc Jocum said the scale of investing over the past quarter had been ""significant"".
""Australian ETFs have just delivered a record $21 billion quarter, demonstrating that they are becoming a core building block in Australian portfolios,"" Mr Jocum said.
""Investors are no longer using ETFs for one purpose.
As more Australians turn to investing, more are exposed to digital assets including cryptocurrencies.
Corporate watchdog ASIC is looking to firm up the regulation of digital assets, today beginning consultation on guidance and standards to accompany new laws which commence on April 8, 2027.
ASIC is preparing guidance to accompany upcoming laws governing digital assets. (Reuters: Dado Ruvic)
ASIC wants digital asset platforms (DAPs) and tokenised custody platforms (TCPs) to hold an Australian financial services licence, provide customer disclosure documents and platform rules, and comply with asset holding standards, transactional and settlement standards, and net tangible asset requirements where relevant.
ASIC commissioner Alan Kirkland said that ASIC's proposal recognised the rapid evolution of global markets and ""the increasing role of digital assets and tokenisation, including in traditional parts of the financial system"".
""Our approach is based on the principle of 'same activity, same risk, same regulatory outcome' to align Australia with the approach of comparable overseas jurisdictions.""
ASIC's job is only likely to get bigger with the pool of investors projected to keep expanding.
A review of nine online trading companies finds several are encouraging Australians to jump on ""complex"" deals, putting them at risk of major losses.
The ASX survey found an estimated 950,000 Australians who do not currently invest plan to start within the next 12 months.
Women, the survey showed, represented 61 per cent of this group.
Australians aged 18 to 29 accounted for almost four in 10 ""investors in waiting"".
The ASX said demand for financial advice was also increasing, with knowledge listed as the number one barrier to entering the market.

94,2026-10-14,"
Some phones look like slabs, some look like jewelry, and the Acer Sospiro A70 looks dressed for a wedding. A black glass camera module runs across the top of the back and ends in a pointed V, exactly like the flap on a blazer pocket. I love it. It gives the phone an instantly recognizable silhouette in a market where most rivals share one camera island and one personality. The V points down the back like an arrow, as if the phone is gesturing at its own camera and saying, look at this. Anyone who keeps a phone in a blazer pocket will feel right at home.
The A70 comes from Senwa Global, the Mexican company that licenses the Acer name for phones sold across Latin America. Tucked into that module is a 1.88-inch second screen that works as a viewfinder, so you can shoot selfies with the 64MP main camera instead of the 16MP front one. Under the hood sit a Unisoc T8200 chip (two fast cores, six frugal ones), 8GB of RAM, 256GB of storage, 5G, and a headphone jack. Pricing and a launch date are still unannounced. Sadly, the A70 isn’t sold in the US, so this one is admiration from a distance.
Designer: Acer






Front cameras are the budget cut nobody advertises. The A70’s 16MP selfie shooter is fine, but the 64MP rear sensor is the one Acer actually bragged about. The 1.88-inch rear screen (240 x 284 pixels) lets you flip the phone around and frame yourself with that better camera. No more guessing where your forehead ended up. The same panel shows the time, notifications, and incoming calls, which suits a face-down phone on a cafe table. At roughly 198 pixels per inch it’s softer than the main display, but for checking your hair that is plenty.
Xiaomi started this whole idea in 2021 with the Mi 11 Ultra, which carried a 1.1-inch AMOLED beside its camera and sold in Europe for €1,199. The Xiaomi 17 Pro and Pro Max grew the screen to 2.7 and 2.9 inches in 2025, starting at CNY 5,000 and CNY 6,000 (roughly $700 and $850). Those are flagship prices, and the phones launched in China first. The A70’s 1.88-inch panel lands between those two generations in size and keeps the same logic, minus the flagship bill. Nobody had a licensed Acer down as the one to carry the trickle-down torch, myself included.

On the red and graphite versions, the module is a black glass band spanning the full width of the back, with a chevron lower edge pointing down the body. The lens rings are tinted to match each body, so the red gets red rings, the white gets silver, and the graphite stays dark. A vertical flash strip sits beside the lenses, and the rear screen fills the right half of the module in Acer’s renders, with a gradient clock and a flower wallpaper overlapping the digits. A small 5G badge sits right at the V point. It is a tidy composition for a phone in this class.
The graphite finish is my favorite, a speckled texture with a faint sparkle that Acer photographed against a slab of rock for maximum drama. The red is glossy and loud, and the white pairs a silver frame with a white module for a cleaner look. Green accents on the side keys add a pop of color in the renders. Back to the pocket flap, though. I would argue a distinctive camera shape does more branding work than the Acer logo, which sits modestly near the bottom. Even from across a table, that V says Acer Sospiro louder than any badge.






The rest of the spec sheet reads like a practical friend. A 5,000mAh battery is the usual all-day size, and the box includes a charger, cable, case, and screen protector, which saves you a trip to the accessory aisle. The 6.67-inch screen is only 720 x 1600 pixels, but it runs at 120Hz (120 refreshes per second), so scrolling stays smooth even if small text is not razor sharp. Storage is 256GB with a microSD slot for cards up to 1TB, so your vacation clips have somewhere to go. The 3.5mm jack and FM radio are on board, and IP64 handles rain but not a pool.
Pricing and a launch date are unannounced, and the A70 isn’t sold in the US, so American readers will need a Latin American friend or a lot of patience. The nearest thing on US shelves is a midrange Samsung Galaxy A phone or a Google Pixel A model, and neither has a second screen on the back. Senwa’s lineup of Acer-branded phones has mostly been budget fare, which makes this one a clear step up in ambition. Xiaomi proved the rear screen could be a flagship feature, and Acer’s licensee is betting it can be a mid-range one. If it works, the cheap seats get the coolest design detail first.

Jio Platforms IPO: Issue opens on October 21, anchor bidding on October 19

India-Mauritius pact brings in Principal Purpose Test 
Finally, the Finance Ministry has amended the India-Mauritius tax treaty to curb treaty abuse by introducing a Principal Purpose Test. The amendment allows tax authorities to deny treaty benefits if obtaining them was one of the principal purposes of a transaction. Investments made before April 1, 2017, remain outside the scope of the test. The move puts greater focus on the commercial purpose and substance of investment structures routed through Mauritius.
